**What was measured and NOT built.** The obvious reading — reward matched hands — was measured first and abandoned: both hands already carry the same tag on **61% of turns**, almost entirely `crack`, because the deck the game builds is mono-tag (`crack:9 rest:3 mental:3`) against a starter deck spread across six tags. Any matching bonus would be a near-constant buff that rewards exactly the homogeneity the builder already produces. `CARD-20` carries what a real new combination would need.

**A joint balance pass came with it, and the change that tripped the guard was not the cause.** `CARD-9`'s ceiling says an extra burn is a leg-up and not a skip, asserted as a **lift with a bar of 4 points at n=900**. At v10.53 that lift measured **3.9** — one tenth of a point of headroom. CARD-19 moved it to **4.1**, well inside a single standard error for a difference of proportions at that sample size, and the guard failed.

The measurements are deterministic at a fixed seed set (two identical invocations returned identical numbers), so this is **drift, not flakiness**: the item crept from the ~2.4 the bar was calibrated against up to 3.9 across v10.49–v10.53, and `GUARD-1`'s standing note is right that this margin is *structurally coupled to the base band* — five tickets moved things around it. **The bar was not touched.** `WIND_PUMP` 0.22 → **0.45**, which is the dial this contract has always used (`BAL-15` set it; `ENG-21`, `ENG-32` and `SIM-6` each moved it: 0.4, 0.55, 0.45, 0.22). The lift now measures **2.4 at n=900** — exactly the reference figure in the guard's own comment — and the default arm is unmoved at 41.9%, because the drafter never buys kit.

**Band: 44.1% (n=3000) against the 44.3% pin, `winds 0` — the pinned arm never touches kit, so `WIND_PUMP` cannot reach it.**

(189 core + 120 balance incl. the ledger; **6 injections kept and negative-tested** — one of which caught a loose assertion of mine that the synergy override was quietly satisfying on its own.)

### v10.53 — INFO-1: the wall paid in information, and the information did nothing (2026-08-19)

**The fifth of the ten. What shipped is half of what I built; the other half was measured against four separate dials and retracted, and the numbers are the interesting part of the entry.**

**The finding, and the source had already written it down four times.** `readAhead` was consulted by **nothing but the display** — every one of the four comments about it says so, each as the reason a read is *"band-safe"*. `ENG-24`'s note is the clearest: the top of the flow bar *"pays in INFORMATION instead… which resolution never consults, so it cannot touch the band."* So the wall paid in information and the information was inert.

**Measured before touching anything:** a player holds a read on **5.4%** of climb turns, mean **1.25**, never more than 2 — reads evaporate as the holds they cover arrive. Only **2 cards of 248** grant one, neither is in any reward pool, and `Take It All In` — read 4, the deepest source in the game — valued at **−3.0: the worst card in the pool**, precisely because what it buys had no effect.

#### What shipped

**A hold you read arrives KNOWN.** `holdKnown` honours it, so `gripShown` reads that hold exactly instead of as a `WOBBLE`-wide span. That is a real use rather than reassurance: a whole grip of uncertainty is the difference between a card that clears and one that might, and now you can tell which before you commit. Band-safe by construction — `holdKnown` is read by `gripShown` and by nothing else. The hold says so on the board and in the accessibility tree, because pips quietly ceasing to be a span otherwise reads as the game being inconsistent.

#### What was retracted, and the four dials that could not save it

I made a read hold worth full **beta** — the game's own word for knowing a hold, which already does exactly the two things knowing should do (`BETA_GRIP` off the grip, exact in the preview). It worked, and it lifted both read cards out of the dead set: `Sight the Line` 5.2 → **12.9**, `Take It All In` −3.0 → **12.4**. It also took the pinned band **44.3% → 46.6% (n=3000)**.

| dial | measured |
|---|---|
| cost both read cards a pump more | **45.7%** |
| halve `Sight the Line`'s depth (2 → 1) | **45.7%** — *no change at all* |
| restore that, raise both prices again | **45.6%** |
| cap the passive sources (FLOW_READ, every signature's read) at one hold | **45.6%** |

**The reason is structural, which is why it is not a tuning problem.** `refillAndDraw` brings at most two holds up a turn, and a read is a *maximum* the player tops up the moment it empties — so if you hold any read source at all, very nearly **every** hold arrives read whatever the depth or the price. It is not a bonus with a size; it is a flat one-to-two grip discount on the whole wall. An isolation run pinned the source: with the read cards **never played** it still read **45.1% (n=2000)**, so the free passive sources — 35 signature holds and the flow bar — carry most of it. Those numbers were all set when a read cost the game nothing.

So the grip half is out, and the guard for it is now the assertion that a read hold is **not cheaper**. `read` goes back to being situational in the dead-card guard for exactly `CARD-18`'s reason: pricing foreknowledge needs a policy that *plans*, which is `SIM-7`'s ground.

**Two guards had to be restated rather than relaxed.** `CARD-18`'s *"reading is information only"* now has a flag in play that must not disturb it — a hold you read is marked, the preview reads it exactly, and the resolution comes out identical either way, with the flag proven to be the only difference. And `RUN-9`'s guard hardcoded the number 2 for `Sight the Line`'s depth; it reads the card's own value now, so it fails on the property rather than on a content edit.

**Three bugs in my own guard, found by the injection pass:** a fixture that never emptied a lane, so no hold ever arrived under the read and every assertion passed for free; a self-referential assertion comparing a value to itself; and a visual and a spoken assertion that both matched the same shared condition, so removing the drawn line tripped the *spoken* one.

**Band: 43.8% (n=3000) — identical to v10.52.**

(188 core + 108 balance; **7 injections kept and negative-tested**, one of which is the retraction.)

### v10.52 — RUN-14: the map was the same 26 stages in every run ever played (2026-08-19)

**The fourth of the ten, and the third row in a row whose premise was wrong.** It said *"act maps branch, but the branches rarely differ in kind"*. They differ in kind at **18 of the 26 stages**, and at the 4 climb-only stages the routes differ in grade, style, clear count and how hard the feet are.

**What is true is simpler and worse.** `ACTS` is a static table, so the map was **byte-identical in every run anybody ever played** — the same 26 stages, the same order, the same options, forever. The *conditions* at each node were already per-run (`forecastFor` hashes the seed with the act, the tier and the node index); the *shape* never was. A second campaign hands you the first campaign's menu.

**A stage's contents are a property of the run now.** One function, `tierNodes(s, tier)`, and everything that asks what a stage offers goes through it. A stage of three or more nodes has one of its **non-climbing** nodes replaced by a camp, an event or a shop it does not already offer, chosen from `runSeed`. **16 of the 26 stages vary, 3–4 shapes each**, and 2,000 seeds produce 2,000 distinct campaign shapes. The 10 that do not vary are all accounted for: 4 boss stages hold one node, 4 are two-node climb pairs at the floor with no support node to move, and 2 already offer all three kinds so there is nothing new to give them.

#### Two versions measured and thrown away, because both numbers say something worth keeping

| version | what it did | measured |
|---|---|---|
| one | **dropped** a node (`length − 1`, floored at 2) | **41.2%** — −3.1, rests 0.7 → 0.4, act 2 deaths 19% → 25% |
| two | **swapped any** node, width preserved | **39.2%** — worse still, talks 5.8 → 7.0, act 3 deaths → 36% |
| three | swaps only the **non-climbing** nodes | **43.8%** — inside the error, talks and rests back on baseline |

**Version one bought variety by taking agency away.** Version two is the interesting one: it kept the width and got *worse*, and the reason is a fact about this game — **sends are what grow a deck**, so trading a climb for a camp is a loss even though a camp hands your skin back. Both failed for one underlying reason: anything that reduces how much you can climb costs several points. Hence the confinement.

**What this deliberately does not do,** stated rather than implied: the climbs themselves are the same every run. Varying those needs a pool of interchangeable routes per act, which does not exist — 36 routes cover 26 stages, nearly all used once — so it is authoring rather than a rule, and version two measured what happens if you fake it by substituting other node kinds. That is `RUN-15`.

**One function, on purpose.** The map screen already carried a note that *"the forecast and every handler address a tier by index, so anything added in the middle would shift them all"* — so a second reading of "which nodes are here" is `ENG-26`'s divergence arriving by the front door. `forecastFor`, `tierAhead`, `bossAhead`, `campBeforeBoss`, the map screen and the act drawing all read `tierNodes`. **`campBeforeBoss` is the one that would have become a lie:** the map prints *"No camp between here and the boss"* off it, and a run that moved its only camp would have been told the opposite on the screen where it matters most. Guarded behaviourally against the run's own stages.

**And the harness was reading the static table** (`sim/run.mjs:157`). Caught before any measurement was taken — left alone it would have reported this ticket as band-neutral while the shipping game dealt something else, which is `SIM-1`'s rule and `ENG-25`'s failure class in one line. The injection for it is kept.

**Derived from `runSeed`, not the live `s.seed`,** which advances as you play: keying off the live seed re-draws a stage under the player between two looks at the same screen. Guarded, and it takes nothing from the run RNG stream so it perturbs no downstream roll.

**Band: 43.8% (n=3000) against the 44.3% pin.**

(188 core + 107 balance; **10 injections kept and negative-tested**, two of which are the retracted versions.)

### v10.51 — FEET-1: the wall could only ever make your feet matter less (2026-08-19)

**The third of the ten. Measuring corrected the row's premise twice before anything was designed, which is becoming the pattern with this set.**

**The choice of foot was already real.** 38 foot cards, **25 distinct power/contact/support profiles, and not one pair where either card beats the other on all three axes.** The lane is filled on **77.5%** of climb turns and you are holding two or more feet on **49.3%** of them. So "a single slot answering a single foothold" understated what was there — `ENG-32` and `SIM-6` had already done real work in this lane.

**What was missing was variety in the question.** Support only ever takes the values **1 and 2** — every powerful foot is Support 1, every Support 2 foot is weak — so it was the same binary trade every turn. And the wall could only push that axis **down**:

| source | effect on Support |
|---|---|
| `Featureless` (the blank) | zeroes it — **28.9%** of the footholds a campaign meets |
| humid weather | −1 |
| two route windows | −1 and −2 |
| Soft Slippers (gear) | **+1** — the only positive in the game, and it is a shoe you buy |

**Nothing the rock did ever made your feet matter more, so the trade never inverted — it only got cancelled.**

**And the fix was sitting in the content table already.** `'foothold': { bite: 2, grip: 2, ability: '', text: '' }` — **the only thing on the board that said nothing at all**, in a table where every hold type and every other foothold has an ability and a sentence. It is 22.6% of the footholds you meet. It is now **`Solid` — a settled foot pays +1 Support**, the first upward pressure from the rock itself.

**Paid a turn late, and that is the design rather than a delay.** `ENG-32` made a rushed placement pay less and its stated trade — *"leaving a good card where it is usually beats moving it"* — had nothing on the other side of it for the feet lane specifically. This is that other side, and it costs something real: a foot left planted is a foot lane you are not re-using, out of about four feet cards in fifteen. Measured swing on a solid foothold: a committed Support 2 foot goes **1 → 3**; every other foothold gives 1 → 2 and holds ENG-32's floor. Brushing strips it (`abilityOf`'s contract). A gifted stance never gets it — there was no placement to settle, the same reason `ENG-21` exempts a stance from the fresh cost. And it reaches the **floored** feet too: a Support 1 foot gains it, which matters because those are exactly the cards that most want a reason to stay put.

**A fifth finding, free to fix and on the same lane:** `FEET_STATS` was read in **exactly one place** in `App.tsx` — the board. Both marks keys listed the four hold types and **none of the four footholds**, so a player who wanted to know what `Featureless` or `Slick` meant had nowhere to look. Both keys carry them now.

**Measured, offset, and the offset is also a correction.** Solid alone took the band **44.3% → 45.9% (n=3000)**, +1.6 against a 0.9-point standard error — real, and not something to accept quietly. The plain foothold was **5 of the 10 weight** on every easy-feet route, so "some routes are footwork routes" would in fact have been *half of act 1*. Moving three of that five onto the smear edge brings Solid down to a characteristic of the rock and reads **45.0% (n=3000)** — inside the error, though on the high side of it, and reported as the number rather than as "unmoved". `normal` and `hard` are untouched and each pool's total weight is unchanged, so no other foothold's share moves.

**Two existing guards failed and both were right to.** `ENG-32`'s settling guard and `SIM-6`'s feet-policy guard both built their fixtures on the plain `foothold`, which now carries a second rule about the same turn; both were moved to a `chip`, which is neutral for Support (`Sharp` is read only by `resolve`'s blow branch), so each isolates its own rule again. The combined behaviour is asserted in the FEET-1 guard instead.

**And the injection pass found two bugs in my own guard**, both of the kinds this project keeps relearning: a source assertion matching `supWith(c, true)` when that pattern appears **twice** in the window — the injection blanked one and the loose form sailed through — and a hand-rolled `indexOf` window that `GUARD-8` refused outright.

(187 core + 107 balance; **11 injections kept and negative-tested**.)

### v10.50 — SEQ-2: five reasons a plan was a side quest, three fixed and two retracted (2026-08-19)

**The second of the ten. It found five defects, and the honest ship is three of them — because two were implemented, measured, and taken back out.** Both retractions are guarded rather than merely commented, so re-adding either fails with its number attached.

**A note on my own roadmap row first, because it was wrong.** I wrote *"nothing on the board makes you plan toward one"*. A running plan is already legible — a green banner with its name, its countdown and its condition, added by `A11Y-9`. The row's premise was half false and the real problem was upstream of the board.

#### What was actually wrong

**ONE — two of the four cards that start a plan were named after other cards.**

| line | card | rarity | what it does |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1151 | `Read the Sequence` | common | Draw 2 |
| 1383 | `Read The Sequence` | uncommon | **starts Static Sequence** |
| 1154 | `Trust the Feet` | common | +2 Power to one lane |
| 1387 | `Trust The Feet` | rare | **starts Committed** |

`CARDS` is keyed `CARDS[c.name] = c`, so both survive as distinct entries and **differ by the case of the word "the"** — which no screen in this game renders differently. The collection, the deck editor, a shelf and a reward offer all read them as one card. And the deck `BUILD ME ONE` hands you contains **`Read the Sequence`, the common** — so the built deck reads as though it has a plan in it and does not. Renamed to `Keep Moving` and `Quiet Feet`; renamed on *that* side because a rename orphans the old name in every save's `owned` list and these are the pair almost nobody holds (1.43% of pack rolls) against two commons every pack roll can hand out. The guard asserts the invariant **pool-wide**, so the recurrence is blocked rather than the two instances.

**TWO — a plan died to one bad draw, and the valuation was right to refuse it.** The four were the four worst-scoring bonus cards in the game: **ranks 57, 58, 59 and 60 of 61**, scoring 0.0–3.6 where the builder's cut-off is ~12.6 and the best bonus is 33.0. `seqValue` was wired in and reasoning correctly — in play a plan was a coin flip (600 campaigns: **55 completed, 53 broken**). A three-turn plan paying two holds that dies to one unlucky draw *is* worth less than a rare that acts now. **`SEQ_GRACE = 1`: a plan survives one miss.** The value rises through reliability, not reward — nothing pays more. The missed turn is **not banked**, so a plan still costs the same number of good turns as its card says.

**THREE — the odds model had the wrong shape, and an off-by-one.** `hold^(turns - 1)` assumed the start turn is free; it is not — `playCard` sets the plan and that same resolution tests it. Replaced with the exact negative-binomial tail: the chance of `turns` successes arriving before `SEQ_GRACE + 1` misses. My first cut used a *binomial* over `turns - 1` trials, which **collapses to certainty on a two-turn plan** — a plan stopped being priced on the deck at all, and the existing guard *a sequence is worth more to a deck that can hold it* failed. Correctly. That guard caught my bug, which is the whole reason it exists.

#### The two retractions

**FOUR — the plan cards were in no reward pool, no shelf and no loadout.** Only a **1.43%** pack roll, so **97% of campaigns never saw one**. The system read as a side quest because it was one. I put all four in `REWARDS`, and measured completion went **44.2% → 26.6% (n=3000)**.

*Dilution, not the cards.* The uncommon pool holds eight names and the rare pool four; four more grows them 37% and 25%, so a quarter of every reward roll becomes a card that pays nothing until a condition has held for two or three turns. Isolated to be sure: with the four back out and every other part of SEQ-2 in place the same measurement reads **45.8% (n=1000)**; with them in but the policy forbidden from ever *playing* a plan it reads **22.9%** — *worse*, so the plans were helping and the pools were the whole of the damage. **This is the ROPE-2 lesson arriving from the opposite direction:** clips in `REWARDS` made the game easier because a cost-0 shed-and-draw is free value; plans made it much harder because a plan is dead weight until you build for it. Neither belongs in a pool that hands cards over at random.

**So I moved it to the shop shelf — sold, not given, exactly the ROPE-2 pattern — and that failed too.** A rotating plan at every post took campaigns holding one from 3.0% to **62.5%**, bought 498 times in 600 campaigns, and measured **44.3% → 40.3% (n=3000)**. Four points, well outside a 0.9-point standard error. No dilution this time: the mechanism is that **`bestOffer` buys the plan instead of a solid move**, because `cardValue` says the plan is worth more. It is not.

**FIVE — and that is why the unit inconsistency I found must NOT be fixed on its own.** `seqValue` prices its payout with `bonusValue`'s per-effect coefficients and then does not apply `BONUS_WEIGHT` to them: `draw` is `2.2` in both expressions and `bonusValue` multiplies it by 3.2. Before this ticket, a **common** drawing 2 *this turn* scored 12.3 and an **uncommon** drawing 3 *two turns from now* scored 3.6. That is a real inconsistency. **Applying the weight lifts the four to ranks 7–34 and is what made the shelf lose four points.** The 3.2× was cancelling a cost this model does not carry at all: a plan does not merely maybe-pay later, it **constrains the play while it runs**, and `norest` forbidding the rest you need in a rising-pump race is a real price. Correcting one side of a two-sided cancellation is worse than leaving both alone, so **the weight is not applied** and the un-weighted number stands — right for the wrong reason, which is worth knowing about it.

**What is left, named as the next ticket rather than implied:** price the constraint, then apply the weight, and only then put a plan somewhere it can be bought. Any one of those alone measurably regresses. Separately, `buildLoadout` is structurally blind to a plan — `Link It Up` scores **−1.2 against an empty deck and +25.0 against the finished one**, because a plan's odds depend on the deck and the greedy builder scores it against the prefix, spending all three `MAX_TECH` slots before a plan is worth anything.

**Band: 44.2% (n=3000), against the 44.3% pin — unmoved, and identical to v10.49's reading.**

(186 core + 107 balance; **12 injections kept and negative-tested**, two of which are the retractions.)

### v10.49 — HOLD-1: 248 cards against seven abilities, none of which outlived a turn (2026-08-19)

**"It still feels like the gameplay needs more to it." The first of the ten items brainstormed against that, and the diagnosis it came from is worth stating because it is measurable rather than a feeling.** The deck has **248 cards**. The wall has **seven abilities** — Rest, Sharp, Razor, Greasy, Squeeze, Two-finger, Committing — and every one of them is a static modifier to the same turn's arithmetic. So the depth was all on one side of the board: your hand changed every turn and the question it answered never did.

**The specific hole.** Leaving a hold unanswered cost `HANG_TAX` per hold and nothing else, which means **WHICH hold you left never mattered, only how many.** Three lanes, one number. There was no triage decision on the wall because there was nothing to triage between.

**A Greasy hold now sweats up while you are elsewhere.** +1 Grip for every turn you leave it hanging, to a cap of 3. The hold you deal with is the hold that stops; the hold you left is getting worse, and you can feel the order you should have gone in.

**Four things had to be true for that to be a decision rather than an ambush or a soft lock, and each is a separate assertion.**

**It is keyed on the ABILITY, not the hold type.** The board already prints GREASY on the hold, so "greasy holds sweat up" is one rule learned once. Keyed on the type it would have been true of the sloper and **silently false of The Wet Jug** — a jug the game also calls Greasy — the same word on the board with a different rule behind it. There are three such holds (The Wet Jug, The Black Glass, The Furnace: a jug, a crimp and a jug). Going through `abilityOf` also inherits two behaviours instead of restating them: a signature hold's own ability wins, and **a CLEAN hold has no ability at all**, so working or brushing a hold now *stops the clock* on it rather than only outrunning it. What it has already cost you stays paid — the grease comes off, the skin does not grow back.

**It is capped at 3.** Uncapped, a lane you cannot answer runs to unanswerable and takes the burn with it, which is not tension but a soft lock with extra steps.

**One predicate, two consequences.** The hang tax and the wear are charged off the *same list* of lanes you left, so `unanswered` is that list's length rather than a second walk of the board. Two walks of one rule is exactly how `ENG-26` got a preview that disagreed with the resolution, and it would have arrived here in a new form. The wear is written into `grip`, not held beside it, so `effGrip` — and therefore `gripFor`, `gripShown`, the preview and the resolution — all pick it up with **no second code path to keep in step**. It runs before `refillAndDraw`, so a hold that arrives this turn has not been left yet.

**And the hold says so.** `ROUTE-6`'s weather windows and the boss phases both set the rule that the wall telegraphs before it lands. The hold prints the rate and the wear already on it (`sweating +1/turn · +2 so far`, then `soaked +3`), the same sentence exists in the accessibility tree, it is words rather than colour alone (`VIS-7`), and the tutorial's hang-tax step now teaches it.

**Called SWEATING, not greasing, on purpose.** The route move `MOVE_WET` already owns *"greases up"* and the tutorial teaches it as one of the **wall's** moves. Two mechanics under one phrase is how you get a player who cannot tell the weather from their own hands. The Furnace's own note — *"the one good hold sweats you off it"* — is where the game had already put this idea.

**Measured, and the honest version of the result.** `PAGES=14 SHARP_AT=99 campaign 3000`: **44.2%** against the 44.3% pin — flat, inside the 0.9-pt standard error, no re-pin. But *flat* is not *dead*, and the difference matters, so the lever was checked rather than assumed: at **4x** the rate and 4x the cap the same measurement reads **42.8%**. The mechanism is connected and worth roughly 0.35 points at the shipped value. It reads small in the harness because `autoPlay` has perfect information and answers a sloper whenever it can; the mechanic bites the player who has a hand they cannot fully spend, which is the case the harness does not model. That is a limit of the ruler, stated rather than glossed, not a claim the change does nothing.

**New family.** `HOLD` — the wall's abilities, as distinct from `ROUTE`'s lines and conditions. Registered in the guidelines above.

(186 core + 106 balance; **12 injections kept and negative-tested**, all twelve caught first time because the failure messages were measured off real runs rather than guessed.)

### v10.48 — ROPE-2 finished: the one route whose shape you choose (2026-08-17)
**Spec §3 and §4. `design/rope-2.md` is closed.**

**The Lost Line is now the only route in the game you decide the shape of, and you decide it on the ground.** Two explicit buttons on its map card rather than a tap, so neither option is the one you get by not reading:

```
0/14 of his pages — every hold is +6 Grip for what you have not
compression · top out at 15 · 6 crux
He went alone and told her he was on the Cathedral.
  [ SOLO IT ▸ ]  [ ROPE UP ▸ ]
Roped: three pitches, and a belay wipes the pump. The headwall is above the last one.
```

With nothing to clip the second button is disabled and reads **NO RACK** — *"Nothing to clip. A post in the alpine sells protection."* — which closes the loop with v10.47.

**What pays for the belays, and neither half is a special case.** A belay is `pump: 0, runout: 0`, which is a large gift on a route whose difficulty *is* accumulated pump. Against it: the rack takes deck slots that would have been moves (ROPE-1's trade), and **the headwall sits above the last belay**. `pitches: 3` puts belays at **5 and 10 of 15**, and the Headwall begins at **hold 13** — so the final third is still one unbroken push, `noRest`, with the window already shut. **You buy two resets and you do not buy the top.**

That property falls out of the arithmetic rather than being written in, so it is asserted — move the phase or change the pitch count and it still has to hold. Everything else is identical, compared field by field: same grade, style, clear, crux, feet, phases, window. **The same route, climbed differently, not an easier variant.**

**§4, and it is the reason this is a story rather than a mode.** His fourth page: *"Told Marge I was working the Cathedral. She would come if I asked and I am not asking. This one is mine to be stupid about."* So roping up is a choice **against his example**. The epilogue gains one line on how you got up, and **Marge** — the person who would have come if he had asked — gets a reply only a roped ascent can give:

> *"He would have called that a lack of commitment. He was wrong about that, and it took me twenty-two years and a funeral to be sure."*

**Neither option pays better**, asserted by comparing the two replies' outcomes field by field. The moment one does, the decision stops being about him and becomes an optimisation.

**And I walked into NARR-17's trap inside the same session.** I gated her reply on `ropedUp` — run state — while her line needs the **summit page** and therefore fires a trip *later*, after `newRun` has cleared it. **The reply was unreachable, and the comment I wrote above it claimed the opposite.** Caught only because I tested reachability instead of trusting my own comment: `repliesFor` returned one reply, not two, on the expedition where the talk can happen. There is a durable `ropedFinale` now, carried by `carryOver`, with the per-attempt flag asserted **not** to leak.

**Off band by construction and measured anyway:** `newRun` defaults to soloing, so the harness never ropes it — asserted — and the band read **45.0%** with act 3 deaths back to 32%.

**Process, and this was the worst of the session.** While probing which assertion a mutant fired, a `cp` from a stale scratch backup **reinstated the very change I had retracted an hour earlier** — the code sat directly under a comment reading *"Left exactly as it was."* Nothing shipped wrong (v10.47 was already pushed and clean) and the balance guard caught it immediately, refusing the same change for the second time. But hand-patching source to read a message has now caused trouble three times today: once colliding with the mutation harness, once clobbering a revert. The harness restores from its own snapshot and never leaves the tree dirty; ad-hoc `cp` backups do. **Use `git diff` against the repo, not scratch copies.**

Two injections were also mislabelled. `pitches: 5` was wrong twice — it left the headwall property intact (last belay 12, headwall 13) *and* orphaned four sibling anchors that reference `pitches: 3`; it moves the **phase** now, which is the likelier future edit anyway. And I re-drove the browser against a **stale `docs/`** while the ship was still writing, exactly the UX-19 mistake I had recorded and said I had learned.

(185 core + 118 balance; **14 ROPE-2 injections kept and negative-tested**. Rendered in a browser both ways round.)

### v10.47 — ROPE-2: the rack ROPE-1 built could not be got, and the change I recommended was wrong (2026-08-17)
**Two findings. One shipped, one retracted, and the retracted one is the more useful entry.**

**WHAT SHIPPED. The three pieces of protection were unobtainable.** `Quickdraw`, `Wired Nut` and `Bomber Cam` exist in `CARDS`, carry `clip: true`, and are what ROPE-1's whole decision rests on — *"carrying protection costs deck slots and speed and insures against catastrophe"*. They were in **no reward pool and no starting loadout**, default or archetype. **Measured: 4,000 act-3 shop stockings offered a piece 0.0% of the time.** The single exception proves it — the `moraine` event's *"Work it free"* hands over a Wired Nut, which needs a trail node, that event, and that branch.

Meanwhile the map card says *"Nothing on your rack. You can lead it, but you will be climbing scared"* and `coach()` warns you about running it out. **The game described a rack you could not have.**

**Sold, not given, and that distinction is measured rather than asserted.** My first attempt put the three into `REWARDS` at their own rarities, on the reasoning that a clip is **narrow** — it does nothing on the thirty-three lines that are not roped — so it would be a draft decision rather than free power. **The band went 44.3% → 48.0% at n=3000 and act 3 deaths FELL from 33% to 29%: adding a catastrophic risk made the campaign easier.** The reasoning was wrong about the card, not the rope. The clip is narrow; `shed 1, draw 1` at cost 0 is not, and free cycling beats its face value in a deck game — compare `Breathe`, a cost-0 common that sheds 2 and draws nothing.

So the post in any act that has ropes keeps one on the shelf, **priced**, rotating by stage. Picked from the **stage rather than the run RNG**, which is the rule the kit shelf states two lines above it and for the same reason: drawing from the shared stream shifts every downstream roll and moves the balance guards for nothing. And *"any act that has ropes"* is **derived from that act's own map**, so a future roped line provisions itself. Band **45.5%**, and the drift guard accepted it.

**WHAT WAS RETRACTED, and it was my recommendation.** I read `lastPiece: spec.roped ? 0 : -1` as a symptom patch: it makes the ground-fall branch in `resolve` unreachable, and I measured **1,600 sessions with the clip cards stripped out and zero ground falls**. I proposed `-1` — a ground fall only below your first piece on the first pitch, since above a belay the anchor really does catch you — recommended it, and Evan chose it on that recommendation.

**The suite refused it by name.** `sim/test.mjs` has carried `a roped route never starts you on nothing` all along:

> *69% of roped attempts used to end as "ground falls" because the rope was treated as beginning from no protection at all. You are tied to the belay.*

Deliberate, measured, guarded — and **the 49–86% ground-fall rate I measured for a rackless climber is that same 69% problem, re-derived.** I found the number the fix was made for and then proposed reintroducing it. The spec never mentioned the guard because I never found it; the `coach()` line I cited as evidence of intent is better read as a leftover from *before* the belay fix.

**It is now a kept injection**, so the next person to try it meets the guard in seconds rather than after a measurement. And `design/rope-2.md` carries the retraction **at the top**, above the reasoning it invalidates.

**And a dead line turned live.** `coach()` promised *"come off now and it is a ground fall"* — unreachable since the belay became your first piece. The state it reached for is real and worse than it sounds: with only the belay in, a fall costs the **entire pitch**, not the ground above your last piece. It says that now. That is also the answer to what the rope's tension IS, which is option (b) of the spec, and it was there all along.

(184 core + 118 balance; **6 injections kept and negative-tested**, one relabelled after measuring — an RNG-drawn shelf reads as a shelf that *never varies* when every stocking gets the same seed, so the rotation assertion fired before the determinism one. Determinism is checked first now.)

### v10.46 — ROUTE-16: two of the four roped lines were one line under two names (2026-08-17)
**Found by accident, and by arithmetic rather than by reading.** I was baselining `ROPE-2` and **The Cornice and The Hanging Slab reported identical send rates, identical ground-fall rates and identical caught-falls-per-session** across 600 sessions each, on different seeds. Three statistics agreeing to three figures is not something sampling does.

They were the same route:

| | grade | style | clear | crux | feet | roped | pitches | signature |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| The Cornice | 8 | slab | 14 | 3 | hard | yes | 2 | sloper, dGrip 3, read 1 |
| The Hanging Slab | 8 | slab | 14 | 3 | hard | yes | 2 | sloper, dGrip 3, read 1 |

Only the name, the note and the signature's own name differed. **So the rope inventory was wrong in the ROPE-2 spec and in what I told Evan: not four roped routes of thirty-seven, but three distinct lines, one of them shipping twice** — which matters rather a lot for a ticket whose entire premise is that the rope is under-used.

**The fix was already written in the route's own note.** *"Two pitches of slab under a band of ice that lets go in the afternoon"* is a closing weather window — `ROUTE-6`'s mechanic, which the finale uses and no other scripted line does. The prose was not changed to fit a fix; the fix is what the prose had been promising all along. It lands at **0.5** rather than the finale's 0.65 (two pitches, and the note says afternoon) and takes the **feet** (`dSupport: -2`) rather than sharpening everything, because ice releasing over slab is a Support problem — and **never Power**, which is ENG-20's absolute rule.

Measured, n=600 per route: with a rack, **80.8% → 76.0%**. Without one the pair now reads 36.2 and 41.3, and that difference is **not** evidence the window helped — changing `dSupport` shifts the hold stream, so those are different random walks rather than a before/after on matched seeds. It is the reshuffle effect `CARD-11` documented, and it is worth saying so instead of banking the number.

**Band: 44.1% at n=3000** against the ~44.3 pin — 0.2pt, inside the ~0.9pt standard error — with act 3 deaths 32% → 33%, which is what one slightly harder act 3 route ought to look like.

**Why nothing caught it.** `ROUTE-12` asserts every signature is real, is named, is used at most once, and **does something**. All four passed. Not one of them asks whether two signatures do the **same** thing, or whether two routes are the same route.

**And the new guard corrected me twice, by being run.** I first wrote it as *"no two signatures may be mechanically identical anywhere"*:

- it flagged **`therail` and `thesitdown`** — which *should* be identical. They are `ROUTE-15`'s `local` pool, which pays in information and never in power (`read: 1`, nothing else) precisely so naming a hold cannot move the band. Identical mechanics is the point of them.
- it flagged **`thenave` and `softtouch`** — identical mechanics, but on genuinely different routes (grade 4 mixed clear 10 vs grade 3 crimp-ladder clear 11). That is duplicated **flavour**, not duplicated gameplay, and much smaller. Logged, not asserted.

So the rule is now the one that actually carries the defect: **routes of the same shape must have signatures that differ mechanically.** The Cornice and The Hanging Slab failed it; neither of those pairs does. What survives standalone is only that a `local` signature must never grow teeth, because that would make naming a hold band-active by the back door.

(183 core + 118 balance; **5 injections kept and negative-tested**, one relabelled after measuring which assertion fired.)

### v10.45 — UX-19: the tutorial pushed COMMIT off the screen, and the bar meant to stop that had never worked (2026-08-17)
**Reported from a phone, with a screenshot: on the first-run tutorial, COMMIT could not be reached.** Two things were wrong and only one of them was the tutorial.

**The tutorial half.** The teaching banner costs **75px at NORMAL text and 113px at LARGER**, and the climb screen has no slack. Measured page overflow on the tutorial climb:

| viewport / text | overflow | COMMIT on screen unscrolled |
|---|---|---|
| 874 / NORMAL | 8px | yes, barely |
| 740 / NORMAL | **142px** | **no** |
| 874 / LARGE | 107px | **no** |
| 874 / LARGER | 252px | **no** |

So on any phone shorter than about **870 CSS px**, a first-timer is shown a banner telling them to press a button that is not on the screen.

**The half that matters more. `A11Y-5`'s thumb-reachable COMMIT bar — the thing built to prevent exactly this — had never worked for anybody.** It was `position: sticky`, and a bare sticky-bottom control div dropped into the same parent sat at **1170px in an 874px viewport**. So `DEV-1`'s goal of keeping COMMIT out of the OS swipe-up zone was never met either. **The cause of the sticky failure is still unexplained** — it is not `.wrap` being a scroll container, and `overflow-x: clip` on `html,body` did not fix it. `position: fixed` sidesteps the question rather than answering it, and that is written down rather than dressed up.

**The fix.** The controls are **fixed**, centred on the content column, 15px above the gesture strip, and **ungated** — the bar used to be behind the one-handed setting, and "the primary action must be reachable" is not a preference. Fixed because its position does not depend on scroll semantics, on which ancestor is a scroll container, or on the page scrolling at all — **which is also the only reason it is verifiable**: the rect either sits inside the viewport or it does not. Verified by geometry *and* by hit-test — the topmost element at COMMIT's centre is the button — at 740/NORMAL, 874/NORMAL, 874/LARGER, 640/LARGER and one-handed-left. **All five: in view, tappable, 15px clear.**

The **bail row moved above the controls**. A fixed bar covers whatever follows it in flow, and it did cover the bail link on a viewport tall enough not to scroll; reserving space for it proved fiddlier than removing the problem, so nothing trails the bar now. It reads better too — a secondary way out does not belong below the primary action.

**Process, and there was a lot of it.**

- **I retracted a reproduction.** I "reproduced" the un-scrollable page with Chrome's touch-gesture synthesiser, then checked the instrument: a plain 3000px control page **also** reported zero scroll. Headless Chromium here cannot drive compositor touch scrolling, so both "as shipped does not scroll" and "the fix does not help" were artefacts. Caught before it reached a fix, but only just.
- **Backticks inside the `const CSS` template literal, twice** — the same trap, in consecutive edits.
- **The first cut of the CSS lost on specificity.** A bare `.climb-foot` is (0,1,0) and the weather tint's `.wrap[class*="wx-"]>*{position:relative}` is (0,2,0), so the bar computed to `relative` and did not move. Found by the hit-test, not by reading it.
- **Three `region()` anchors in my own new guard contained the very text their injections rewrite**, so all three failed through GUARD-8's *"the opening anchor is gone"* instead of naming the defect. That is the anchor-must-not-contain-what-it-tests rule, broken three times in one guard, and it is why the guard now matches the declaration as a whole and checks the selector before the declaration.

(182 core + 118 balance; **7 injections kept and negative-tested**. No band effect — layout only.)

### v10.44 — ENG-9: engine.ts 6,527 → 5,882, and the reason v6.6 failed (2026-08-17)
**Attempted at v6.6 and reverted. The row said it fell over on "mechanical import surgery, not on design." That was half right, and the other half is why it worked this time.**

**Two rules, both now asserted rather than remembered.**

**One: `engine.ts` re-exports everything it moves out.** `App.tsx` and `sim/entry.ts` were **not touched by any of this** — they still import every name from `./engine`. That is what "mechanical import surgery" turns into when you refuse to do it: zero lines. The guard fails if a caller ever reaches past the engine, or if a moved name stops being re-exported.

**Two: data out, rules stay.** And this is the half the old row did not say. `CARDS` looks like the biggest content block in the file — 346 lines — and it is **not content**:

```ts
export const CARDS: Record<string, CardDef> = {}
for (const c of [ mv('Crimp Grip', 2, 5, 'starter', {...}), ... ]) ...
```

Data **built by rules**. Moving it moves the `mv` factories, and then the content file holds rules and the split has bought a longer import list and nothing else. Same verdict for `CONTENT: DIALOGUE`: 602 lines that are `TALKS` wrapped in `talkOpen`, `availableTalk`, `postTalk`, `metCount` and `loreFor`, every one of which needs `GameState`. **Both correctly stayed, and the guard fails if either arrives.** *That* is what v6.6 hit — not imports, but a thousand lines of rules wearing content's clothes.

**What moved, in three passes:**

| | lines out | guard fixes | injection paths | caller changes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `src/types.ts` — leaf types | 61 | **1** | 0 | **0** |
| `src/content.ts` — ascent, weather, rock, journal, sequences, mutators, boons | 190 | **0** | 0 | **0** |
| `src/content.ts` — 37 routes, 36 events | 406 | 0 | **11** | **0** |
| **total** | **645** | **1** | **11** | **0** |

`engine.ts` **6,527 → 5,882 (−9.9%)**, `content.ts` 642, `types.ts` 88.

**Measured before starting instead of guessed, and it corrected me.** I told Evan the churn would get *worse* in the content sections. It does not: across all 2,350 `CONTENT:` lines there are **42 injection anchors and only seven guard windows**, about **two references per 100 lines**, and an injection's path is a one-token edit. The expensive part was never the references.

**The net did its job three times.** `UI-4` broke loudly — it read `export type NodeType` out of engine.ts by regex and reported *"read 0 node types"*. Repointing it made it **stronger**: it now derives the list from `E.ACTS`, so every node type that **actually appears on a map** needs a mark, where before a union member no map used was demanded one. `GUARD-9` named all six orphaned injection anchors by id. And `region()` never once returned a bad window, because it throws instead.

**And an honest ceiling, revised downward.** I quoted 2,350 movable lines. About **1,000 of those are rules** — CARDS and the dialogue section — so the real ceiling is nearer 1,350, of which 645 is now done.

**Process.** I invented `E.NODE_KINDS` fixing UI-4 (it does not exist), left a malformed `for` line in the new guard, and wrote the cycle assertion as `!A || B` — which the injection walked straight through, because adding a value import *alongside* the type import makes both halves true. **The fourth vacuous assertion of the session, and the second caught by its own ticket's injection rather than versions later.** It counts value imports now.

(181 core + 118 balance; **5 injections kept and negative-tested**. Campaign band **44.3%**.)

### v10.43 — NARR-21: the audit was closed against a bracket, not a measurement (2026-08-17)
**A gap in the five versions that came before this one, found by looking back at them rather than forward.**

The whole campaign audit — `NARR-16` through `NARR-20` — was measured against two harness policies: **never take a trail node, and take every one**. They produced `known` ending figures of **0.0% and 51.1%**, which *bracket* the truth without ever measuring it. Worse, **`NARR-18` is unmeasurable by either**, because it changed what the node *says* and the sim cannot read. Five versions of narrative work and no number anybody could defend.

`reads` is the player that node is now written for: they know his pages turn up out there, so they take it **while there are still pages to find** and stop once the journal is full. Measured at 60 careers × 8 expeditions:

| policy | completion | pages/15 | `known` | `partial` | `stranger` |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| climbs | 28.3% | 6.0 | **0.0%** | 82.0% | 18.0% |
| reads | 41.7% | 11.2 | **43.1%** | 48.3% | 8.6% |

**And the finding is that `reads` is indistinguishable from `events`.** At 200×10 they measure **50.7% and 51.1%** known, **12.5 pages each**. The clause I added — stop once the journal is full — almost never fires inside a realistic career, because *the journal does not fill inside one*.

That is not a defect in the model. **It is the answer.** The trail node costs nothing — completion is *higher* with it, 41.7% against 28.3%, because pages become beta — so the optimal informed policy really is "take every one". **The two "extremes" were never extremes.** They were *believes the label* and *does not*, and the gap between them is what `NARR-18` bought: **about 43 points of the `known` ending and 5 pages**.

**The delta is asserted, not just the band**, because a band on `reads` alone would still pass if the node became free for everybody. And the fourth injection is the one that matters most: **every policy starts taking the trail node**, which collapses the two arms of the comparison into one and makes the whole measurement vacuous *while still passing its own band*. That is the failure mode a comparison-based guard has, and it now has a name.

**An honest gap, left open rather than papered over.** The `nextPage` clause in `reads` carries **no injection**, because removing it makes no measurable difference today — `reads` and `events` are the same policy until page fourteen. It is kept because it states the intent and because the two diverge the moment anything makes the journal fill faster; but coverage is coverage, and it does not have any.

**And the question no harness can answer:** whether a human reads the label. That is the last thing open in the audit, and it needs playing rather than measuring.

The ledger is **8m44s** now. Two career runs is ~28s of that, which is the price of having a narrative band at all.

(180 core + **118** balance; **4 injections kept and negative-tested** — one relabelled after measuring, which is the fourth time in six versions that I guessed which assertion would fire instead of checking. Campaign band **44.3%**.)

### v10.42 — NARR-20: the largest body of prose in the game did not know the story existed (2026-08-17)
Thirty-six events, more words than the journal and the conversations put together, and **nine of them knew there was a story**. The biggest thing in the game was narratively inert while the smallest thing in it carried the whole campaign.

**Two things this is deliberately not.** It is not more events — the original ticket said *"give the events story awareness, rather than more of them"*. And it is not sprinkling the dead man through all thirty-six: **a world where every rockfall reminds you of him is worse, not better**, so the restraint is asserted rather than trusted (`withLore.length < EVENTS.length / 2` fails if somebody gets enthusiastic).

**What it is: ten events answer a page he wrote, and only for somebody carrying that page.** The terrain starts recognising him as you read him.

| the event | the page | what you notice |
|---|---|---|
| Seepage | 3 · *Conditions* | *"Nine days a year, he reckoned… You are standing in the rest of the time."* |
| A Line Nobody Has Done | 9 · *On the rock* | *"Everything up here is rubbish, and then somewhere one fin of something hard and grey… This is the rubbish. That is almost encouraging."* |
| Somebody Left A Bag | 15 · *On the name* | *"No name in it. He had a whole page about that… Somebody else may just have been careless."* |
| Rockfall | 12 · *On being frightened* | *"…willingness to be frightened for nine hours. You have managed about four seconds and you would like that on the record."* |
| The Old Campsite | 4 · *On going alone* | *"He told Marge he was working the Cathedral. This is not the Cathedral."* |
| Under the Moss | 1 · *First entry* | *"He sat under his for a whole evening and did not touch it. You could do that. You are not going to."* |

plus *Weather Coming In* ← 14, *A Friendly Grade* ← 2, *The Logging Road* ← 10, *The Window* ← 6.

**A specific page, on purpose, and it is the one place that is right.** NARR-17 was a lesson about gating **progression** on a named page — nothing there could open without it, and ten of fourteen page grants sat behind the gate. **Nothing depends on this**: miss the page, miss a sentence. And a count gate would print *"he wrote about this rock"* to somebody who has read three pages about the weather, which is the whole effect thrown away. The guard states the distinction so the next person does not "fix" it into a count.

**Text and never power.** `lore` has no outcome and cannot grow one — checked on the data as well as the type, because a stray field is not a type error — and `loreFor` is asserted to be **called exactly once in the engine, the declaration itself**. The moment resolution reads a sentence, the sentence is a number. That is what makes the whole ticket **band-neutral by construction rather than by measurement**, which is the same argument ROUTE-10, ENG-24 and NARR-12 settled.

**Rendered in a browser, both ways round**, because half of this feature is its absence: with a full journal, *Somebody Left A Bag* carries the block; with none, *A Friendly Grade* — which **has** a line, keyed to page 2 — correctly shows nothing. The pool was forced by marking every event with nothing to say as already had, since `rollEvent` is random and the first attempt rolled `oldtimer` and proved nothing either way.

**What the guard cannot check** is whether each line actually answers its page. That is a reading, not an assertion, and it is written down here instead of pretended at. What it does check is every way the mechanism goes wrong: a page that is not findable, a page answered twice, a line shorter than a sentence, a line that repeats the event's own text, the gate ignored, the gate turned into a count, a generic fallback for events with nothing to say, the screen gating itself, and resolution reading any of it.

**9 injections, 9 caught on the first pass** — the first ticket in this run where none of my labels were wrong.

(180 core + 106 balance; **44.3%** at `PAGES=14`.)

### v10.41 — NARR-19: seven beats over six expeditions, and nowhere to read it back (2026-08-17)
**The last of the campaign audit's four, and the only one that adds rather than repairs.**

`seen` recorded **that** a conversation had happened and nothing whatever about it, and your own half was discarded the moment you left the fire. Marge's thread is **seven beats delivered across about six expeditions** — days of real time — so an arc arrived as seven half-remembered moments with no way to look at it. **Who You Have Met**, in the Books, keeps both halves:

```
MARGE
  I looked for two seasons. Walked every drainage on the north side with a
  photocopy of a survey photo. You know what I found? Good bouldering.
  you said something. This was before anyone was writing it down.

  That is his hand. I would know it upside down in the rain. Where.
  you · Under a cairn, well off the trail.
```

**Grouped by person and in authored order**, because that is what makes it an arc rather than a log — `after` chains the beats, so the authored order is the only order they can happen in, and a trip-ordered list would interleave five people into noise.

**Three deliberate refusals.** The reply is stored as its **label**, not its index — an index rots the moment a reply is reordered or reworded, and it rots *silently*, showing you a sentence you never said. Conversations from before this shipped are in `seen` and absent from `said`, so the screen **says so** instead of inventing an answer. And `fa:`/`con:` conversations — the ones generated about lines you put up yourself — are counted and named but **not quoted**, because their prose is rebuilt from current state and a transcript of them would show text nobody ever said.

**SAVE-7's guard fired, on the first new account field since it was written.** `said` went into `saveGame` **first, on purpose**, to find out whether that guard had actually bought anything:

```
saveGame keeps said as account state and carryOver does not hand it to
a new expedition — that is SAVE-7 again
```

A transcript that emptied itself every trip would have been a feature whose entire subject is memory, losing its memory. That is what a diff-shaped guard is for, and it is the reason SAVE-7 was written as a diff instead of as two field names.

**And an existing navigation guard caught the new screen** with a BACK button and no parent, before I ever opened a browser.

**A map needs its own clamp.** `said` is read straight off disk, and SAVE-6's `cap` only knows about arrays. Count, key length and value length are all bounded — one 10MB value is the same attack as a million short ones — and the count is derived from the cast plus the lines you can put up, which is every id that can ever be a key.

**Three of my eleven injections were mislabelled, and chasing them found two real faults in my own guard.**

- `ok(/said:/.test(reply))` was **vacuous**: `said: st.said` matches it *and throws the answer away*, which is exactly the bug. It asserts `[talk.id]:` now — something recorded against **this** conversation.
- Removing the loader's type filter made the test **crash rather than fail**, and the reason is a genuine hole: `.slice` on a number throws, `loadGame` catches, and **one crafted value makes the entire slot read as unreadable**. That is SAVE-6's sharpest lesson — a crafted field must be *dropped*, not take the save down with it — and it now has its own assertion and its own injection, separate from the one about coercion.

Both were found by injections written for this ticket rather than surfacing versions later, which is the second time in three versions that has happened.

**Off band by construction:** nothing in the change is reachable from the harness — the sim never renders, and `said` is written only where a human taps a reply. **44.3%** at `PAGES=14`.

(179 core + 106 balance; **11 injections kept and negative-tested**.)

### v10.40 — NARR-18: the only door to the campaign advertised nothing (2026-08-17)
**Not a defect in the code. A defect in what the game was willing to tell you.**

Every node on the map sells itself with numbers — a grade, a forecast, a price, a pitch count, which holds you can read. **One did not:**

> **Something on the trail** · ? · *No climbing. No telling.*

And that node is the only route to his journal outside the conversations. The two bracketing measurements from the audit say what it cost:

| | takes trail nodes | never takes one |
|---|---|---|
| `known` ending | **51.1%** | **0.0%** |
| campaign completion by trip ten | **44.7%** | 37% |

**Taking them is not even a sacrifice — those careers finish MORE often**, because pages become beta cards. The story-seeking path was strictly better in every dimension and the game never said so. A player was choosing between a graded route with a weather report and a blank, and the blank was where the campaign lived.

**What it says now, and what it deliberately still does not.** *"No climbing. No telling."* is untouched: `rollEvent` picks the event **after** you commit, and the not-knowing is the entire node. What is added is the one thing true of every one of them — *his pages turn up out here · you are carrying 3 of 14* — read off `pagesHeld` and `FINDABLE` rather than counted on the spot.

**And the journal screen now says what the pages are FOR.** It said *"They keep between runs"* and never once that they are the finale's beta and the ending's threshold. A player who does not know that has no reason to walk up a drainage looking for them. Both numbers come off the functions that impose them — `unreadGrip`, the same call the wall makes, and `KNOWN_AT` — so the screen cannot promise a different game from the one it gives you. **Rendered in a browser at 0 pages and at 7**, and the Grip figure moves from +6 to +3, which is NARR-16's fix visible on screen.

**The guard pulls against itself on purpose.** One half requires the node to say enough that the choice is informed; the other forbids it from naming the event, because that would both spoil it and be wrong most of the time. A guard with only the first half is satisfied by printing the event id.

**Two process notes, both about my own guards.**

The browser check found the change **absent** on first look — I was driving the shipped `docs/` build, which was v10.39 and predated the edit. Rebuild, re-drive, and it was there. Worth keeping because the failure mode looks exactly like "the code does not work".

And the negative test caught a **vacuous half** of my own assertion: `ok(/his pages/.test(node))` is satisfied by **either** branch of the ternary, because both states say "his pages" — so blanking the branch that advertises still passed. That is the **repeated-fragment** trap, now the most frequent way a guard in this project has gone hollow, and this is the first time it has been caught by the injection written for the same ticket rather than versions later. The assertion counts to two now: there are two states, and the node must speak in both.

**Off band by construction:** the sim does not read UI text, and nothing outside the two screens changed. **44.3%** at `PAGES=14`.

(178 core + 106 balance; **6 injections kept and negative-tested**.)

### v10.39 — NARR-17: the story's spine dead-ended in 100% of careers (2026-08-17)
**The audit's headline finding, fixed. Nobody who has ever played this game has seen the back half of Marge's thread.**

**What the gate was.** `marge4` — *"That is his hand. I would know it upside down in the rain. Where."* — gated on **page 4**. The journal is interleaved on purpose (`1, 8, 2, 9, 3, 10, 4, ...`), so page 4 is the **seventh** page delivered, and `nextPage` hands them out strictly in that order. So the line wanted **seven page grants**. And **ten of the fourteen page grants in the game live inside the Marge chain that page 4 unblocks.** Pages gated the talks; the talks supplied the pages.

**Measured, 200 careers × 10 expeditions, on the policy that plays to climb:**

| | before | after |
|---|---|---|
| `marge4` *"That is his hand."* | **0.0%** | **100.0%** |
| `marge5` — she gives you the drainage | 0.0% | 99.0% |
| `marge6` *"You have got that look."* | 0.0% | 95.0% |
| `marge7` *"Did he name it?"* | 0.0% | 92.5% |
| `marge8` | 0.0% | 88.0% |
| all twenty conversations within ten trips | 0.0% | **85.5%**, median trip 6 |
| journal pages | plateaued at 4.8 | 6.0, still climbing |
| epilogue reads `stranger` | 35.5% | **15.1%** |

**A gate on a count cannot deadlock**, which is the whole point: any page satisfies it, so the supply is never downstream of the gate. It is also what the scene means — she is recognising a body of handwriting, and it does not matter which page you hand her. Three pages, which clears on the climbing policy.

**The one gate that really is a page is now named.** `marge6` was `needsPage: 7`, and page 7 is the one he wrote at the top — you get it by getting there. That is `needsSummit: true` now, because *"he wrote this at the summit"* was the entire reason and the number said none of it. The same fact was spelled `7` in **nine places across three files**; it is `SUMMIT_PAGE` in all of them. NARR-16 was two expressions of "six" drifting apart, and this was nine of "seven" waiting to.

**And the rule is written once.** The availability condition existed **twice** — four hand-written clauses in `postTalk` (the counter) and four more in `availableTalk` (the fire). One edit away from a conversation that exists at one and not the other. Both callers ask `talkOpen` now.

**Why the guard that existed did not catch it.** `every conversation is reachable` opened with `journal: [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7]` — **it handed itself the pages.** It proved the chain works *given* them and never once asked whether they arrive. It now starts with **nothing**, may only collect what a reply actually hands over, and is given **no event pages at all** — because the four events that grant one are the only supply from outside the chain, and a story that needs them is a story that depends on a map node whose entire description is *"No climbing. No telling."* The chain has to stand up alone, and it does.

Three assertions rather than one, and in this order: the **static invariant** first (whatever a gate asks for must be suppliable from outside its own chain), then the **walk**, then that the gate actually **bites** — a gate that is never checked satisfies every reachability test ever written.

**Off band, and measured rather than assumed:** **44.3%** at `PAGES=14`, n=3000. Unchanged, and for a stateable reason — the newly-reachable replies pay in pages (a no-op at a full journal) and in one rare card the harness's reply policy declines (it scores a page at 3 and a card rarity at 0); the psyche gains sit in `marge7`/`marge8`, which need `seen` carried between trips, and the band harness measures one run from scratch.

**The carve-out, stated rather than buried: the `known` ending is still 0.0% on this policy.** It wants ten findable pages and the climber reaches six. Event-biased careers get **51.1%**, so it is not unreachable — it is gated behind knowing that the unlabelled node is where his pages are. That is `NARR-18`, and this ticket did not fix it.

**Process, and it was mine.** I hand-patched `src/engine.ts` to read a failure message **while the mutation harness was still mutating that same file**, then diagnosed the resulting nonsense as a real defect for several minutes: the backup I restored from had one of the harness's own injections baked into it. Nothing was lost — the harness restores from its own snapshot — but two injection verdicts had to be thrown away and re-run. **The harness owns the tree while it runs.** And the two labels really were wrong once measured cleanly: I had them **swapped**, because I had guessed which assertion would fire instead of checking.

(177 core + 106 balance; **7 injections kept and negative-tested**, one of them added because fixing the labels revealed that nothing was exercising the static invariant.)

### v10.38 — NARR-16: eight of his fifteen pages were worth nothing on the wall (2026-08-16)
**The eighth audit, and the first on story rather than systems. It found two defects; this is the second one, shipped first, because it is the one that is purely arithmetic.**

**The audit is worth stating before the fix, because the fix is small and the finding is not.** I built a `career` mode for the harness — the existing one measures a RUN, and a story is not a run-scale thing. It carries `seen` and `journal` across expeditions the way `carryOver` now does (SAVE-7, one version ago) and reports which conversations a player actually reaches. Two hundred careers, ten expeditions each, default policy:

```
trip   completed%   talks seen (cum)   journal pages (cum)
   1       33.0%              5.6/20              1.9/15
   3       39.0%             13.8/20              4.4/15
   6       29.0%             15.0/20              4.7/15
  10       37.0%             15.0/20              4.8/15
```

**It plateaus at trip six and never moves again.** `marge4` through `marge8` fire in **0.0% of careers**, the `known` epilogue in **0.0% of 673 wins**. Marge's thread — the one the source calls the spine — stops dead after *"I looked for two seasons… You know what I found? Good bouldering. That is all."* That is `NARR-17`, and it is next.

**This ticket is the other one.** The finale's blindness penalty read:

```ts
const unread = spec.finale ? Math.max(0, JOURNAL.length - 1 - s.journal.filter(p => p <= 6).length) : 0
```

One expression in which **both halves meant six** — six findable pages, six Grip of blindness, read them all and the route is legible. `NARR-11` grew the journal to fifteen and pulled the halves apart: the first became **fourteen**, the second stayed capped at six. Measured mean hold grip on the finale:

| journal | before | after |
|---|---|---|
| nothing | 24.37 | **24.37** |
| pages 1–6 | 18.37 | 21.37 |
| **the eight pages NARR-11 added, alone** | **24.37** | 21.37 |
| all fourteen findable | 18.37 | **18.37** |

**Holding all eight of the new pages measured exactly the same as holding none of them.** They have `BETA_CARDS` entries and they count toward the ending; they simply could not be read on the wall. And the map card said `6/6 of his pages — you can read the whole thing` at the precise moment eight Grip a hold of blindness remained, which is a **false sentence on screen**.

**Two constants instead of one, and the band does not move.** `BLIND_HOLD = 8` names the flat cost NARR-11 added by accident — **kept, not reverted**, because eleven versions of balance have been pinned on top of it and taking it back out is a far bigger change than leaving it in. `UNREAD_MAX = 6` is NARR-7's range, and it now runs off **every findable page**. Both measured endpoints are untouched by construction, which is what makes this shippable without a re-pin.

**A ceiling rather than a round, and the reason is not arithmetic.** Six Grip over fourteen pages is about two pages a step whichever way it rounds, so two steps are always flat — the only choice is *where*. Rounding puts them at the start, so the first page you ever find changes nothing. The ceiling puts them at the end, where the last pages are already paying you in the ending and the every-page deed. **The first page he wrote should be worth something on the wall. The fourteenth does not need to be.**

**Why no guard caught it in eleven versions.** The balance suite's `the journal makes the finale readable` compares blind against read — a **relative** claim, and a uniform bug is invisible to a relative test. Worse, its "read" state was `gripAt(6)`, which *was* the whole journal when it was written and is six of fourteen now; it was testing a half-read journal and calling it read. That is fixed here rather than left. The new guard asserts the **invariant** instead of the numbers: what the wall charges may depend on HOW MANY of his pages you hold and must not depend on WHICH — checked by measuring **two disjoint halves of the same journal** and requiring them equal.

The core guard covers the shape that allowed it: there must be **one** function that says how blind you are, and both the wall and the screen must call it. Two hand-rolled counts of the same thing in two files is what NARR-11 walked into.

(176 core + 106 balance; **6 injections kept and negative-tested**.)

**Measured.** `PAGES=14 SHARP_AT=99` at n=3000: **44.3%** against the ~44.3 pin — the same number as the baseline, digit for digit, which is what "band-neutral by construction" is supposed to look like. Three bands at n=1500: **33.9 / 43.3 / 45.1**.

**Process, twice.** One of the six injections **survived**, and it was my assertion at fault rather than the code: `unreadGrip([...all, 7])` is 0 whether or not the summit page is counted, because `Math.min(held, FINDABLE)` clamps fifteen back to fourteen. A guard has to be asked where the count is *not* clamped — holding nothing but the summit page, which is a real state and the exact one the mistake would reward. That is the **vacuous-assertion** class again, in its subtlest form yet: not a repeated fragment, but a clamp downstream of the thing under test.

And a **killed mutation run left a ROUTE-15 injection applied to `src/engine.ts`** — `restore()` handles SIGINT and uncaught exceptions, not SIGKILL. The ROUTE-15 guard and GUARD-9 both caught it within a minute of the next run, which is the system working; but the lesson is that after any interrupted `mutants.mjs` the sources are suspect until proven otherwise, and the proof is a diff against the last commit rather than a passing suite.

### v10.37 — SAVE-7: the journal never survived a second expedition (2026-08-16)
**NARR-9 was meant to be closed as stale. Auditing it found a bug that had been live since `carryOver` was written.**

**The ticket first, because it is the reason anybody looked.** NARR-9 asked for *"fifteen to twenty pages, gated by act"* on the premise that *"you will read most of them in one run"*. Both halves were wrong:

- **The count was already done** — `NARR-11: fifteen pages` shipped at **v9.32**, and the row says so in its own title while keeping the stale sentence underneath.
- **You do not read most of them in one run.** Measured: **2.0 pages** on the default policy, **2.6** taking every event node, out of fourteen findable. The proposed fix — gating by act — would have slowed a drip that is already slow.

**And then the reason it feels like the journal does nothing.** The pages are saved and loaded as **account** state, the "known" ending needs **ten** of them, and the `everypage` deed asks for **all fourteen** — none of which is reachable at two a run unless they accumulate. They did not:

```
carryOver() — "Everything that belongs to you rather than to a run.
               A new expedition must carry all of it;
               hand-copying a subset is how it got lost."
```

**It was hand-copying a subset, and `journal` was not in it.** Starting an expedition reset it to `[]`, and the next autosave — which happens at every safe point — wrote the empty array to disk. Verified end to end: save five pages, start a trip, load, and they are gone. **The game's only meta-progression (NARR-1's words) never survived a second expedition**, and the ending and the deed that depend on it were unreachable in normal play for as long as the function has existed.

**A second field was missing too, found by diffing rather than by looking.** `dailyTried` is SAVE-4's anti-farm gate — *you have had your go today even if you bailed*. `dailyDay` was carried and this was not, so: start the daily, come off it, start an expedition, and `dailyUsed` reads false again.

**The fix is the diff, not the two fields.** Anyone can add two lines; the thing that lost them was a hand-written list. The guard now compares **what `saveGame` writes as account state** against **what `carryOver` hands a new run**, with five named exemptions and a stated reason for each (`arch`, `style`, `mutators`, `loadout` are arguments to `newRun`; `challenge` is a mode flag `modeReset` clears by design) — and it checks those exemptions stay true, so a field cannot stay exempt on the grounds that `newRun` takes it after `newRun` stops taking it. **The next field added to the save fails the suite instead of going quiet for a year.**

**Off-band by construction and verified:** the balance harness calls `newRun` directly and never touches `carryOver`, and it sets the journal itself for each band. Measured 45.1% against the ~44.3 pin. Real players now accumulate pages as designed, which is the point.

One assertion of my own came out for being vacuous: `ok(dailyUsed(...))` is true by construction once `dailyTried` is carried, so it could never be the assertion that fires. That is the shape SKIRM-8 cut two of, and the consequence it was there to state is written in prose instead.

(175 core + 116 balance; **4 injections kept and negative-tested**.)

### v10.36 — A11Y-10 and BAL-17: the two decisions, decided (2026-08-16)
Both were logged as *"a decision, not a defect"*. Both are now decided, dated, and written where the thing they govern lives.

**A11Y-10 — and the answer was neither of the two options.** Three screens carried `role="status"` on a block present when the screen **mounts**; seventeen carried nothing. A11Y-9's rule — a live region is for what appears while you are already on a screen — made those three look like noise to delete. **Deleting them would have made the app worse**, for a reason that only turns up when you go looking:

> the only `.focus()` in `App.tsx` was the bottom-sheet one.

**Nothing moved focus when the phase changed.** This is a single-page app, so swapping a whole screen is not a navigation: a screen reader announces **nothing**, and the user is left with focus where the button they pressed used to be. Those three were not noise — they were the only announcement three screens had, and the other seventeen were mute.

So neither keep nor delete: **fix the gap they were papering over.** Focus now moves to the screen's own heading on every phase change, which announces the screen *and* puts a keyboard user at the top of it. Every title already carries `role="heading" aria-level={1}` — A11Y-8 asserts there are at least twenty and that all of them do — so it needed **no per-screen markup**, and it made the three redundant, which is why they came out in the same change rather than being left to rot. Verified in a browser: focus was on `BODY` before and lands on each screen's `h1` after.

**BAL-17 — the floor stays at 5, and here is why rather than inertia.** BAL-16 left the note that the floor was set when the roster spanned **3.3 to 29.8** and now lives between 6 and 9. Re-measured at n=2000 for the decision:

| | | | | |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Boulderer 8.7 | Onsighter 7.7 | Alpinist 7.3 | Comp Kid 7.0 | **Trad Dad 6.6** |

Spread **1.32×** against a 2.2× ceiling — and the lowest is the **Trad Dad** now, not the Comp Kid, which BAL-16 bought back. The fine pass sorts, so it followed without being told.

- **6.6 clears 5 by 2.9 SE** at n=2000 — a measurement this project can afford, and exactly what BAL-16's guard already checks.
- **Raising it to 6 would cut that margin to 1.1 SE** — unresolvable at any affordable sample, which is precisely the state BAL-16 spent a version escaping.
- **A relative floor** (60% of the best is 5.2 today, nearly the same number) would move with the roster, so the one failure an absolute floor exists to catch — every climber sinking together — would stop firing.

Revisit if the roster's floor drops under **~6.2**, where the 2 SE margin runs out. The reasoning sits next to the assertion, not in a changelog, so the next person to look at the floor reads it there.

**Process, again.** A Python edit script aborted on its third anchor and therefore wrote **nothing** — so the focus effect silently never landed, and only the guard written for it caught that. That is the "a failed anchor means nothing landed" trap, and the guard existing before the code is what made it a five-minute problem. `tsc` then caught a **temporal dead zone** — the effect read `booted` above its declaration — which is SAVE-6's shape and the **third** time this session that `tsc` has caught one.

(174 core + 116 balance; **4 injections kept and negative-tested**.)

### v10.35 — SKIRM-9 and PERF-2: the last defect, and the question closed (2026-08-16)
Two recommendations, done together because both were small and neither needed a decision from Evan.

**SKIRM-9 — the circuit ending was wearing trip furniture.** The run-end subtitle was unconditional:

```
{ACT_NAMES[st.act]} · stage {st.tier + 1} · deck {st.runDeck.length}
```

A circuit is not a trip. It has no acts and no stages, so CIRCUIT OVER read *"Act 1 · the forest · stage 1"* — whichever act and stage a run happened to be left on. Exactly the class SKIRM-8 caught twice on the walk-off (a route header naming a route you were never on; *"Burns used 3/3. Got 6 of 5"*). It reads **"the circuit · the grind · deck 15"** now, worded identically to the walk-off screen so the mode's two endings match, and the deck stays on both because it is the one number that means the same thing either way. Found while shipping NARR-15, logged rather than bundled, verified in a browser.

**PERF-2 — "the 190KB question", and the row's premise was stale in both numbers.**

| | raw | gzip |
|---|---|---|
| react + react-dom alone | 190 KB | 59 KB |
| the whole build | 497 KB | 157 KB |
| **so the game itself** | **307 KB** | **~98 KB** |

React is **38%** of it, not *"most of the 333KB bundle"* — and the game, not React, is what grew since that was written. Bytes were never the question anyway: what a big bundle costs on a slow CPU is parse, compile and first render. Measured under Chromium CPU throttling (4× ≈ mid-tier phone, 6× ≈ low end), cold context each time:

| throttle | interactive | first paint | turn repaint |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1× | 115 ms | — | 20 ms |
| **4×** | **378 ms** | 592 ms | **50 ms** |
| 6× | 605 ms | 832 ms | 110 ms |

**A mid-tier phone has the game up in about a third of a second and repaints a turn in 50 ms.** React's share of that boot is bounded by its share of the script — call it **90 ms** — and buying it back means hand-rolling a renderer underneath four thousand lines of `App.tsx`. **The answer is that it does not matter**, and the question is closed rather than left open for somebody to re-ask from the same guess.
