The bias is a **trade inside an opposed pair** (grease↔dry, crumble↔gust) so the four always sum to a fixed 100 and the roll still draws exactly one number — **stream-neutral**, no guard moves on drift. The band was the whole risk (moves alter grip/bite live, and the act climates aren't evenly wet/dry): tuned to net flat and **measured at 46.7% → 46.7% at n=300 and 48.4% → 48.2% at n=500** — a fraction of a point, deliberate. Guarded: every weather×rock combination still totals 100, a seeping day leans toward greasing, a crisp day toward drying, sandstone flakes more than granite, and wet and crisp produce genuinely different weightings — so the condition can't quietly become decorative.

### v9.67 — A11Y-7: the text-size setting finally reaches the story (2026-08-10)
The second ship from the second audit, and the closing of a real WCAG-resize gap. Every class-based size in the CSS scaled with the `--fs` text-size setting — but the nine multi-line **narrative** blocks (event text and its result, campfire dialogue and your reply, the FA claim, the four run/session/burn/ending recaps) were hardcoded `fontSize: 13` inline, so a low-vision player on **TEXT LARGER** still got the densest reading in the game at the *smallest* size. All nine now read `calc(13px * var(--fs))`, exactly like the surrounding chrome. The quiet part: a guard named **"every CSS font size scales with --fs"** was supposed to catch this and had passed clean for the whole life of the setting — because it sliced `engine.ts` for a `const CSS` template that lives in `App.tsx`, matched an empty string, and asserted zero-of-zero. It now reads `App.tsx`, checks the real CSS template, **and** asserts no bare `fontSize: 13` survives in the JSX — so the prose can't silently fall off the setting again. **UI-only, off the guarded band**; the slow balance guardrails were run as insurance and the completion band held.

### v9.66 — META-9: deeds earned late, and a new app icon (2026-08-10)
The first ship from the second audit, plus a fresh icon. **META-9** adds a **mastery tier** to the deeds. The original eleven are all first-contact markers — one full campaign clears nearly the list — so a returning player had no deed-shaped goals and `META-6` had only early deeds to gate on. Five more, read off the record the game already keeps and earned over many trips: **Seasoned** (top the Lost Line five times), **The Whole Quiver** (win as each of the five climbers), **The Hard Way** (win with a mutator on), **Left No Trace** (top the finale and put the stone back — the honest ending), **Every Last Page** (find all fifteen journal pages, not just the ten). Like every deed (INJ-1) they **pay nothing and gate nothing**, so they cannot become a grind — a map of the game's depth for a player who has already seen its breadth. The one prerequisite was a `mutators` field on `RunRecord` so "won with a mutator" can be read back; `recordRun` stamps it now. **Off the guarded band entirely** — deeds are pure predicates over persistent state, no RNG. Guarded: the mastery deeds are false on a fresh save, only a genuine record earns them (a mutated loss or a clean win does not earn The Hard Way, four climbers do not earn the Quiver), and the whole set is still pure and pays nothing.

**The app icon.** The installed-app icon was a small "SB" over a faint topo line and a red ×, busy and low-contrast under a launcher's mask. Replaced with the game's own motif drawn bold: an ascending **topo route** in ink with worked-hold dots and the **red crux star** at the top, on journal paper, filling the frame with no tiny text and sitting inside the maskable safe zone. Rendered to the 192 and 512 PWA icons the manifest already points at. 111 in-file + slow guardrails unmoved.

### v9.65 — ROUTE-10: the crux gets a character (2026-08-09)
The crux — the defining hard move — was the same `bite-4 / grip-8` "Committing" hold on all thirty-five routes, a V2 forest crux reading identically to the V10 finale. Each one now reads as its **style's own feature**: a crimp-ladder crux is **the razor**, a slab crux **the blank**, compression **the squeeze**, power **the throw**. On the board, in the read-ahead, in the coach's line, the crux is the line's signature move, not a generic "crux."

Where this stopped short — honestly — is the resolution. The audit wanted a crimp crux and a compression crux to not *resolve* identically, and the natural lever was the crux's Bite. But the crux's difficulty is load-bearing: its Grip is the superlinear stat the whole campaign is tuned around, and a bite lean, measured, moves the pinned band hard — a −1 Bite on a common style is worth **+4 to +5 points** of completion (the value-sim gains far more from a softer crux than it loses to a sharper one, and the style mix is not symmetric, so no combination of leans nets to zero). So the crux keeps its Committing resolution and its exact stats, and the character is its identity — which holds the band at **46.7%**, unmoved. A `dBite` lever is left in the data at 0, with the measurement recorded beside it, if the band is ever deliberately re-pinned. Guarded: every style names its crux, no style leans the crux's Bite or Grip, and two styles' cruxes read differently but resolve identically (same grip, same bite, still Committing). 106 slow guardrails.

### v9.64 — CARD-10: boons that change a rule (2026-08-09)
The type is documented *"Gear gives you numbers. A boon changes a rule"*, but the genuinely run-reshaping ones — the **wild** boons, a big gain bought with a rule you live with — numbered only four. Two more now. **Redpoint**: +2 Power on every move, but there is no shaking out — rests do nothing. **Static**: +2 Contact on every move so nothing spits you off, but every fall costs an extra skin. Each is a real trade, and each reaches a different lever (raw Power vs. the Contact threshold), so a run can now be reshaped around holding on or around going for it.

The tuning is the story. Boons are the one pickup the sim grabs first, indiscriminately, so a new boon with a cost the harness *feels* drags the whole band — a first cut priced **Redpoint** at a lost burn and **Static** at a heavy fall penalty, and completion fell to **39–43%**, because the harness falls on more than half its boulders and pays every fall-and-burn cost in full. The wild boons that already shipped survive precisely because their costs (no rests, dump-hand, a dyno's halved Contact) barely touch a value-sim. So these were priced the same way: Redpoint's cost is `noRests` (nearly free to the sim, real to a player who wanted to shake out), and Static keeps a single fall-skin. Retuned, completion sits at **46.7%**, on the ~46 line RUN-10 set — the two new boons net to neutral in the sim's hands while still being real choices in yours. Guarded: both are wild, both trade a recognized gain for a recognized cost, and holding no boons still changes nothing. 105 slow guardrails.

### v9.63 — A11Y-6: settings assistive tech can use (2026-08-09)
The settings had a strong accessibility story that a screen reader could not hear. Every toggle was a plain button with "ON/OFF" baked into the label — no switch semantics — so a screen-reader user heard *"SPOTTER ON, button"* with no announced state and no sense that tapping changed anything. The on/off toggles are **real switches** now (`role="switch"` + `aria-checked`), with a clean spoken name each, and the cyclers (text size, grades, one-handed reach) announce their current value. MOTION's fill was **inverted** — it darkened when motion was *off* while every neighbouring toggle darkened when *on*, so the same look meant opposite things side by side; it fills when ON now, like the rest.

And the toggles a new or low-vision player needs first — **text size, colour-safe, motion, reach** — sat dead last, under eight archive menus in one long scroll. Settings **lead** the screen now; the books (Collection, Logbook, Journal, and the rest) follow. UI only, no engine or balance impact. Guarded by a test that at least six toggles expose switch semantics, that MOTION fills when ON and the inverted styling is gone, and that settings sit above the archives. 104 slow guardrails unmoved.

### v9.62 — RUN-10: the stages you see every run stop repeating (2026-08-09)
Two déjà-vus, each in every campaign. The **two project nodes** in an act pointed at the **same boulder** — you worked The Sandbag, then a few stages later worked The Sandbag again — and the once-per-act **first ascent** was always the same flat *"An unclimbed line"* with one fixed note. Now each act's second project is its own line: **The Second Guess**, **The Rotisserie**, **The Hanging Slab** — a stat-for-stat sibling of the act's other project (same grade, length, cruxes, style, feet), a different climb at the same difficulty. And a first ascent has a **shape** now (an unclimbed *prow / wall / slab / roof / line*, by its style) and an act-specific note (green seams in the forest, varnish and silence in the desert, rime and cloud in the alpine) — still unnamed, because you name it after; that was always the point.

Honest about the balance: this is the one audit item that moved the number, and on purpose. Completion goes **~48% → ~46%**, because the two projects being the *same* boulder quietly handed the second one the first's **beta** — a discount that only existed by accident. Distinct projects remove it, so the second project is honestly a little harder. It is deliberate and dated in the drift guard's ledger, and well inside the defended band. The first-ascent change moves nothing: every difficulty stat is untouched and it builds off an isolated RNG, so only its name and note differ. Guarded: no act points two projects at one boulder, the two are identical in difficulty and different in name, and a first ascent stays unnamed with a shape and a note. 104 slow guardrails.

### v9.61 — SKIRM-4: the Circuit becomes a real mode (2026-08-09)
Endless mode was a bare treadmill — pull on, send, pull on again, with a card every third line and no sense of where you were — while the daily got the full SKIRM-3 / SOCIAL-1 treatment. It has a **shape** now. The Circuit climbs through **named zones** as the line count rises — the Warm-Up, Getting Steep, the Grind, the Business, Into the Dark — and the between-lines screen shows which zone you are in, calls the moment you cross into a new one, names the next zone one line up, and marks when you have passed your own best (from there, every line is new ground). The end screen gets a **real summary** in place of the campaign trip readout: lines sent, how deep you got, the hardest line you worked, roped pitches led, and your best.

It is pure framing: `circuitZone(n)` is a function of the line count with **no new state and no difficulty change** — the grade curve and the Alpinist's `enduro` deed (8 lines, which now lands squarely in the Grind) are exactly as they were, and the Circuit sits off the guarded campaign band regardless. Guarded: the zones only ever deepen with the count, there are at least four of them, the Warm-Up does not last forever, and the endurance deed sits in a real zone past it. Mid-circuit events, camps and a shop are a larger change (a new phase flow) and stay open. 104 slow guardrails, unmoved.

### v9.60 — NARR-12: the climber shows up in the story (2026-08-09)
Archetype changed every number and not a single word — the whole cast at the fire spoke to a generic "you" no matter which climber you brought, even where the fiction begs otherwise (Nita: *"I climb harder than he did. On plastic."*). Now each specialist climber has their own lines. Talk replies can carry an `arch` tag, shown only to that climber: the **Comp Kid** answers Nita's plastic boast with kinship and gets read by Dale as a gym rat who can actually climb; the **Trad Dad** talks ethics and racks with Dale and Nita; the **Alpinist** trades weather and suffering with Marge and Ranger Ellis; the **Onsighter** gets clocked for first-try hubris on the FA's rock. Everyone still sees the base replies — the specialist lines are extra.

The design keeps it **off the balance entirely**: an `arch` reply carries **no `outcome`** — it changes the conversation, never the reward — and the **Boulderer**, the default climber and the one the sim plays, gets no gated line, so the guarded campaign band and the climber-spread never see one. `repliesFor(talk, s)` is the single filter the screen and the harness both use (SIM-5), so a climber is only ever offered a line they can actually give. Guarded: every specialist climber has at least one line, the boulderer none, no gated line carries an outcome, and the filter hides other climbers' lines and never leaks outside a run. 102 slow guardrails, unmoved.

### v9.59 — UX-17: the beginner's climb screen (2026-08-09)
Two first-timer fixes on the one screen a new player spends all their time. The **tutorial step** used to render dead last — below the board, the pump meter, the hand, the COMMIT bar and up to six same-styled advisory boxes — so learning "tap a card, then a lane" meant scrolling past the whole game and picking the instruction out of a stack of look-alikes. It **leads** now: a pinned, ink-filled banner at the top of the climb, impossible to miss against a page of paper boxes, and the old buried box is suppressed on the tutorial route so the step never shows twice.

And the **marks key** is one tap from a climb. The fourteen hand-drawn ink glyphs (the holds) and seven family marks (the cards) are the whole "scannable without reading it" pitch, but they were only decodable from *How It Works*, two menus deep — unreachable mid-climb, and not even in front of a first-timer during the tutorial. A **"✦ what the marks mean"** line in the climb header now opens the same bottom-sheet the "what is left" pile uses, with the glyph key and the card marks right there. UI only — no engine or balance impact — guarded by a test that the banner leads and is not duplicated, that the key opens from the climb, and that the tutorial content it surfaces actually exists. 101 slow guardrails unmoved.

### v9.58 — ENG-23: flow you can feel (2026-08-09)
Flow was a stated pillar you couldn't feel: capped at 3, its whole payoff a single on/off switch at flow 2 (unanswered lanes stop costing tax), and only two cards ever read it — flow 1 versus flow 3 was invisible. It's a **track** now. It climbs to 5, shown as a number that ticks up as you keep sending (*"flow 4 ▸▸ dialed"*), with a second, **earned** breakpoint: at flow 4 you're *dialed in* and the **dyno lands far more often** (+15% to stick, on top of the +5% flow already gave). You reach it only by clearing a hold every turn without resting, so it can never be a turtle's reward, and it rewards a real flow-and-throw line.

What it deliberately does **not** do is touch the clock. The first cut gave the dialed tier a −1 flat-tick relief — and the sim measured that at **+7 to +13 points of completion**, because a per-turn pump relief compounds over a climb exactly the way per-turn pump *costs* do (the lesson, in reverse). So it was cut: the reward is the throw, not the pump, and a guard now asserts the end-of-turn clock is **identical at every flow level** for an identical board. Momentum cards keep their Power capped at the old ceiling too, so the raised cap feeds the track and the dyno, never the explosive stat. Completion lands at **48.0%** against the pinned 47; the preview-vs-resolve sweep (all 28 deck/route shapes) still passes, since both clock sites were changed in lockstep. 100 slow guardrails.

### v9.57 — ROUTE-9: each act gets its own weather (2026-08-09)
The three acts were climatically identical. `forecastFor` rolled weather and rock **uniformly and act-agnostic**, so the desert could come up freezing-drizzle and the alpine wall hot-sun — the forest/desert/alpine identity was a background PNG and nothing more. Each act climbs in its own climate now: a **mild forest** (crisp, still, a little seep — no baking sun, no freeze), a **hot dry desert** (hot sun and cold mornings on sandstone and basalt — never wet), and a **cold wet alpine** (freezing and drizzle on granite and gneiss — the weather act, for real). Every weather and rock is still reachable somewhere across the campaign; no single act is a one-note climate.

The safety of this rides on a detail: `forecastFor` derives from an **isolated per-node RNG** (a hash of seed/act/tier/node), never the shared run stream, so keying the palette to the act shifts **no** guard by stream drift — the climber-spread and ladder numbers don't move. Only the completion band moves, via difficulty, and it was tuned to stay put: the kinder forest offsets the harsher desert and the brutal alpine, landing full-journal completion at **47.7%** against the pinned 47. And because the alpine is now the hardest-weather act, the death curve steepens in order (act1 → act2 → act3), *reinforcing* the acts-get-deadlier guard rather than fighting it. Guarded: a campaign-wide coverage test (every weather appears somewhere) and a per-act identity test (hot sun is the desert's, the freeze is the alpine's, nobody drizzles in the desert, no act has fewer than three kinds of weather). Note this makes act 1 even gentler — that is `BAL-13`'s problem, still owned by `BAL-13` (fix via the boss or stage count, not conditions), not this. 100 slow guardrails.

### v9.56 — VIS-5: the weather channel gets its colour, and urgency (2026-08-09)
A fresh audit's cheapest and most-broken finding. The whole weather-window readout paints with `var(--blue)` — the menu forecast, the map "the window shuts" note, and the climb "THE WINDOW HAS SHUT / WEATHER · N HOLDS OFF" boxes — but `--blue` **was never defined**: not in `:root`, not in the colour-safe palette. It fell back to inherited ink, so the one channel telling you about the biggest send-rate lever in the game read the same colour as danger and flavour. Now it is a slate blue on the paper, and a **violet** in colour-safe mode (where green is already a teal-blue, so blue would have collided).

And the climb screen stacked up to six identical `.spot` advisories in source order, so a shut window or the route's next move carried no more weight than a phase flavour-note. They are ordered by **urgency** now — a hazard you must answer this turn (the route's next move, a shut window) sits at the top and takes an `urgent` weight (a heavier rule, a touch more ground); the plan, the phase note and the read-ahead follow. UI only — no engine or balance impact — guarded by a test that the colour is defined in both palettes and actually used. 99 slow guardrails unmoved.

### v9.55 — CARD-9: the extra-burn consumable (2026-08-09)
The last thing `CARD-7` carved out: a consumable that grants a **burn**, not a mid-climb bump. **Second Wind** — *"chalk up, shake out, tie back in. One more burn on this line."* You spend it at the fall, when you are out of burns, to pull on one more time.

The whole mechanic is **one new rule**: spending it lights `bonusBurns`, which `attemptsFor` reads, so the boulder's attempt cap goes up by one and the ordinary "out of burns?" check simply says no — the retry that follows is the same retry the game already runs, paying the same skin. It is per-boulder: `startBurn` clears `bonusBurns` on a fresh line, so a Second Wind buys a go on **this** route and never carries over. A burn consumable does nothing mid-climb, so `useKitStep` refuses it and the climb screen hides it — a mis-tap can't waste it.

The carve-out was that this can only be **judged by a harness that buys and spends it** — a shelf the drafter ignores tells you nothing about a burn. So the sim learned to (behind `KIT_BURN=1`) buy a Second Wind at every post and spend it at the last fall. Measured: full-journal completion goes **46.7% → 49.0%** — a real few-point leg-up, not a skip. The default drafter still never touches kit, which is why the pinned **47%** band is unmoved; the guard runs the campaign both ways and asserts the bought-and-spent wind lifts completion yet stays under the band ceiling. 96 slow guardrails.

### v9.54 — A11Y-5: one-handed reach (2026-08-09)
`A11Y-4` shipped haptics and an assist; the one-handed idea stayed open because reach wants a real phone to judge. The mechanism ships now regardless: a **ONE-HANDED REACH** setting — OFF · RIGHT THUMB · LEFT THUMB. On, the climb's primary controls (COMMIT, TAKE IT BACK, the kit) drop into a **sticky bottom bar** in thumb range and **mirror** onto the working thumb, with a bigger COMMIT target; off, it is the two-handed layout it always was.

Layout only — the engine never branches on it, proven by a guard that resolves an identical board with the setting off vs left and gets an identical board back. It is the player's, so it rides the save and carries across expeditions like the other settings. The exact sticky offset and target sizes are the part that still wants a device; the reachable layout itself is here and toggleable.

### v9.53 — META-6: climbers earned, not waited for (2026-08-08)
Unlocking a climber was a level threshold and nothing else — you waited it out. Now each of the four locked climbers has a **deed** that earns it, and the deed fits who they are: the **Comp Kid** by sending V5 (gym power), the **Trad Dad** by putting up a line (the trad ethic), the **Alpinist** by an eight-line Circuit (endurance), the **Onsighter** by a flash (a first-try send — the whole idea).

The level stays as a **backstop** — `archUnlocked` is `deed OR level` — so the deed is a faster, earned path rather than the only one, and a climber **can never become unreachable** however the deeds change. That is the guarded invariant: a test checks the starter is always in, that max level unlocks every climber, that each deed exists and earns its climber at level 1, and that below both the deed and the level a climber stays locked. The prepare screen names the deed now — *"Locked — send something V5 or harder, or reach level 4."*

Builds straight on `META-8`'s `deedsDone`; no engine or balance impact (the sim picks climbers directly and never gated on unlocks). 93 slow guardrails.

### v9.52 — SOCIAL-1: the share string (2026-08-08)
The daily finally had something worth sharing — the conditions everyone battled and a weekly ladder — so this is the string you paste, the way people post a Wordle grid. No server, no account: `dailyShare` builds a few lines off the attempt — the date, a row of squares for the holds you worked, the grade and the shared conditions, the score, the streak and the week:

```
Sandbagged · 2026-07-29
▪▪▪▪▪▪▪▪▪▫▫ 9/11 · V6 · hot sun
340 pts · flashed it
6-day streak · week 1240
```

A **SHARE RESULT** button on the daily result copies it to the clipboard — the same path the seed and save codes already use. It reads straight off the attempt, so two people who did the same thing write the same line, which is the point of a daily. Guarded: deterministic, one grid mark per hold filled for what you worked, "flashed it" only on a topout. No engine or balance impact.

### v9.51 — SKIRM-3: conditions of the day, and a weekly ladder (2026-08-08)
Two additions to the daily, both riding the seed it already turns on. **Conditions of the day**: the daily has always rolled its weather and rock off the date — that is how everyone lands on the same problem — but it never *said* what they were before you tied in. It does now, on the menu: *"hot sun on sandstone."* Conditions are the biggest lever in the game (`ENG-20`), so that is a real shared modifier — a hot-sun granite day plays nothing like a crisp gneiss one, and everyone gets the same one. `dailyForecast` reproduces `startDaily`'s derivation exactly, pinned by a test.

**The weekly ladder**: each day's score now stacks into a running weekly total (`weekId` / `weekScore` / `weekBest`), shown under the daily — *"This week: 1240 (best 1600)."* `bankDaily` accumulates it once a day and the week resets on a new seven-day block; it stays idempotent on the `!s.daily` guard so a second bank never double-counts, and it persists and carries across expeditions like the streak.

No rule-twist mutator, and that is deliberate: the mutator system is `inRun`-scoped and the daily is not a run, so forcing one would mean making the daily a full run — a larger change than this. The conditions *are* the shared modifier, which is what "conditions of the day" asks for. 93 slow guardrails.

### v9.50 — META-8: deeds (2026-08-08)
A tick-list of things worth doing, read straight off the record the game already keeps — the logbook (which stores the weather and whether you flashed), the lines you have put up and how you graded them, the pages you have found, the daily streak, the styles unlocked. Eleven to start: a first send, a flash, V5, a send **in the wet**, ten journal pages, the finale, your first FA, a **sandbagged** FA (the game's namesake), a three-day daily streak, an eight-line Circuit, and unlocking the Onsight style.

It doubles as a map of the game's breadth for a new player — conditions matter, you can put up and sandbag your own lines, there is a style past Redpoint. All eleven are **derived from existing persistent state**, so there is no new save field and nothing to migrate, and every predicate is pure and safe on an empty save.

A deed **pays nothing** — no XP, no card — the same rule `INJ-1` holds, because the moment a deed pays you it becomes a grind rather than a record; a guard asserts each deed carries only id/name/text/done. Display-only (a new DEEDS screen under THE BOOKS), so it touches no balance — slow guardrails unmoved. It is the surface `META-6` can gate a climber unlock on; it gates nothing yet. 93 slow guardrails.

### v9.49 — CARD-7: one-shot kit (2026-08-08)
Gear is a rule you carry for the whole run; there was no one-shot layer — a thing you buy and spend when it counts. Consumables are that. Three to start: **Chalk Shot** (shed 5 pump), **Beta Napkin** (draw 2), **Crux Pad** (+2 Power to every lane you have out, this turn). Bought at a trading post on their own line, carried in a kit capped at `KIT_MAX` (2), spent mid-climb, gone once used — and the post is once a stage, so they cannot be farmed.

They ride their own shop line and their own inventory, never displacing a card offer, and `useKitStep` is the one shared path that applies them (`SIM-5`) — a consumable is not a card and never enters a pile. The drafting harness values cards and does not touch the kit, so consumables are a **cash sink for the player, not a lever on the balance band** — which is why the guardrails do not move.

One thing worth recording: the first cut picked the shelf consumable with `rng.int` off the run RNG, which shifted every downstream roll and moved the climber and `ROUTE-8` guards by ten-plus points — **not a balance change, a perturbed deterministic stream**. The pick is derived from the stage instead now, zero draws, and the baselines came back exactly. The extra-burn idea (lighting the dormant gear `attempts` field) is carved out as `CARD-9`: it needs the burn/fall flow and harness support to measure honestly. 93 slow guardrails.

### v9.48 — ENG-22 + CARD-8: two audit cleanups (2026-08-08)
Both from the v9.42 audit, both about code that read as one thing and was another. No behaviour change — text, a comment, and two guards.

**ENG-22** — the **Cycle** card and its glossary entry both said *"draw when you place it"*, but the draw fires in `resolve`, once per turn the move is on the board (it re-draws while it holds on). Both now say what it does, and a guard fails if either says "place it" or the glossary stops describing when it draws — the same doc/behaviour drift `SIM-5` exists to catch, here in prose.

**CARD-8** — the `bargain` cause sat in `EARNED_CURSES` but was never passed to `addCurse`. `curseEarned` reads a fall for rawskin/exposed and `claimCurse` reads your grades for sprayed; **Sandbagged Beta is a choice you make in an event**, applied through the `curse` outcome, so the branch read as reachable and was not. Documented as the odd one out and pinned shut: `curseEarned` must never return bargain from any run-end state (or it would double-apply Sandbagged Beta with the events), and the card must stay reachable from an event. 89 slow guardrails.

### v9.47 — A11Y-4: haptics on their own switch, and an assist (2026-08-08)
Two accessibility gaps, both around things that already half-existed — the recurring shape of this audit. The **buzz** on commit / phase / fall / send was already wired but gated on the **SOUND** setting, so you could not have haptics without audio, or silence the vibration while keeping the sound. It rides its own **HAPTICS** toggle now.

And an **ASSIST** toggle for players who find the grip ranges hard to parse: every hold shows its exact Grip instead of a lo–hi span. It is **display only** — `gripShown` reads the number the way projecting or beta already would, and the grip underneath and everything `resolve` does with it are untouched — so it trades the guessing game for clarity without moving a single value. Uncertainty is a pillar, so the boundary is guarded: a turn resolves identically with assist on or off across 80 turns. Both settings persist and carry across expeditions, with the save guards extended to prove it; old saves default haptics on, assist off.

The one-handed-reach half of the item needs a real device to judge and is carved out as `A11Y-5`. 89 slow guardrails.

### v9.46 — ROUTE-8: choose your line, for real (2026-08-08)
The line-choice screen already existed (**WHICH WAY UP**, three options before a climb — another *check the disk* moment) but the choice was false. **"The direct" was −2 holds and +3 cruxes and measured the *easiest* of the three** — 53% against the guide's 47% and the traverse's 42% — because a shorter climb banks less pump than a handful of extra cruxes ever cost. Always taking the direct beat always-guide by six points of free completion, and the auto-picker duly always took it. It was measured universally best, across every climber, so it was not even a deck choice.

The lever choice was the whole story and is worth recording: `dTax` is enormous (±1 swung a line **~27 points** — the compounding trap this project has hit five times), `dGrip` is superlinear and nearly as violent, `dCrux` is almost free, and only **hold count** is a medium lever where shorter is always easier. There is no way to make a shorter line *equally hard*, so the lines trade **texture**, not difficulty. The direct keeps one fewer hold but four more cruxes — short and cruxy, a line a powerful deck eats and a weak one walls on. The traverse drops two cruxes at the same height — the safe way past the hard moves. All three now land within a point of the guide for a default deck (**46.7 / 46.0 / 46.3**, auto 46.0): none a free win, none a trap.

Guard added: the deck-optimal line may not beat always-guide by more than a hair, and no line may sink into a trap far below it — the check the original +6-point direct would have failed. 86 slow guardrails.

### v9.45 — META-7: The Onsighter (2026-08-08)
A fifth climber, built entirely out of engine hooks that had been *read* but never *set* by any archetype: `noBeta`, `ignoreWeather`, `firstTurnPower`, `dHand`, `dSkin`. She never rehearses — no beta ever banks, so every hold stays a guess and projecting buys her nothing — but nothing the weather does touches her, her hand runs a card deeper, she takes a move tougher, and she commits off the first one. Her starter deck **reads the line as it goes** (Sight the Line, from `RUN-9`) rather than working it. Unlocks at level 16, after the Alpinist.

`noBeta` is a blunt, heavy cost — projecting is a pillar — so it is paid back with raw ability, and deliberately **not** with more attempts: an onsight is one try. Balanced against the `BAL-12` floor, not by eye. She lands at **7.7% against a 5% floor**, right in the pack (Boulderer 7.8, Trad Dad 7.3, Comp Kid 5.8, Alpinist 5.7), spread **1.37×**. The path there is in the code comment: `dHand:2` was a cliff to 11.8%, `dSkin:2` non-monotonically worse, so the package settled at `dHand:1 + dContact:1 + dSkin:1 + firstTurnPower:2`.

Guards updated for a fifth climber rather than weakened: *"signature pays over a burn"* now recognises `noBeta` and `ignoreWeather` as genuinely over-a-burn (`firstTurnPower` alone still would not — it fires once); the climber-spread guard reads `ARCHETYPES.length` instead of a hardcoded four; the unlock-order guard points at the real last climber. Old four-climber saves fall back to default loadouts (`loadGame` already length-checks). 85 slow guardrails.

### v9.44 — RUN-9: read the sequence (2026-08-08)
Projecting is a pillar, but you could only ever read a hold's grip *range* — the **order** of what was coming off the route deck was hidden. `RUN-7` is a stage lookahead and `UX-14` peeks your own deck; the route's own order was the untouched gap.

A new card, **Sight the Line** (common, cost 0: read the next 2 holds, draw 1), reveals the top of the hold deck. State carries `readAhead` — how many upcoming hand-holds are known — set in `playBonusStep` and counted down in `refillAndDraw` as those holds come onto the board, so it always means *"the next this-many are known"*. The climb screen shows a **COMING UP** strip, nearest first, each as a grip range until you have been on it; the map flags a deck that can read ahead.

It is **information, not power**, and that is the whole of it — guarded four ways: a turn resolves identically whether `readAhead` is 0 or 5 (same holds worked, pump and board over 80 turns), the reveal never pops the deck, it counts down in range as holds arrive, and it goes through the one shared `playBonusStep` path (`SIM-5`), which now also protects `c.read` from being re-implemented in the screen or the harness. Deterministic from the seed, spends no randomness; campaign and climbers unmoved. 85 slow guardrails.

### v9.43 — ROUTE-7: boss signatures (2026-08-08)
The audit found the bosses reused a compact phase set — `allCrux/dBite/dTax/lockLane/noRest` — and only The Hourglass's locked limb was truly dramatic. This makes each boss read as one named thing, surfaces it on the map before you commit, and guards that the four threats actually differ.

**The alpine act is the weather act now.** The Lost Line's window (`ROUTE-6`) is the final boss's signature, and Summit Block's roof is reflavoured as **The Gale** — the wind funnelling over the lip. The map names the finale's window: *"the window shuts at 65% — be through the hard climbing before it does."* Across the four bosses the dramatic mechanics span a locked limb, a closing window, committing holds and a faster clock, which a guard now holds.

**Summit Block was not given a persistent window, and that is the measured result rather than an omission.** The obvious move was to make its gale a real `ROUTE-6` window — +1 Bite from the roof, telegraphed. It dropped the Comp Kid to **5.0 against a floor of 5**. The reason is exactly `BAL-13`: a phase's `dBite` ends when the next phase takes over, but a window reaches the top, so it adds Bite precisely where the act-3 critical path is tightest — and that charges the Comp Kid (one less burn) and the Alpinist (−2 Contact) twice. So the persistent window stays on the finale, which few runs reach and which the guard already clears, and Summit Block keeps its numbers with a weather reflavour instead. Campaign and climbers unmoved: Comp Kid **5.8**, spread **1.37×**, full journal ~46%. 82 slow guardrails.

### v9.42 — ROUTE-6: the weather window (2026-08-08)
`ENG-20` measured conditions as the largest single lever in the game — the same boulder sends 96% crisp and 50% in hot sun — and `VIS-3` put them on the paper. But they were fixed for a whole climb: a still morning stayed a still morning to the top. The one place a mountain is most itself — the weather turning while you are committed on it — the game never modelled.

It does now. A route can carry a **window** that turns the conditions at a fractional height, telegraphed a hold ahead the way a phase is, so it is a decision rather than an ambush. Like every condition in this game it may move **Bite and Support and never Power** — the absolute rule `ENG-20` wrote after a flat ±1 Power swung a route 69 points. Because the window feeds the two functions the preview already routes through — `biteAgainst` and the Support term in `powerAgainst` — the preview stays exact for free; there was no second copy of the maths to drift, which was the whole shape of `ENG-19`.

The finale carries it, because the journal has been circling it the whole game: the nine days of weather he waited for. It holds while you climb the lower wall and **shuts on the run to the headwall** (at 65%) — +1 Bite across the route and −1 Support, cloud over and nothing under your feet, on top of the crux sequence and the no-rest headwall that were already there. A blue **WEATHER · N HOLDS OFF** telegraph reads a hold before, then **THE WINDOW** in the log when it lands.

Sized by measurement against the band `BAL-14` nailed down: full-journal completion **47.7% → 46.7%**, inside the 40–58 band and barely moved, because the window only reaches the top third of one route — it is tension, not a difficulty knob. Act 3 stays the deadliest and the act order holds. Four guards: the window is telegraphed exactly one hold before it lands, it carries no Power field and leaves a move's Power untouched with no feet on, it sharpens Bite by the amount it states, and the preview matches resolve on every turn it is shut. The boundary guard earned its keep on the way — a comment reading "the weather window." tripped the check that the engine never says `window.`, which is the DOM. 81 slow guardrails pass.

Also logged from the same audit and queued below: `ENG-21`, `ROUTE-7`, `RUN-9`, `META-7`, `CARD-7`, `SKIRM-3`, `ROUTE-8`, `A11Y-4`, `META-8`, and two cleanups — `ENG-22` (`cycle`'s text and behaviour disagree) and `CARD-8` (the dormant `bargain` branch).

### v9.41 — EVT-4: events that remember (2026-07-27)
Thirty-two events, every one self-contained. You caused an access closure, you trundled a block onto the trail, you believed a man about a grade — and nothing ever referred back. The reason was one line: **`eventsSeen` recorded which event fired and never which branch you took**, so nothing could look back even if it wanted to.

A run remembers the branch now, as `id:index`, and an event can require one. Four of them exist only because of something you did.

**The Sign Is Bigger Now** — you parked down the road and slipped in, and the sign has been replaced with a larger one and a second board listing the dates the gate is locked, *which is all of them*. Say it is on you and it is *partly* on you; there were four other cars. **Where The Block Was** — you trundled it, and there is a party under the scar now saying it must have come off in the winter. Say nothing and you let them have the winter, which is not even a lie exactly; own it and the oldest one nods: *"Better on the ground than on somebody."* **Word Got Round** — somebody you have never met repeats your opinion about the bolt back to you slightly wrong, as though it were a thing you are known for. **Him Again** — the same man, the same table, the same certainty, about a different line, and no memory at all of telling you the last one was V2.

Three properties, all measured rather than assumed. **Zero callbacks in 2,000 rolls before their cause.** Reachable once earned — the access one turned up 149 times in 4,000. And **never once the range is exhausted**: the old roll allowed repeats when it ran out of fresh events, and meeting the same consequence twice reads as a bug rather than as a consequence, so callbacks are excluded from that fallback specifically. Every callback also has to point at a branch its cause actually offers, which is the kind of thing that rots the moment a choice is reworded.

The harness had to learn it too, or a callback could never fire for the sim — the same shape of gap `SIM-5` was about, caught this time because the roll needed a new argument and both call sites had to be found. 36 events, 226 checks, campaign unmoved at 47.7%.

### v9.40 — VIS-2: the silhouettes were already there (2026-07-27)
**The item was wrong and I wrote it.** `VIS-2` said *"holds are all the same rectangle"* and they are not — there is an eleven-shape glyph set covering **every hold type in the game**: jug, crimp, sharp crimp, sloper, pinch, pocket, crux, foothold, smear edge, chip, blank. It has been there a long time. **That is the fourth time in this session I have described this game from memory instead of reading it**, and the pattern is worth more than the fix.

The true version is much narrower: the silhouette was **22px in a corner**, so it read as decoration and the eye went to the words. Making it structural turned out to have no room.

**Behind the text at 16%** put small body copy over an ink wash, and I could not measure whether it survived — three attempts at a contrast metric all ended up measuring the wash rather than the words, including one that reported *more* separation at higher opacity, which is the giveaway. **The band above the pump line** turned out to be where `.pv`, the preview readout, already lives. **The corner at 30px** needed `padding-right` on the name to clear it, which pushed the longest names into more lines and towards the preview.

So what shipped is **22px to 26px and slightly darker**, which is the whole safe headroom on a 112px card that already carries a name, an ability, two lines of text, a preview line and two pips. Recorded because a small change after four abandoned attempts is more useful written down than quietly dropped.

The measurement worth keeping: comparing the three hold cards to each other, whole-card difference barely moved (24.5 to 25.0) because **the text was already doing the differentiating** — which is the real answer to the original question and the reason there was so little to win here. 224 checks.

### v9.39 — the grip pip clipped a two-digit span (2026-07-27)
Reported from a phone: a hold showing **12–13** wrapped onto two lines inside its pip and got cut off.

The pip was a **fixed 20px circle**, which was correct for every value it had ever held — a grip is one digit most of the time — and became wrong the moment `ENG-10` started showing an unworked hold as a *span* rather than a number. Five characters at bold 10.5px is about 37px, and 47px at the largest text size. Nothing measured it because nothing was measuring the pip; `UX-9` pinned the card and the preview slot, not the thing inside them.

The grip pip grows into a **pill** now, with a minimum of 20px so a single digit still reads as a circle, `nowrap` so it can never break a span across lines, and padding to grow into. The power diamond keeps its fixed 20px, because it is rotated 45° and a stretched diamond is a different shape rather than a bigger one.

Guarded by arithmetic rather than by looking: the widest possible row — the 20px diamond plus a five-character pill plus the gap — must fit the **93px content strip** `UX-9` established, at 1.3 text scale. It comes to about 67px. And a second check that no hold in the game can reach a **three**-digit grip, since the next span wider than five characters would need this doing again. 224 checks.

### v9.38 — VIS-3: the conditions are on the paper (2026-07-27)
`ENG-20` measured weather at **46 points of send rate** — the largest single lever in this game, larger than any card, boon or archetype — and it read as the word *"hot sun"* in a subtitle. It is on the page now.

**Crisp** gets a cool clarity off the top of the sheet. **Hot sun** a warm wash from above, as though the paper has been out in it. **Humid** takes damp blotches into the fibre, in three places that do not line up with anything. **Freezing** puts frost in all four corners. **Drizzle** runs rain across the whole page on a slant. And **still** gets nothing at all, which is the point of it — the baseline has to look like the baseline.

Three things it had to get right, all asserted rather than trusted. It only appears **while you are actually out on something** — a menu has no weather, and a run-end screen does. It sits **under the text and never over it**, on a fixed pseudo-element at `z-index:0` with the content lifted to 1, because a decoration that fights the thing it decorates is worse than no decoration. And **colour-blind mode keeps the texture while dropping the hue** — the frost, the blotches and the rain still read, they just stop being blue and orange.

Measured before shipping rather than after. Six tiles mocked at true size, and the number that mattered was not raw contrast — my first metric was measuring its own sampling, since the plain page scored identically to the washed ones — but **the gap between ink and paper against a plain page**: 68 to 70 against 69. Every overlay costs at most one point of separation, at the largest text size. 222 checks.

### v9.37 — UX-11: an account of the trip (2026-07-27)
`UX-10` rebuilt the screen you see after a climb and left the one you see after a **run** saying you won or died and nothing else — while the trail, the logbook, the lines you have put up, the pages you have found and the thing you are carrying were all being tracked and none of them shown.

It gives you the account now. **The hardest thing you have ever sent**, with its grade. What you sent on this trip, and how many things you have flashed. **How much of his journal you have** — nine of fifteen. **The last line you put up**, with the grade you chose to give it. What you are **carrying**, if anything, and how many more trips it has to run. And underneath, **HOW IT WENT**: the whole trail, every stage in the order you did them, which `UX-16` has been keeping since v9.22 and only the map has ever read.

It also turned up a bug that has been there a long time. The subtitle read the **literal string "Act 1"** whatever act you actually died in — so every run that ended on the alpine wall reported itself as having ended in the forest. It names the act now, and there is a test that fails on the literal string, because that is the kind of thing that reads fine until you notice it once and then cannot unsee it.

Measured at 390px across all three text sizes: the longest row, *hardest thing you have sent · Summit Block · V9*, comes to 293px of 358 at the largest. 220 checks.

### v9.36 — BAL-13: the wrong lever (2026-07-27)
Act 1 kills about **6% of runs across nine stages** against act 3's 32% — twenty minutes of campaign where nothing can go wrong, which makes every decision in it free. That is a real problem and this is not the fix for it.

`BAL-10` had left act 1 alone on purpose, because act 1 is where you learn. That is still true of its first half, so the attempt was to give the **back half** teeth: one extra hold on the V3s and above. It worked on the thing it aimed at — act 1 went to 10%, act 2 to 15%, act 3 to 32%, **a rising curve for the first time**.

And it broke something better. The **climber spread went from 1.4× to 2.9×** — 9.5 / 4.3 / 7.7 / 3.3% — and it was not random which two fell. **The Alpinist pays −2 Contact and the Comp Kid pays a burn a day**, so a longer route charges them for their signature a second time: more turns is more bite taken, and more holds is more burns needed. `BAL-12` had just spent five sweeps getting those four level, and **act 1's length turns out to be load-bearing for exactly the climbers that give something up.**

Narrowing it to the last three routes did not help either: same 2.9× spread, and act 1 straight back to 5%, so the change was doing nothing except costing the two climbers who could least afford it. Reverted in full — climbers **8.8 / 6.3 / 8.5 / 6.5%** and campaign **47.3%**, exactly the v9.35 numbers.

Worth recording how this was caught rather than shipped: the arch sim is **deterministic**, so running it twice gave byte-identical output and the 3.3% was demonstrably not variance. That is the check I should have run before believing the numbers in `BAL-14`, and it is the reason this reverted in one pass rather than three.

The guard that came out of it holds the **order** rather than the flatness — act 2 may never kill fewer than act 1, act 3 never fewer than act 2, and act 3 must stay above 15% — because the ordering is the part that must never invert, and the flatness needs a fix that is not route length. The item stays open at P1 with the lever named: the boss alone, or fewer stages. 218 checks.

### v9.35 — BAL-14: no single cause (2026-07-27)
Two versions ago I logged that all four climbers had drifted down, **moved a test floor from 5 to 4 to accommodate it, wrote "worth a deliberate look before it moves again", and then shipped three more items on top.** Lowering a guard instead of fixing a cause is the exact thing `BAL-9` exists to prevent, so this is the look.

First, the scale of it. Under the identical command that read **12.0 / 11.5 / 12.8 / 10.3%** at v9.24, it read **3.6 / 3.8 / 3.8 / 4.4%** — a genuine threefold drop, not a change of measurement. And the player-facing number had gone with it: the campaign was **53% at a full journal and had become 43.5%.**

**Six hypotheses, and not one of them was worth more than two points.** Shopping, since `BAL-5` took posts from 0.4 a run to 2.5 — turning it off moved three climbers up and the Alpinist *down*, averaging out. `ENG-20`'s conditions, worth one or two. `CARD-6`'s curses, worth nothing measurable. The stage `NARR-10` added to act 2, worth 0.8. The talk outcomes I bulk-edited in `NARR-11`, which turned out intact — 28 outcomes across 35 replies, all their skin and psyche and cash where it should be. And the journal itself, which helps.

**So there is no single cause.** It is six changes each worth a point or two, compounding — which is precisely the failure mode `BAL-9` documented, and the reason a guard that only watches one number cannot see it coming. Worth saying plainly: I went looking for a bug and there was not one. The game had simply got harder six times in a row, each time defensibly.

Corrected with one dial, **`RUN_SKIN` 8 to 9**, which recovers four of the ten points: campaign **47.3%** at a full journal, climbers **8.8 / 6.3 / 8.5 / 6.5%** at n=600. The floor is back at 5 where it belongs.

Two guards changed. The campaign band is now **nailed down with a date and a number somebody chose** — *"set at 47 in v9.35"* — so the next drift is measured against an intention rather than against whatever last happened. And the climber guard runs 600 instead of 300, because a 5% event at n=300 has a standard error of 1.3 points and it was failing on variance, which is the same fix the ladder guard needed at v9.25. 216 checks.

### v9.34 — VIS-4: the map reads like a guidebook (2026-07-27)
`RUN-8` put the act on a drawn page and it did not look like one. Three faults, all visible the moment it was on a phone.

**The path was a scribble.** It swung by 0.28 of the width on a sine of the *stage index*, which at nine stages is a **±99px zigzag changing direction four times** — that is oscillation, not a line up a hill. The sine now runs across the whole act rather than per stage, at a sixth of the width: **104px of drift, changing direction once.** There is a test for both numbers, because "looks like a scribble" is measurable if you count direction changes.

**The contours were ruled stripes.** Full-width lines at even spacing read as notebook paper. They are arcs now that **bulge uphill and narrow toward the wall**, four to six of them depending on the act, which is what a contour actually does when the ground rises toward something.

**There was no wall.** The most important feature on the page was a word. It is drawn the way a guidebook draws one: a line across the top with **hachures on the uphill side**, which is the standard symbol and reads instantly to anybody who has used a topo. **FOREST**, **DESERT** or **ALPINE** above it, THE ROAD below.

And the stages ahead were 3px hollow circles nobody could see. They are **numbered** now, the next one is a dashed amber leg, and the rest of the act is a faint dotted line so you can see the shape of what is coming without it competing with where you have been.

All of it went in the engine — contours, cliff, hachures and path — because it is geometry, and the tests check every piece stays on the page, contains no `NaN`, and that the hachures do not point off the top. Mocked at 390px before any of it was written, which is how the scribble got caught in one pass instead of three. 214 checks.

### v9.33 — INJ-1: something you carry (2026-07-27)
A run was twenty-six minutes and then nothing carried but XP and cards, so a bad decision had no memory. This gives one a memory, and the **design values set hard limits on how** — they say skin, injury and psyche model *"care, risk and consequence — not grind-through-pain optimization"*, and that *"nothing valorizes training through injury"*.

So this is deliberately **not a system**. Climb a whole trip down to nothing — skin at zero — and you pick up a **tweak**: a pulley in the ring finger and crimps let you know about it; something in the elbow and anything you press down on aches; the shoulder, *fine until it is above your head*; tips that have not come back and anything you squeeze is raw. One named hold type costs +1 Grip for two trips, and then it is gone.

**There is nothing you can do about it.** No camp clears it, no shop sells the cure, no gear helps, and there is no way to speed it up — the menu says so in as many words: *"Nothing to be done about it — no camp, no shop, no gear. It goes when it goes."* They do not stack, so there is no spiral. And **it pays you absolutely nothing**: no XP, no bonus, no reward for climbing with one. That last part is the entire design, because the moment an injury pays you anything, carrying one becomes a strategy.

The tests assert the **values** rather than the mechanics, which is the right level for this. A tweak must cost on its own hold and nowhere else. **The tweak block may not contain the words `xp`, `cash`, `dDraw`, `powerAll`, `dPower` or `reward` anywhere in its code** — so a future edit that pays you for being hurt fails the suite. Only a trip passing may move it, and the screen may not clear one. **Topping out never earns you one**; only climbing a trip down to nothing does. And each one must name a real hold type, explain itself in plain words, and **not read like advice to push through it** — there is a regex for *train*, *push through*, and *tough it out*.

That last check found its own bug: the first version failed because the code block *says* "no camp, no cash, no gear" in a comment, and a test that reads prose instead of code fails on its own documentation. Comments are stripped now. 206 checks.

### v9.32 — NARR-11: fifteen pages (2026-07-27)
`NARR-8` made the journal decide which of three endings you get, on **seven pages** — so never knowing and knowing were four pieces of paper apart. Fifteen now, inserted in **narrative order with fresh ids**, because the table is the reading order and the id is the stable key events address: *The survey photo* (nine-tenths cloud, and he knows he has stopped looking at it and started looking at what he decided it is), *On the rock*, *The walk in* (eleven times and it never once got shorter; what changed is he stopped resenting it), *On Marge* (she let him lie, and knew, and let him anyway), *On being frightened* (**"I am not brave. I want that written down somewhere by me and not by somebody else afterwards"**), *The traverse*, *The day it rained*, and *On the name*.

Three things had to be fixed for fifteen pages to mean anything.

**Only six pages were findable.** Each grant named a specific page, so the eight without their own event branch were unreachable by construction. A grant can ask for **the next one he wrote** now and a pool does the rest. Eleven of the twenty conversations hand one over — Marge has his van and his things, and hers is the thread that runs the length of the game. A run found **0.4 pages**; it finds **1.7**, so the journal reads over about eight expeditions.

**The harness could not grant a page above 6**, because it hardcoded `[1,2,3,4,5,6]`. Which means every journal measurement ever quoted was of the first six pages only.

**And fourteen pages measured no better than seven** — 41.2% against 40.4% — because every beta card past the ones you need is a card you draw instead of a move. That is `DECK-1`'s finding from the other end. **The beta you carry up the wall is capped at eight**, and the curve is monotonic again: **32.8 / 42.5 / 43.5%.** The pages are the story and the ending; the beta is what you can carry.

The ending thresholds are proportions now rather than counts — 30% and 70% of what is findable, so *stranger* / *partial* / *known* sit at 5 and 10 pages and cannot drift away from the journal again.

Tests: every findable page must map to a beta card that exists and is a beta card, the page you find at the top must not be one, no two pages share an id, every entry must be long enough to be an entry, the pool must hand out each page once and then stop, and — in the slow suite — **the journal must be findable at better than 1.2 pages a run and worth more than five points, with more pages never worse than fewer.** 201 checks.

### v9.31 — NARR-10: no act is the thin one (2026-07-27)
**The item was half wrong and I wrote it.** Act 2 was thin on climbs — 8 against 11 and 12 — but the four conversations were **act 3's**, not act 2's. Acts are zero-indexed and I read `act: 2` as the second one. Act 2 had six people to talk to; the alpine wall, the end of the whole story, had four.

Three desert lines: **The Blowhole**, a compression tower where the wind comes up through it about four and you want to be off by then, with its own named feature — **The Bellows**, a slot the wind comes through, cold hands and you can hear it coming. **Squeeze Chimney**, which *nobody has ever enjoyed and goes in the book anyway*. And **Sunstroke Slab** — *no shade, no holds, no hurry; two of those are a problem.* Act 2 gains a stage as well, so it is 8 against 9 and 9.

Three conversations for the alpine, and the useful thing about the gap was who was missing rather than how many. **Pim's thread stopped in the desert** — the one person who was never going to follow you up there — so now he says so: *"This is where I turn round."* Ask him to come as far as the moraine and he thinks about it longer than you expected, then means it when he says ask again next year, **which is worse**. **Nita** has been on that face twice and came down both times unable to say why. And **Marge** wants it on the record that she did not try to talk you out of it, and gives you a stub of chalk gone hard with age out of his van.

**11 / 11 / 11 climbs and 7 / 6 / 7 conversations**, counted off the act maps rather than the table — because the three new lines are appended at the **end** of `ROUTES`, since inserting them into the act 2 block would have shifted every index in acts 2 and 3. That moved the tutorial from index 30 to 33 and nothing broke, because everything finds it by flag; there is now a test asserting both that and the absence of any hardcoded route index, which is what the *"never append to ROUTES"* warning was actually about.

The signature guard earned its keep on the way: it caught The Blowhole being given The Chimney's named hold, which would have put the same feature on two different lines. 197 checks, campaign 49.0 / 52.3%.

### v9.30 — CARD-6: a curse you earned (2026-07-27)
The audit was sharper than the item. There are **ten curses, not twenty** — the twenty was my own count of the string `'curse'` — and **only two of them ever arrived**: Cold Shut and Sandbagged Beta, from three events of which two hand out the same one, plus the *Sandbagged* mutator and `TAKE TWO`. **Eight were unreachable.**

A curse should be the price of something you wanted, so three of them now come from decisions the game was already watching you make.

**Flapper** — you came off, and your tips were already gone. *You went again on tips that were already gone.* **Doubt** — you came off with the top in reach, past the point `ENG-13` calls exposed. *That stays with you.* **Ego** — you put a line in the book graded higher than the ones before it, which is the record `FA-1c` has been keeping and `NARR-8` started using. *You told everyone the grade before anybody repeated it.*

One line in this needed care and is asserted rather than trusted. The design values say **climbing on wrecked skin is a bad decision the game permits and never rewards**, so *Flapper* had to be the consequence arriving rather than a payout for grinding: **topping out never costs you anything, however wrecked you are.** Only coming off does. There is a test for exactly that, because the difference between those two is the difference between modelling care and rewarding damage.

One of each kind a run — the same mistake twice is the same lesson — and every curse the system can hand out must exist, be a curse, and say why. Campaign 49.0 / 53.0% against 50.5 / 57.0%, which is about the right price for consequences that were not there before. 195 checks.

### v9.29 — TEST-4: the screens where a run ends (2026-07-27)
Four of the twenty-seven screens appeared in neither suite — `circuitNext`, `prepare`, `sessionEnd`, `stats` — and **three of them are where a run ends**, which is where state gets written to the file. That is the exact shape of the bug `NARR-4` turned up by accident, after it had been quietly emptying saves for who knows how long.

The audit found something on the way in. **The circuit's exit was doing its own `Math.max` on your best score, inline in the screen** — a hand-written state transition at the moment a run ends, which is the pattern `SAVE-2` exists to forbid. It is `walkAwayStep` in the engine now, and it is tested: banking a new best, **not** overwriting a better one with a worse run, clearing the route and the deck, and losing nothing of yours on the way out.

The other three are covered by what they are actually at risk of. **Ending a session** must bank the holds you worked as beta and file today's attempt — and must not touch your level, collection, conversations, ticks or first ascents, nor file a plain session as today's problem. **The numbers screen** must survive a save with nothing in it, which is the classic divide-by-zero: the reduce that finds your hardest send starts at −1 and has to stay a number on an empty book, and every rarity pool has to be non-empty or the collection line divides by zero. **Prepare** walks three indices into three tables, so each must stay inside its table, and `xpMult` must return a number even when handed a mutator that does not exist.

And a guard so this cannot recur: **every screen the app can show must be named somewhere in the suites.** Mentioning a phase is a low bar, but the four that were missing were missing entirely. 190 checks.

### v9.28 — BAL-5: the post does not cost you the day (2026-07-27)
The oldest open P1, and the measurement was worse than the item claimed. A trading post was offered in **4 of 25 stages** across the whole campaign — one stage each in acts 2 and 3 — so picking at random you would see **1.00 posts against 2.67 camps**. The draft economy, the price of a boon, and the conversations `NARR-4` put behind a counter were all built on a node you meet once a run.

Adding more was the obvious fix and it was not enough. **The harness was skipping the posts it was offered, and it was right to.** Forcing the visits cost **eight points of completion**, because a post *cost you the stage*, and a stage of climbing is worth more than anything on the shelf. Posts were not underused. They were correctly refused.

So the fix is not the supply, it is the price. **A trading post is in town, on the drive between areas — it does not cost you a day on the rock.** You stop in, buy, and still take a climb. Once a stage, so it cannot be farmed, and the node greys out with *"You have been round it already. Take a climb."* Nine more posts appended to existing stages as well, never inserted as new ones, because the forecast addresses a stage by index.

Posts now run **1.0 to 2.9 a run** against 0.2 to 0.6, and the campaign is unmoved at 50.3 / 55.3%. **Talks went from 2.7 a run to 5.3** — `NARR-4` put five of the seventeen conversations behind a counter nobody could reach, so the whole story is now hearable in about three runs instead of eight. That was the real cost of this bug and it was invisible until the post became reachable.

One harness finding on the way: it was buying **up to six cards a visit**, and every card past the ones you need dilutes the draw. It buys only what beats the median card in its own deck now, which is what somebody weighing a purchase actually does — and that alone was worth six points.

A test pins the shape: a post must be offered about as often as a camp, leaving must not advance the stage, the same post must not open twice at one stage, and it must open again at the next one and in the next act — with a check that act 1 stage 3 and act 2 stage 3 are not the same key. 184 checks.

### v9.27 — VIS-1: the meter shows where the turn takes you (2026-07-27)
I described the pump meter in the brainstorm as *"a numeral"*, and it was not — it has been an eleven-segment bar with a danger zone for a long time. **Third time in a row I have proposed something by describing the game from memory instead of reading it.** What was actually missing is better than what I proposed.

`previewPump` has been computing exactly where COMMIT leaves you since `UX-4`, verified accurate over 5,800 turns, and **the meter never drew it.** The entire push-your-luck decision — the pillar the game is built on — was one function call away from being visible and was being left as an inference.

It draws it now, ahead of where you are. Segments you are about to fill come up **hatched in amber**; if the turn puts you over the top they hatch **red** and the readout says **THAT IS A FALL**; if you are shaking out, the segments you are about to give back hatch **green**, so a rest reads as something coming off rather than nothing happening. The label carries it in words too — **6 / 11 · ▴ 9 AFTER THIS** — and near the top the whole bar trembles.

Three things it had to get right. The trembling is off under the **motion** setting, which is asserted rather than assumed. The projection is in the `aria-label` as well as the hatching, so it is not a visual-only feature. And it may never draw past its own end — checked over 150 climbs, because a meter reading 13 of 11 is exactly the bug `UX-10` shipped with `peakPump`.

Measured at every text size: the longest state, *9 / 11 · ▴ 11 AFTER THIS · THAT IS A FALL*, comes to 288px of 366 at the largest. 179 checks.

### v9.26 — RUN-8: the act as a page of the guidebook (2026-07-27)
The campaign map was a vertical list of buttons, which is the one thing the founding art direction said it would never be: *hand-drawn topo lines*, *every screen looks torn from the journal*. Ninety-odd versions of climbing-culture detail sitting under a dropdown.

It is a drawn page now. **Contours in ink**, four to six of them depending on where you are, on a different jaggedness per act — forest, desert, alpine. **The drainage runs out to the road along the bottom**, labelled, with the terrain named along the top. And the nine stages **climb up the page** rather than sitting at index 8 of an array, so being one move from the boss means being near the top of the map.

Two things already existed and were waiting to be drawn. `UX-16` has been keeping a **trail** of what each stage turned out to be, so the line behind you is inked in solid with a mark at each stage — a star for a send, a triangle for a camp, a cross for coming off — and `RUN-7`'s one-stage lookahead becomes the **dashed line** going on ahead of you. Where you are is a red dot that says **YOU**.

The node list stays underneath, because that is still how you choose. Tapping a 3px ink mark on a 390px screen is not a control, and the map has one `aria-label` describing the whole thing rather than fifteen unreachable targets.

**The position maths went into the engine rather than the component**, because it is geometry and geometry goes quietly out of bounds. A test checks all three acts at three seeds: every stage on the page, every stage **above the one before it**, no two within six pixels of each other, no `NaN`, a one-stage and a zero-stage act both drawing without falling over, and the same seed drawing the same map twice — otherwise it redraws itself on every render.

One false alarm in the string check: *FOREST* and *DESERT* were absent from the build because the labels are uppercased at render time. The lowercase table is there. 178 checks.

### v9.25 — ENG-20: two conditions that lied (2026-07-27)
**The item's premise was wrong, and I wrote it.** `ENG-20` said conditions moved grip and bite by ±1 and were worth about one hold. Measured: the same boulder with the same deck sends **96% in crisp conditions and 50% in hot sun** — a spread of forty-six points — and rock type is worth twenty-one. Conditions are one of the largest levers in the game.

What was actually wrong is that **two of the six did not do what they said.**

**Freezing** promised *"great friction, no feeling"* and measured **+2 and +9** across two different decks — a straight benefit. The reason is arithmetic: −1 Bite on every hold on the route is worth more than −2 Contact on every card in hand. It costs **−3 Contact** now, and that turns it into a real trade rather than a bonus: **−9 points for a sturdy deck, +3 for a fragile one**, because good friction genuinely protects a deck that cannot take bite. At −4 it was −84, which is why there is still a ceiling.

**Drizzle** said *"slopers are off"* and measured **+1 and −4**, because **only 6 of 231 cards care about slopers.** Wet rock should mean nothing sticks underfoot either, so it takes a point of Support — which every deck feels, since every deck has feet.

Two things about the guards. The existing one capped Contact at ±2, written after a weather that moved Power swung a battle 69 points; it is ±3 now, **with the measurement recorded beside it** and the Power rule still absolute. And a new one checks the thing that was actually broken: **a condition whose text promises a cost must carry one**, drizzle must reach the feet as well as the slopers, and no condition may be pure flavour text.

Then the ladder guard failed at 2.8% on the hardest rung where a direct run read 8.0%, and chasing that found something worth more than the fix. The guard was passing **`SHARP_AT=3`** — an override that forces the camp policy to sharpen and never rest. That *was* the real policy when the guard was written and has not been since `BAL-11` and `SIM-5` rewrote it, so **three balance guards have been measuring a deliberately worse player than the game has.** Dropped, and the sample raised to 400 because an 8% event at n=150 has a standard error of two points. 175 checks.

### v9.24 — BAL-12: four climbers, four ways to fail (2026-07-27)
The four measured **12.0 / 8.2 / 12.4 / 11.2%**, and the Comp Kid was bottom for a structural reason rather than a tuning one. Three signatures **compound over a burn** — beta coming back at −2 Grip, +1 Contact on every move, settling all the way to +3. The Comp Kid's fired **once, on turn one**, which on a twelve-hold route is worth about half a hold. Its runs ended at 80 turns against 121: explosive, then out of ideas.

It is now **Plastic — +2 Power on every move, and one less burn a day. All engine, no patience.** Power that pays every turn, priced in the only currency this climber actually feels.

Finding the price took five sweeps and most of them failed usefully. **A smaller hand is not a cost, it is a cliff**: +2 Power alone measured 25%, +2 Power with one less card in hand measured 4.5%, and nothing sits between. **Skin is free to this climber** — identical results at −2 and +3 skin, because it was never dying of skin. **Settling is nearly free too**, 25% against 23%, because its cards do not survive long enough to settle. Attempts were the only lever that moved, and only in whole steps: +2 Power and one less burn came out at 8.8%.

What closed the last three points was not the signature at all. The Comp Kid was shipping with **one rest card** — below the two that `DECK-1` had just established a deck needs to outrun its own pump, and the direct cause of those 70-turn runs. A second Shake Out, one fewer Bump.

**12.0 / 11.5 / 12.8 / 10.3%**, a spread of 1.24× where it was 1.51×. And they now fail in ways that match who they are: the Comp Kid dies of psyche at 24% having run out of tries, the Alpinist of skin at 59% having ground itself down, the other two in between.

Two guards. Every signature must **do something over a burn** rather than once, and every unlockable one must **pay for itself** — the Boulderer is exempt by name, because it is the baseline the others are measured against and its signature is the zero point. And every starting deck must be one you can climb with: fifteen cards, two rests, two feet, six hands. That last check is the one that would have caught this. 172 checks.

### v9.23 — DECK-1: build me one (2026-07-27)
Fifteen slots out of everything you own is a real decision, and not everybody wants to make it cold. **BUILD ME ONE** fills an empty loadout; put in the two or three cards you actually want to build around and it reads **FILL OUT THE OTHER 12** and builds around them instead. Whatever you picked stays picked — it fills in beside you, it does not overrule you, and a test checks that.

It runs on `cardValue`, which is the most carefully measured thing in this game and until now was only ever consulted by the drafter. It also builds on the tags you have already chosen, so seeding it with two gastons gets you a deck that wants opposition rather than a generically good one.

**And on its own, `cardValue` builds an unplayable deck**, which is the finding worth keeping. It scores a card in isolation and has no concept of what a deck looks like. The first version produced **fifteen rest cards, every time** — an uncapped synergy bonus feeding on itself, so each rest card made the next one look better. Capping rests, it then built **thirteen feet cards on a board with two hand lanes**, because feet cards carry high Contact and Support and nothing said a deck is mostly hands. Both versions measured **0% send rate**: worse than the hand-built default, handed to the exact player this feature exists for.

Shape is the builder's job, not the valuation's. Hard gates now, rather than weights: at most 3 rests, 3 technique, 4 feet; at least 8 hands and 9 moves, with the floors taking over once the remaining slots would not cover them. From a beginner's pool of starters and commons it takes an early boulder from the default's **11% to 87%**; from a full collection, 68–96%.

The measurement is the test. Structure is checked for both a beginner and a full collection, across three different seed decks, with copy limits and no curses — and then the only thing that really matters: **the built deck must send at least as often as the loadout it replaces**, which is the check both broken versions would have failed. 166 checks.

### v9.22 — UX-16: where you have been (2026-07-27)
Checked properly first, by reading the map screen rather than grepping for a name I had made up — which is how three of the last five suggestions turned out to already exist. This one genuinely did not: `tier` was a bare index and **nothing anywhere recorded which node you had actually taken.**

A run is nine stages and the map showed one, so twenty minutes in there was no way to see the shape of the trip. It reads back now, most recent first: **◂ flashed Deer Tick · camped · the storm · off The Fridge · and 3 before that.** Sends say which route and whether it was a flash, events are named by their own titles rather than logged as *"an event"*, camps and posts say so, and the first ascent node reads *put up a line* or *failed on the unclimbed one*.

The part worth the effort is where it is recorded. **Four separate places advance a stage**, and any one of them forgetting would leave a hole in the trail that nobody would notice until a run read wrong. All four go through `noteTrail` now, and **a test walks every `tier: s.tier + 1` in the engine and fails if there is no `noteTrail` beside it** — so a fifth advance added later cannot silently skip it.

The rest of the tests cover the wording, because that is the whole feature: a flash reads as a flash, a third-burn send does not, an event is remembered by its name, every note is under forty-two characters so it fits a phone, and the trail appends rather than replaces. 162 checks.

### v9.21 — UX-14: finding a card (2026-07-27)
Five quality-of-life items were proposed and **three of them already existed.** Worth recording plainly rather than quietly: the deck-peek sheet (*WHAT IS LEFT*, grouped by name, with the right caveat that you see contents and not order), typing in a seed (`seedIn`, an input, and `codeSeed` wired to `pickSeed`), and a stats screen — all present and all missed, because they were checked for with greps for identifiers that had been invented rather than by reading the screens. The lesson is the same one this project keeps learning from the other direction: **check the disk, not your model of it.**

What was actually missing: the loadout screen offered fifteen slots out of everything you own, sorted, and **no way to find anything.** `BAL-2` made 203 cards reachable inside 33 runs, so that list only gets longer.

Search now, by **name or by what a card does** — typing *pump* finds every card that mentions it, not just the ones with it in the title — and four ways to narrow: hands, feet, technique, or only what is already in the deck. The count line says *"48 of 203"* while a filter is on, and tells you plainly when a search finds nothing rather than leaving a blank list.

The filter predicate is in the screen, so the test checks the search box exists and is announced to a screen reader, that all four filters are wired, and then runs the predicate itself against the real pool: searching a card you know exists finds it and not everything else, every filter returns something and less than everything, and **searching what a card does returns cards that do not have the word in their name** — which is the difference between a search and a name match. 158 checks.

### v9.20 — NARR-8: three ways to reach the top (2026-07-27)
There was one epilogue, and it was well written, and it **told you he had done it and told nobody** — whether you arrived having read seven of his pages or none of them. That is precisely the thing `NARR-7` built the journal for: the pages are the cost of ignorance, and the ending was handing that away for free.

Three endings now, on what you actually understand when you get up there.

**Known**, five pages or more: the last page is in the same hand as the others and you can hear it by now. He did it thirty years ago, on his own, in the same nine days of weather you just used, and the page says why — and it is not modesty. *The line was the only thing he had ever done that nobody could take off him by having an opinion about it.*
