**Partial**, two to four: you recognise the handwriting, which is more than most people would, and the page does not say why. *You are holding the end of a story you have most of.* It sits badly, and not because you did not climb it.

**Stranger**, nought or one: a cairn somebody built, a page in handwriting you have never seen, a date and some numbers that might be a grade or might be a time. *You climbed the hardest thing on the wall and you are standing in the middle of somebody else's story without knowing you are in it.* And the line that should sting: **his pages were down there the whole time.**

The final choice stays — write your name, or put the stone back — but what it means changes with what you know. Writing your name under his is a different act from writing it above a blank line.

On top of that, the game has been counting something since `FA-1c` and never used it: **how you grade your own first ascents.** Sandbag them and the top of the wall tells you that whatever you write in the book, somebody is going to get on it expecting easier. Spray them and somebody is going to get on it and know. A full grade either way is an opinion rather than a lie — every guidebook disagrees with itself by that much — so the threshold is deliberately past one.

A quiet catch worth recording: the run-end screen compared `ending === 'told'`, and the ending is now a composite of what you knew and what you did. That comparison would have silently stopped matching and the winning text would have shown the wrong half of the story. Found by grepping for exact comparisons after changing the shape. Tests pin that understanding **only ever improves as you read more**, that the id carries both halves, and all four honesty verdicts. 154 checks.

### v9.19 — SKIRM-2: today's problem (2026-07-27)
Every design note since the founding brainstorm justified the seeded RNG the same way — it is *"what makes daily-seeded skirmish possible later"*, *"why the RNG is seeded from day one"*. Every shuffle, every draw, every event roll, every condition roll and, since `ENG-10`, every hold's hidden wobble has gone through it for ninety-odd versions. **The word "daily" appeared nowhere in the codebase.** This is the collection.

**TODAY'S PROBLEM** sits at the top of the menu: one route, generated from the date itself, the same for everybody, and one go at it. V3 to V7, nine to thirteen holds, harder than a skirmish and short enough to do in a coffee break. You get a score — holds worked, a bonus for topping out that scales with the grade, and how quick and how unpumped you did it, because that is how people actually compare a session — plus a personal best and a streak of consecutive days.

The determinism is the whole feature, so it is tested at three depths: **the same date gives the same spec**, sixty consecutive days give sixty different seeds, and — the one that matters — **the rock itself comes out identical**, hold for hold, grip for grip, wobble for wobble. Two people on the same day are on the same problem, down to which holds they cannot read yet.

Banking an attempt is tested for the things that go wrong with streaks: it files under today, closes the attempt, **cannot be banked twice**, continues a streak only if you played yesterday, resets after a gap of years, and still scores what you climbed if you came off. 152 checks.

Nine more items logged from the same session — `NARR-8` (more than one ending), `BAL-12`, `META-2`, `NARR-9`, `EVT-3`, `UX-11`, `ENG-20`, `AUD-1`, and `SHIP-1` which was already there.

### v9.18 — ENG-14 answered: not a technical failure, a design one (2026-07-27)
Third attempt, and the first one that got a real answer. **`ENG-19` was the blocker all along** — resolve was handing `powerAgainst` the pre-move board while the preview used the post-move one, so on a route-move turn the two disagreed about power by exactly enough to cross the link threshold. With that fixed, linking went in and worked: **the preview said it 339 times and it happened 339 times, zero wrong across 2,051 turns**, and the 28-combination sweep passed at zero.

So the mechanic is correct, and it is still not shipping — because once it worked it could finally be measured, and what it does is the opposite of what it was for.

`ENG-14` existed to **let good play shorten a route**. What it actually does is **let decks that are already winning finish faster**. Free, at a threshold of 3, it fires on 17% of turns and takes the ladder from 52/51/54/33/38/16/14 to **91/85/87/73/72/53/53**. Raised to 7 it fires **0% of the time on a starter deck** and still lifts the campaign from 53% to 70% — it only ever pays out for decks with power to spare, which is the definition of a rich-get-richer amplifier.

Two corrections were tried and both failed instructively. **Lengthening acts 2 and 3 to compensate made it worse** — 71.2% — because more holds means more chances to link, so the fix fed the mechanic. **Charging pump for the hurry** was catastrophic in the other direction: ladder top down to 24.7%, campaign to 17%, and the pump preview broken. That is the same lesson this project has now learned five times: **a per-turn pump cost compounds out of all proportion to its size.**

There is no middle. Free it gives away fifteen points to the players who need them least; priced it takes away thirty-five from everyone. Reverted, and **closed rather than left open** — the item is answered. If linking ever returns it needs to be something a player *chooses* and pays for out of a resource that does not compound, not something that happens to them for being ahead. 150 checks, and the ladder and campaign are exactly where they were.

### v9.17 — DES-4: Font grades (2026-07-27)
Open since the first day and never done. Everything read in **V-scale**, which is one of the two scales climbers actually use, and this game is fussy enough about real hold types, real ethics and real conditions that it should not be parochial about the numbers.

**GRADES** in MORE switches the whole game between **V-scale** and **Font** — V3 becomes 6A, V7 becomes 7A+, V11 becomes 8A — across the map, the guidebook, the logbook, the route track, the post-climb screen and the first-ascent claim, where you are choosing a number rather than reading one. The setting is yours rather than the run's, so it carries across expeditions and into the save file.

Ungraded things stay ungraded in both: the finale and an unclimbed first ascent read **?**, because nobody has put a number on them yet and that is the point of them.

The conversion is the real one — 4, 5, 5+, 6A, 6B, 6C, 7A, 7A+, 7B, 7C, 7C+, 8A, 8A+ — and a test pins all thirteen pairs, because climbers will check and being wrong about this is the kind of thing that tells somebody the game was made by a person who does not climb. It also checks the scale never changes how hard anything actually is, and that a grade off either end of the table returns something rather than `undefined`. 150 checks.

### v9.16 — SAVE-2: guarding the transitions (2026-07-27)
The data loss `NARR-4` turned up existed for a specific reason worth fixing at the root: the suite tested **save then load**, and never tested **the transitions between modes**. A round trip through the file was verified in six ways; starting a second expedition was verified in none.

Two guards. The first checks the **shape** rather than the symptom: anywhere a state is built from `freshRun` or `newRun` — from nothing, rather than from the state you are already in — `carryOver` or `loadGame` must be right beside it. That is the only pattern that can lose you, and there are three places in the file that do it. The second walks **a lived-in save through every mode you can start** — a new expedition, the Circuit, a one-off route, and the file itself — and checks twelve persistent things come out the other side: your level, your collection, the conversations you have heard, your logbook, the acts you have ticked, the lines you have put up, your circuit best, and four settings.

Also checked, and clean: what `carryOver` keeps against what the save file writes. Twenty-five fields against forty-one, and the only difference in one direction is `slot`, which is the file's own identity and written separately, and in the other direction is all run-scoped — skin, cash, gear, the journal pages you have found this trip. No second hole.

Negative-tested by putting the original bug back — hand-copying one field instead of carrying everything — which the shape guard catches by name and line number. Worth noting that `tsc` also caught it, but only as an unused import, which would have passed silently if `carryOver` were used anywhere else in the file. 149 checks.

### v9.15 — NARR-4, and the run that ate your save (2026-07-27)
The story is not thin: seventeen conversations across five people, and Marge's seven-part thread is the spine of the whole thing. It is **gated**. Conversations only ever happened at camps, a run gets two or three of those, and the measured rate was **2.2 talks a run** — so hearing Marge out took four completed expeditions and most players would never get there.

A trading post has people in it, which is the obvious second place for someone to be standing. Talks there now, and Marge stays at the fire where she belongs. The first version raised nothing at all, because it filtered the *first* available conversation and Marge holds seven of the seventeen at the front of the queue — it has to look **past** her. **2.2 to 2.7 a run.**

Then the measurement asked a question I should have asked first: does what you have heard survive between runs? It is saved, so it is meant to. **It did not.**

`startLostLine` built a fresh state and hand-copied five fields across it — level, xp, owned, sends, wins. **Everything else persistent was dropped on the floor**: the conversations you had heard, your logbook and the beta it buys, the acts you had ticked, **the first ascents you had named**, your run history, your circuit best, your unlocked styles, and every setting down to text size and colour-blind mode. And because the next save writes whatever is in memory, **starting a second expedition and reaching a save point wrote the emptied values over the file.** Not a display bug — data loss, affecting most of what this session built.

Fixed by naming the thing rather than listing it again: **`carryOver` returns everything that belongs to the player rather than to a run**, and the one place that starts an expedition spreads it. A test walks a lived-in save through a new run and checks all twenty-five fields survive — and, in the other direction, that `carryOver` drags nothing out of the run just ended. 147 checks.

### v9.14 — SIM-5: one way to play a card (2026-07-27)
Playing a technique card was implemented twice: once in the screen's `playBonus`, once inside the harness's `autoPlay`. A rule added to one failed to reach the other **three times running**. Sequences never started for the drafter, so `SIM-3` measured them at −3 and every number quoted for `ENG-7` described a game without them. Free Rein measured **+0 three separate times** in `BAL-9`. Clipping bought nothing in `ROPE-4`, and the first measurement of the fix showed no change at all. Each time the mechanic looked worthless, and each time the fix was to teach the harness a rule the game already had.

One function now. **`playBonusStep` owns the rules** — cost, Free Rein, sequences, clipping, skin, shed, draw, restore, Power, grip cuts, brushing — and both callers go through it. The screen went from **43 lines to 8**, and what it kept is what a screen should own: the undo stack and advancing the seed. The harness kept what a policy should own: which card, which lane, and whether it is worth the pump.

Separating those two things exposed that the policy had been carrying a bad habit. Picking **the first occupied lane** rather than the best one cost about ten points across the ladder — a Power boost is worth most on the lane closest to clearing, where it turns a near miss into a hold worked, and a grip cut is worth most on the hold furthest from going. The first version of this refactor shipped that mistake and the balance guardrails caught it: a climber at 4%, the hardest rung at 2.7%. Fixed, and the ladder reads **52.0 / 51.0 / 54.5 / 33.0 / 38.0 / 16.0 / 14.0%** with the campaign at 54.0 / 58.8%.

A guard holds the line: the screen may not handle `shed`, `draw`, `gripCut`, `powerAll`, `clip` or `seq` itself, the harness may not either, and both must be seen calling `playBonusStep`. 146 checks.

### v9.13 — TUT-2: teaching the game that exists (2026-07-27)
The tutorial taught eight things: tapping, Power against Grip, the feet lane, Greasy, pump, Settle, Sharp, and the crux. Since it was written the game has grown **opposition, dynos with a stick check, the route acting every few turns, exposure, greedy scaling, sequences, boons, mutators, choosing a line, hold uncertainty, named holds and resolve order** — and it mentioned **not one of them**. A new player met a fourteen-mechanic game with an eight-step introduction, and most of what is on screen in the first ten seconds went unexplained.

The Warm-Up Boulder is twelve holds now, with a lesson on each. The new ones are the things you meet immediately rather than eventually: **a hold reads as a range until you have been on it**, **the route says what it is about to do a turn beforehand**, **sideways moves need something pulling back**, **lanes resolve in the order you placed them**, and **EXPOSED**, which arrives near the top of every route you will ever climb.

Two faults, both found by measuring rather than reading it back. The tutorial deck contained **no opposing cards at all**, so the opposition lesson taught something the player could not do — two gastons and an undercling now sit in it. And the lessons had drifted **out of alignment with the holds they stand on**: the Sharp lesson was landing on the crux. Every lesson is now written against the hold at its index, with the hold order in a comment above them so the next edit cannot silently shift it. It still tops out 71% of the time for a player doing nothing clever.

The glossary had the same rot in a smaller way: eight entries missing, and two describing rules that have since changed — **Commit still promised a guaranteed clear**, from before `ENG-15` made a dyno a gamble, and **Greedy still described the binary +2** from before `ENG-16` made it a slope. Both corrected, eight added, and one entry that said *"Takes 1 less Bite"* and nothing else expanded.

Two guards: the tutorial must mention the mechanics you meet in the first minute **and be playable with the deck it hands you**, and no glossary entry may describe a rule that has changed out from under it. 145 checks.

### v9.12 — ROPE-4: what a piece is for (2026-07-27)
The question was whether the rope earns its place at all: three roped routes out of thirty-one, three protection cards out of two hundred and nineteen, and a measurement from `ROPE-3` saying that **carrying a rack made a roped route worse**.

Re-measured first, and the finding got narrower and clearer. Roped routes are not unfairly hard — they read 0–5% against an Act 3 boulder's 6% with the same deck, which is the deck being weak rather than the ropes being broken. What was broken is that **a rack never helped**: 5%→2%, 2%→2%, 0%→0%. Protection only ever *reduced what a fall cost*, and at an average runout of **1.2 holds** that is worth about one skin. Nothing is worth three card slots for one skin.

So protection stopped being mitigation and became an enabler, which is what a piece actually buys a climber. **Clip, and for one turn you climb like somebody who is not going to hit the ground** — +1 Power on everything, on a rope only. The runout still resets; that was never the point. A rack now reads **5%→29%, 2%→63%, 0%→52%**, and does nothing at all on a boulder, which is asserted rather than assumed.

That makes a roped line a gear check, so the map says so before you commit: **"2 pieces on your rack — clip and you can try hard off it"**, or **"Nothing on your rack. You can lead it, but you will be climbing scared."**

The first measurement showed no change whatsoever, and the reason is one this project has now hit three times: **the harness has its own bonus-playing path**, and it had never learned to clip. `SIM-3` was this, `BAL-9`'s Free Rein was this, and so was this. Clipping is handled in `autoPlay` now — but the real answer is one code path for playing a card, and that is worth its own item.

### v9.11 — ENG-19 closed: the move the preview saw and resolve did not (2026-07-27)
Closed by Evan, and the cause was a line I had looked straight at twice.

`ENG-11` applies the route's telegraphed move at the top of `resolve`, writing the changed holds into the local `boardH`. **It does not write them into `s`.** So `powerAgainst(s, …)` and `biteAgainst(s, …)` were reading the board as it stood *before* the move, while `previewLane` — which builds itself a post-move state — was reading it after. On every turn except the one where a move landed, those are the same board and everything agreed.

The specific case is neat: a **crumble** on the feet hold sets `clean`, which strips the hold's ability, which means a Featureless foothold **stops being featureless and starts granting Support**. Power changes for both hands on exactly that turn. Six of the twenty-eight combinations, one or two turns in four hundred — which is about how often a move lands on the feet.

I had the evidence in front of me. The failing logs I printed twice both contained *"blank loses a flake. Sharper, and whatever it did is gone"*, and I read it as noise because I knew the preview applied route moves. It did. **Resolve was the one that did not**, and I never checked the direction of the mismatch.

The fix is three lines: a post-move state built once and handed to both calls. The sweep now passes at **zero across all 28 combinations** and the guard is strict again rather than sitting at the 1% compromise `TEST-3` shipped with — negative-tested by handing `powerAgainst` the pre-move state, which fails six combinations immediately. 142 checks.


The six combinations `TEST-3` left behind, investigated by instrumenting the failing turns rather than reasoning about them — which is what the last two of these cost me.

**The guess was wrong.** They are not phase boundaries. On the clearest case: a Gaston and a Layback, both opposing moves. The Gaston works its hold and then comes off, which leaves the Layback with nothing to pull against — its power drops from 6 to 2 against a grip of 3, so it does not clear. Resolve gets that right. **The preview reports `blows=true` for the Gaston and still predicts the Layback clears**, which are two statements it should not be able to make at once.

The likeliest cause looked like duplication: whether a lane comes off was **written out twice**, once for the lane itself and once inside the simulation of the lanes before it, and two copies of one formula drift apart. Extracted into a single `laneBlows`, used in both places. **The count did not change**, so that was not it — but one formula in one place is right regardless, and it removes a whole class of future drift.

Not closed. Six combinations, one or two turns in about four hundred, and the sweep holds the line at 1% so a systematic break still fails loudly. The item now records what it is not, what it is, and the next step: **instrument `previewLane` from the inside** and print the live board at the point of the power call. Probing it from outside has now got twice as far as reasoning did, and neither has finished the job. 142 checks.

### v9.9 — TEST-3: the deck-shape sweep (2026-07-27)
Three separate bugs have now shipped behind a green suite for the same reason: **every accuracy check ran the same starter deck on the same kind of route.** The drafter could not see sequences (`SIM-3`), the preview was wrong 13% of the time on opposition (`ENG-18`), and it was blind to named holds' abilities for six versions (`v9.8`). Each was found by accident, while looking for something else.

The suite now sweeps **seven deck shapes against four kinds of route** — starter, opposition, technique, dynos, greedy, rests and protection, against a plain route, a route with a named hold, a roped route and a boss with phases. Twenty-eight combinations, every one exercised for at least fifty turns.

**It found eleven broken combinations on its first run.** Most of that was mine rather than the game's: **a caught fall on a rope rewinds `cleared` back to the last piece**, so the difference between before and after is not what the lanes did, and every roped combination failed on a metric that could not measure them. Those turns are now skipped rather than fudged, and named as such, alongside dynos — which state a probability rather than make a prediction.

What survived is six combinations missing **one or two turns in about four hundred**, all on boss routes where a phase can trigger part way through a turn, and on greedy decks. Logged as `ENG-19`. The guard is set at **1%** rather than zero, which is an honest compromise: it tolerates the edges I know about and would have caught every one of the three bugs above, all of which were an order of magnitude worse.

Negative-tested by putting the `ENG-18` bug back — reading the placed board instead of the live one — which the sweep fails on immediately. 142 checks.

### v9.8 — the preview was blind to named holds (2026-07-27)
`ENG-14` was attempted a second time, following the note the first attempt left: linking as **an extra clear inside `resolve`**, from power the preview can already see, rather than a discount applied at refill where it cannot look. That was the right diagnosis — accuracy went from **295 wrong in 2,217 turns to one wrong in 2,064**.

One is not zero, and it could not be explained. The preview reported a link on a surplus of 2 against a stated minimum of 3, which the code as written should not do, and two rounds of instrumenting the failing turn did not account for it. **Reverted again** — the same call as v9.2, for the same reason: a preview whose behaviour I cannot explain is worse than a mechanic the game does not have. The item now says the third attempt should start by instrumenting `previewLane` itself rather than reasoning about it from the outside.

**The attempt paid for itself anyway, because chasing that number found a real bug.** `ROUTE-5` gave ten routes a named signature hold with its own ability, and taught `abilityOf` to return it. **The preview never got the message** — it was still reading `HOLD_STATS[hold.name].ability` directly, so from v8.0 onward it has been blind to every named hold's ability, and to brushing, which a stray `!hold.clean` beside it was quietly compensating for. The Guillotine, the Wet Jug and the Organ Pipe all resolved differently from how the preview said they would.

Fixed by having the preview ask `abilityOf` like everything else does, and guarded two ways: the preview **may not compute an ability by hand**, and `abilityOf` must return every signature's own ability and drop it under a brush. 141 checks.

### v9.7 — RUN-7: what is coming (2026-07-27)
The map showed one stage at a time. You picked one of three or four nodes, advanced, and were shown three or four more — which meant every decision was made **blind to what it was choosing between**. Spending your last camp two stages before the boss and spending it right before were the same click, and you could not tell which one you were making.

It shows the next stage now: **▸ AFTER THIS · a camp · Cathedral Traverse (V4) · whatever turns up · an unclimbed line**. Named climbs are named, with their grades; everything else is described in the words the game already uses. Two warnings sit under it when they are true: **the boss is next — whatever you are short of, this is where you get it**, and **no camp between here and the boss**, which is the question anybody actually asks a guidebook.

It is deliberately one stage, not the whole act. Seeing everything turns a run into a solved problem; seeing one move ahead turns it into a plan you can be wrong about.

The tests found two things. The *no camp before the boss* warning fired on the boss tier itself — true in a useless way, since the boss was underneath you rather than ahead — which needed `bossAhead` as a separate question from `campBeforeBoss`. And the boss test failed on **act 3, which has two boss tiers**: Summit Block and then The Lost Line. The assertion assumed one per act, which is true of every act except the one the whole game is named after. 139 checks.

### v9.6 — ENG-18: the order you place them (2026-07-27)
**`ENG-17` was dropped rather than built, and that is the first thing worth recording.** It proposed making skin a currency you spend for power — tape it up, go again on raw tips. The project's own design values say: *"Climbing on wrecked skin is a bad decision the game permits, never a rewarded strategy."* A card that pays you for skin makes grinding through pain the optimal line, which is precisely the thing that rule exists to forbid. It came off the list.

`ENG-18` instead. Left, right and feet were **three independent sums sharing a clock** — nothing any lane did could matter to another, so a turn was three parallel bets rather than a plan. Now **the order you place your cards is the order they resolve**, and every cross-lane rule reads the board as it stands: **a hand that has already come off is no longer holding anything for the other one.** Place the gaston first and it has its partner; place it second, after the hand supporting it has blown, and it does not. The cards carry a small 1-2-3, and the screen says the rule out loud.

Then the interesting part, which is the same shape as the last two turns. Every test passed — **and the preview was wrong 13% of the time.** The suite's default deck contains no opposing cards at all, so nothing exercised the one rule that had just become order-dependent. Measured against a deck built out of opposition: **295 wrong in 2,217 turns.**

Unlike `ENG-14`, this one was fixable, because whether a lane blows depends on Contact against Bite and **never on opposition** — so the preview can work out which lanes come off before this one in a single pass, with no recursion. It does that now, and the same measurement reads **2,217 right, 0 wrong**.

That deck is now a permanent test, because a suite that only ever plays one kind of deck will keep passing while the game is broken for every other kind. 137 checks.

### v9.5 — ENG-16: fighting (2026-07-27)
Pump only ever went one way. You gained it and you shed it, and **nothing in the game ever wanted you to have any** — which made *shake out or push on* a question about delay rather than a real choice, because one answer was always safer.

The mechanic for this turned out to already exist and to be doing almost nothing. `greedy` was **a binary +2 above pump 7, on two cards** — a footnote rather than a way to play. Rather than adding a second keyword that meant the same thing, the existing one was made to **scale**: a greedy move gains +1 Power for every 2 pump you are carrying, so it is worth nothing fresh and +5 when you are nearly off. Five more cards carry it, so it is seven in total and enough to build toward.

What makes it a real decision is that it **argues with `ENG-15`**. A dyno wants you fresh — 90% when you are, 30% when you are cooked. A greedy move wants you wrecked. A deck holding both is genuinely pulled in two directions every time the pump meter moves, which is what this was for. The card says which way it is going: **FIGHTING +3** in red, or *NOT PUMPED ENOUGH* when you are too fresh for it.

Tuned by measurement, and the first version was wrong for a reason worth keeping. At **+1 per 3 pump** a deck with four greedy moves measured **4 points worse** than a plain one: the bonus arrived at pump 9, which is one turn from falling off, so it was reward that showed up too late to spend. At **+1 per 2** it lands at +1 — break-even, which is what a way of playing should be rather than a strictly better one.

A test pins the shape: a greedy move must gain with pump, it must be **a slope and not a cliff** (at least three distinct values across the meter, which the old binary version would fail), a plain move must not change at all, and **dynos and greedy moves must want opposite pump** — because if they ever agreed, the tension this was built for would be gone. 135 checks.

### v9.4 — ENG-15: the commitment check (2026-07-27)
The design baseline written on day one says **"Dynos skip segments on a commitment check."** What actually shipped was a keyword called `commit` that read *clears any hold, then burns out* — **guaranteed, no check, no skip**. The most committing move in climbing was the safest card in the deck, and had been for ninety versions.

A dyno is a gamble now, and the odds are yours to set. **Being pumped is what makes it one**: fresh you stick it about 90% of the time, close to falling about 30%, with your feet on worth another 15 points and being in flow a little more. Stick it and you go **straight past the next hold** — the skip the baseline promised. Miss it and you are off it, and the card is gone.

The number is on the card before you commit — **STICK 72%** in green, amber or red — because a gamble whose odds you cannot see is not a gamble, it is a punishment.

That raised the question `ENG-10` raised: the preview is verified exact, and a dyno cannot be predicted exactly. The answer is the same one — **the preview states the odds instead of claiming a certainty**. It reports the chance and says the lane does not clear, rather than the old behaviour of promising a clear that is now a coin the game has not flipped yet.

**Every existing test passed immediately, which was the wrong kind of good news**: the preview suite's decks contain no dynos, so nothing was checking the one card whose outcome had just become random. The gap is closed with the test I would most want here — **the stated odds must be the real odds**, five hundred climbs with three dynos in the deck, bucketed by what the preview claimed and compared against what actually happened. It failed first time at *stated 60%, stuck 95%*, because it was counting any lane that cleared on the same turn rather than the dyno itself. 134 checks.

### v9.3 — UX-10: what actually happened (2026-07-27)
Reported from a phone, and obvious once seen: topping out gave you a title, one line of *That is a send. Chalk it.*, six raw log lines, a button, and **two thirds of a blank screen**. The most satisfying moment in the game was its emptiest.

It now leads with what kind of send it was — **FLASHED IT** first go, *SECOND GO*, or *WORKED IT OUT* — and says something about it: *first go, no beta, no rehearsal. That is the one you tell people about.* Falling reads **SO CLOSE** within two holds of the top, or *NOTHING DOING* if you never got off the ground.

Under that, what the climb actually was: **holds worked, turns taken, the highest your pump ever got** — in red if you were within two of coming off — and the conditions you did it in. A send shows **CHALKED** with the XP and cash it paid and what the logbook now says about it: *first entry in the book for this one*, or *3rd time on it, best 1 burn*. A fall shows **WHAT STUCK** — the beta you banked, and how many burns you have left. The log is still there under **HOW IT WENT**, where it belongs, rather than being the whole screen.

The highest-pump figure needed something the game was not keeping, so `peakPump` is now recorded — and getting it right took three passes. It was first written mid-resolution, **before the hang tax**, so it could read lower than the pump it was meant to be the ceiling of. Moved to the end, it then read **13 of 11**, because pump spikes past the maximum before the fall check clamps it. Clamped on all six exits from `resolve`. And the test still failed, which turned out to be the familiar one: **`PUMP_MAX` was never exported to the harness**, so the assertion was comparing against `undefined` and could not pass whatever the code did.

Verified at 390px: the stat row fits on one line at every text size, 240 to 307px of 366 available. 132 checks.

### ENG-14 attempted and reverted (2026-07-27)
The last item on the battle list, and it did not ship. Recorded here because the reason is more useful than the feature would have been.

**Linking** — Power spent past what a hold needed carrying into the next hold in that lane — was built and worked. It fired on **25% of turns**, which is the right frequency, and it addresses the thing measured four separate times: hold count dominates everything, so a twelve-hold route is mostly more turns of the same turn, and good play could only ever survive a climb rather than shorten it.

Then the preview accuracy test failed: **one lane in 2,113 predicted a clear and blew instead**. Not a large number, and a preview that is right 99.95% of the time still sounds fine — but that test exists precisely because `UX-4` established the preview as something the player is entitled to trust, and a preview that lies occasionally is worse than one that is honestly vague.

Bisected cleanly: recording the surplus is harmless, **applying** it is what desyncs. The carry discounts a hold dealt at refill, after the preview for that turn has already been computed, and somewhere in that ordering a different lane changes behaviour. **I do not understand the mechanism yet**, and shipping a mechanic that makes the preview wrong on a guess about why is not a trade worth making. Removed entirely — a half-applied version would be worse than none.

The item stays open with a note on how to do it properly: linking should be an **extra clear inside `resolve`**, where `previewLane` can predict it from the power it can already see, rather than a discount applied at refill where the preview cannot look. 131 checks, all passing, and the preview is exact again.

### v9.2 — ENG-13: exposure (2026-07-27)
The last hold of a route played exactly like the first. Nothing about being nearly at the top changed anything — not the odds, not the stakes, not how it read.

Making holds bite harder as you climb would have been the obvious move and the wrong one: **bite is the most explosive dial in this game**, and `ROUTE-4`, `MODE-2`, `BOON-2` and `BAL-9` have each measured a per-turn cost compounding out of all proportion, twice to 0% completion. So exposure changes what a mistake **costs**, not the odds of a turn. Past **70%** of the way up you are **EXPOSED**, and it shows in red with how far up you are.

What it costs took two wrong answers to find, and both are the same mistake. **An extra skin on a high fall** took skin deaths from about 25% to **58–81%** across all four climbers. Charging **a psyche instead** took psyche deaths to **72–79%**. The reason is obvious in hindsight and worth writing down: **you fall constantly — that is what projecting is** — so any per-fall charge is not a sharpening of the ending, it is a different game. The balance guardrail caught the first version before it shipped, by which point the archetype spread had already gone past 2.5×.

Exposure now touches only the thing that is **rare and deliberate**: choosing to walk away when you were nearly there costs an extra psyche. Backing off the second hold of a route and backing off the last one are finally different decisions, and nothing else moved — archetypes read 10.5 to 16.0% and the campaign 42.0 / 53.0 / 54.3% by pages read.

The test was rewritten to assert the design rather than the first guess: exposure must cost something on a bail, and must cost **nothing** per fall — with the measurements that establish why written beside it. 131 checks.

### v9.0 — ENG-12: which hand (2026-07-27)
The board has three lanes and only ever really had two. Left hand and right hand were **interchangeable** — nothing in the game ever cared which one a card went into, so every placement decision was "hands or feet" and never "which hand".

Climbing's own answer is opposition. Fourteen moves now **pull sideways** — gaston, undercling, sidepull, layback, cross-through, the compression family, arm bar, chicken wing — and a sideways pull needs something pulling back. **Alone it is −2 Power. Opposed by another sideways move in the other hand it is +2.** A gaston with an empty hand beside it reads 1; with any card beside it, 3; with a second opposition, 5. Plain moves are untouched either way, which is asserted rather than assumed.

It lands in `powerAgainst`, which means the resolve preview picks it up for free and stays exact — the one place in this engine where adding a rule costs nothing elsewhere. The board says what is happening: a sideways move with nothing beside it reads **NOTHING TO PULL AGAINST −2** in red, and **OPPOSED +2** in green when the pair is there. All fourteen cards say *Opposition* in their own text, and a test checks that none can be added without saying so.

Two things went wrong in the marking, both the same shape. A regex that skipped cards already carrying the flag **looked 160 characters ahead and saw a neighbouring card's**, so five moves were silently missed — caught by listing every intended card against what actually took it. And `Stem` turned out to be a **feet** card, which cannot oppose anything, so it was left out.

Campaign completion 53.6 / 56.0% by pages read, essentially unmoved. 130 checks.

### v8.9 — ENG-11: the route makes a move (2026-07-27)
*The route is the opponent* has been the thesis since the founding brainstorm, and in eighty-odd versions **the opponent has never done anything**. Holds were static numbers; the only thing that ever changed one was a boss phase. Every fight was really a solitaire puzzle that happened to be scored.

The route now acts, roughly every third turn, and **always announces it a full turn before it lands**. *The left hand hold is greasing up* — and next turn it does, at +2 Grip. *A breeze on the right hand hold, it is coming into condition* — −2 Grip, because the route is not only ever hostile. *Wind coming up the face, all of it is about to bite* — +1 Bite on every lane. *Something is flaking off* — sharper, and whatever that hold's ability was is gone with the flake. It reads on screen as **NEXT TURN**, in red for trouble and green for a gift, and it is announced to a screen reader as a live region.

The telegraph is the whole design. A move that simply happened would be a hidden tax; a move you are told about is a turn you get to plan around, which is what makes it a fight rather than a dice roll.

That produced the right failure immediately: **the preview accuracy test broke**, because the preview did not know about the pending move and was mispredicting 66 lanes out of 2,109. The fix is not to exempt it — a telegraph is **information the player has**, so the preview now applies the pending move before predicting, and is exact again. A test pins that a preview reads differently when the route is about to act, so it can never quietly stop accounting for it.

Cadence measured at every third turn: campaign completion 53.6% against 57.6% at every fourth. Kept at three, because the point is that the route is doing something *to you* often enough to notice. 128 checks.

### v8.8 — ENG-10: you have not been on it yet (2026-07-27)
`UX-4` made the resolve preview **100% accurate**, verified over 7,000 lanes and 5,800 turns. It is some of the best work in this file and it quietly cost the game one of its stated pillars. **Push-your-luck requires luck.** A turn you can read exactly before you commit is not a gamble, it is arithmetic, and the preview had turned every commit into a sum.

The fix is not to make the preview worse. The numbers underneath are still exact — the harness still checks them, and that test still passes untouched. What changed is **what you are allowed to see**. A hold you have not worked reads as **a span rather than a number**: *9–10* on the board instead of *9*. Once you have beta on that kind of hold it reads true. **Projecting is what buys certainty**, which is what projecting has always been for, and now the mechanic and the fiction say the same thing.

The uncertainty is deliberately **epistemic rather than random**. Each hold's wobble is rolled once when the route is built, from the run RNG — so the hold is exactly what it is, you simply have not touched it, and **a seed still replays identically**. That mattered enough to get its own test: same seed, same grips, same wobbles, or seed sharing is broken.

Getting the disclosure right took a second pass. The first version returned an exact number for holds that happened to have no wobble, which **would have told you which holds were flat just by whether they showed a span at all**. Every unknown hold now shows a span exactly `WOBBLE` wide, so the display gives away nothing about which side of it this particular hold sits on — and that is asserted for every hold on a route.

Campaign reads 49.2 / 56.8% by pages read, a little harder than before, which is what you would expect when the player can no longer see the answer. 126 checks.

### v8.7 — EVT-2: a tax with a menu (2026-07-27)
You meet about eight events a run and nobody had ever priced what their choices are worth. All 68 of them, converted into one currency — skin, psyche, cash, XP, a card, a boon, a journal page — so branches can be compared against each other.

Most of the set is healthy: *Through The Window* spans 47.6 points between its two branches, which is a real gamble, and the mean choice sits at about zero. Two faults came out of it.

**`Water In The Wash` had a spread of 0.4** between moving camp in the dark and gambling on the weather. Both branches cost you something similar, which is not a decision, it is a toll booth with two lanes. Moving camp now pays properly — you watch the wash become a river from high ground, dry, with the kettle on — and staying put costs gear *and* skin. `Access Notice` and `Poison Ivy` were the same shape and were given the same treatment: doing the decent thing now has a reason beyond a clean conscience.

**The pricing itself was wrong first, in the way that keeps happening here.** It had no term for `curse`, so *A Friendly Grade* and *The Hold Goes* — both of which put a curse in your deck on one branch — read as the flattest events in the game when they are among the sharpest. Priced at −11, the value `CARD-5` measured, they came out fine and the real culprit was left standing alone.

The test that came out of this was written too strictly and had to be relaxed, which is worth recording as a design judgement rather than a bug. It first demanded that **every event have a branch worth taking**, and four failed: running out of water, rockfall, the drive to the tower, a logging road. Those have no good option because **they are bad news, and the choice is which cost you pay** — a storm does not owe you an upside. The guard now requires that every event be a real decision, and that grim ones stay under a quarter of the set. 124 checks.

### v8.6 — BAL-11: the van is not the answer (2026-07-27)
`SIM-4` left this open with the title *"sharpening is a trap"*, on the evidence that blocking it made *Flash* **easier than the rung below it**. Measuring the camp's three options against each other says the title was wrong and the problem was real.

**Sharpening is not a trap. It is high variance.** Sharpening the right card — three copies of Lock Off in a fifteen-card deck — is worth **+26 points of send rate**. Sharpening the wrong one, two copies of a card the deck barely uses, is worth **+1**. That is a skill expression rather than a defect, and it is the sort of thing `UX-1`'s hints exist to help with.

The actual problem was the other option. **A find is worth +28 to +42** — a good shoe measured at +42 on its own — so going through the van was the correct answer at **every camp in the game**, and the rest and the sharpen were decorations next to it. That is what `META-5` bought when it added the van to fix find starvation, and the price was that the camp stopped being a decision.

The van is now **once a range**. It still fixes the starvation it was added for — you get three finds a run rather than none — without being the only thing worth doing at a fire. The camp says so plainly, and reads *THE VAN IS PICKED OVER* once you have been through it.

*Flash* against *Headpoint* was **46.4% to 36.8%** before this and is **34.9% to 36.0%** after it, at n=700 — the inversion is gone and the rung is properly harder than the one below. The campaign reads **45.0 / 58.7 / 62.0%** by pages read at 26 minutes a run.

A test pins the whole rule: the van opens once, the raid still uses up the camp whether or not it pays, a second raid in the same range gives nothing, and it reopens in the next one. 123 checks.

### v8.5 — BAL-2: the long tail (2026-07-27)
`BAL-2` had been sitting at P2 with a note saying the collection took about 127 runs, logged long ago and never checked. It was right, and the cause was one line.

`rollPackCard` **chose a rarity first and only then preferred what you did not own.** So a player who had collected all 88 commons still spent **60% of every pack roll** inside a tier that had nothing left to give them. The rarity is now drawn only from tiers that still hold something.

Measured against the pool a pack can actually reach: **95% of the collection went from 127 runs to 33, and 100% from 290 runs to 35** — about 126 hours down to 15. The first half of the pool is unchanged at ten runs, which is right; it was only ever the tail that was broken.

Two false trails on the way, both worth recording. The first target was "own all 216 cards", which is **impossible by construction** — 7 of those are starters you begin with and 6 are beta cards that only come from the journal, so no pack can ever reach 13 of them and every measurement read 600+ runs. The second was the level curve: `xpToNext` grows linearly at `25 + level * 10` while run income does not, so a plateau looked like the obvious fix. Capping it did speed things up, but the original curve turned out to be **fine on its own once the pack roll was fixed** — 50% at ten runs either way — so the cap was reverted. Two dials moved and put back before the actual defect was found.

Two tests: **a pack must reach past a rarity you have completed** — three hundred rolls against a full set of commons, none of which may come back common — and **every card in the game must be either collectable or a starter**, so a card can never be added that nobody can ever own. 122 checks.

### v8.4 — UX-1: the shelf explains itself (2026-07-27)
`cardValue` is the most carefully measured thing in this game — `SIM-3` taught it about sequences and protection and boons, `CARD-5` repriced the whole pool against a measured send rate — and the player has never been able to see a single bit of it.

Showing the number was never the answer; that exposes the machine and turns drafting into arithmetic. The shelf gives **the reasons** instead: up to two short lines under each card, drawn from the same signals the valuation uses, so the two cannot disagree. *You are short on feet. Nothing in here to shake out on. Plenty of technique already. Nothing to clip on a boulder. Your Kneebar Merchant makes this chip too. Wants work a hold 3 turns running. A curse — it does nothing good.* They show on the reward shelf and in the trading post, with a **DRAFT HINTS** toggle for anyone who would rather work it out.

The first version was ordered wrong, and that is the interesting part. Hints came out in the order they were computed, so a generic note about deck shape crowded out the reason *this* card was worth a slot — **Quickdraw's "nothing to clip on a boulder" was being cut** in favour of "no technique in the deck yet", which is true of every technique card ever offered. They are built in two passes now, **specific before structural**.

Two tests: **no more than two hints, none empty, none over sixty characters** — a phone constraint, checked against all 219 cards — every curse warning you first, and **a hint that changes when the situation does**: protection must read differently on a boulder than on a rope, and holding a boon must change what its cards say. 120 checks.

The toggle also had to be written twice. The first attempt used a `<Toggle>` component that **does not exist in this file** — the settings are plain buttons — and the string check caught it by reporting zero occurrences in the build.

### v8.3 — SIM-4: the rung that did nothing (2026-07-27)
*Flash* is the fifth rung of the ascent ladder and its entire effect was that camps give back one less skin. The harness's camp policy was `skin > SHARP_AT && best` — sharpen unless you are nearly dead — so it **almost never rested**, `campSkinFor` never entered the decision, and **Headpoint and Flash returned identical numbers to the turn for eight versions**. One of the seven ways to play had never been measured.

The policy now weighs what each option is worth at that moment: how much skin a camp can actually give back against how badly it is needed, what the best available upgrade is worth, and how little kit you are carrying. And then it still would not rest — **which turned out to be correct**. At near-full skin, healing is worth less than the alternatives, so the honest conclusion was not that the harness was broken but that **Flash was an empty rung**: 0.4 points against Headpoint over 800 runs.

So Flash was given a cost that bites what a camp is actually for: **no sharpening. The deck you brought is the deck you have.**

That is where it got interesting. Blocking sharpening made Flash **easier than the rung below it** — 43.3% against Headpoint's 33.8% — and dropping how much the policy valued sharpening moved Headpoint from 33.8% to 39.3% without touching the game at all. **Working on your deck at a camp measures worse than resting or going through the van.** The camp's most-used option is the wrong choice, and the old policy took it every single time. That is a game-balance finding rather than a harness one, and it is logged as `BAL-11` at P1 rather than patched over here.

The ladder now reads **65.6 / 50.0 / 54.4 / 36.8 / 46.4 / 28.8 / 22.0%** — Flash still sits above Headpoint, which `BAL-11` is what fixes.

A guard was written and immediately rewritten. Comparing measured completion between rungs flagged Second Go and Redpoint as identical, which is a false positive — two different mechanics landing inside the sampling error — and no threshold can separate that at any sample size worth running. It now compares the **cumulative mods** instead, which is deterministic: no two rungs may be mechanically identical, and every rung above the first must add something of its own. 118 checks.

### v8.2 — BAL-10: the routes catch up (2026-07-27)
`BAL-9` bought ten points with `BITE_BUMP`, a global multiplier on every hold in the game, and said plainly that the rest was structural. This is the structural half, and the first thing it does is **hand the global dial back**: `BITE_BUMP` is 1 again.

Laying the routes out act by act showed the problem in one line. **Mean hold count went 8.0 → 8.9 → 9.4 across the three acts — a 17% rise — while the mean grade went 2.5 → 6.0 → 7.6.** And since `BAL-8` established that `bumpFor` saturates at V6, the grade does nothing at all above act 1. So acts 2 and 3 were barely harder than act 1 in the one dimension `ROUTE-4` measured as dominant, and the whole difficulty curve was resting on numbers printed next to route names.

Nineteen routes rescaled: act 2 gains two holds each, act 3 gains three and a crux. Acts now read **8.0 / 10.9 / 12.2 holds** and **18% / 13% / 25% crux**. Act 1 is untouched, because act 1 is where you learn what a hold is.

Measured with the global dial back at 1: the ladder runs **69.5% at *Working It* to 19.0% at *Ground Up***, a 3.7× span, which is better than the stopgap managed and comes from the routes rather than from a multiplier. The campaign reads **48.8 / 60.8 / 62.8%** by pages read — a fourteen-point journal spread — at **26 minutes a run**, and Act 3 now does the killing it is supposed to at 24–38%.

One mistake, of a kind worth naming. The rescale was first written as a loop that **mutated the source inside a `finditer` over the original string**, so every edit after the first landed at a drifted offset — act 3's crux share came out at 50% and the file stopped parsing. Redone by collecting all matches first and applying them **back to front**, so earlier offsets stay valid.

A new guard: **each act must be more than a hold longer than the one before**, the last act must carry more cruxes than the first, and no route may be so long it cannot finish inside the daylight. 117 checks.

### v8.1 — BAL-9: the drift (2026-07-27)
The campaign had drifted from 30 / 42 / 49.6% at v7.7 to 56–68%, and the first job was finding out why. Toggling each recent feature said it was **none of them**: the van made no difference, the draft economy was worth two points, and turning the boons *off* made completion **higher**, at 68%.

That last number was the real finding, so boons and gear were measured against each other on the same route for the first time. **Gear ran +3 to +30 points of send rate. Boons ran +0 to +26 — and three of them did nothing at all.** Taking a boon was, on average, a punishment.

Two of the three were dead for reasons worth recording. **Crux Junkie** and **Second Wind** were simply too thin, and were given more. **Free Rein** measured +0 three separate times: first because the harness had **its own bonus-playing path that had never learned the rule**, which is `SIM-3` again in a different corner; and then, once the harness knew, because **most technique cards cost 0 pump**, so "the first one is free" was free of nothing. It now also draws, which is worth something to every deck. Free Rein +15, Second Wind +7, and the gap between the two kinds of find is closed.

Closing that gap pushed completion up to 72–82%, which made the underlying problem plain: **the whole ascent ladder had drifted up about thirty points** — *Working It* at **83.5%** where it read 52.5% at v7.2 — and the existing balance guard never fired because **every rung moved together and the band only watches one number**. `BITE_BUMP` from 1 to 2 brings the ladder back to **76% at the top and 14.5% at the bottom**, and the campaign to 65 / 68 / 76% by pages read, with the journal still worth eleven points.

A new guard watches the **span**: the easiest rung must complete under 88%, the hardest must be under two-fifths of it, and the hardest must still be above 3% so it is a difficulty rather than a wall. 116 checks.

Ten points of the thirty are closed. The rest is structural and logged as `BAL-10`: the card pool has been repriced, the loadouts rebuilt and the drafter fixed, and **the routes have not moved to match**. That wants a route-side pass, not another global multiplier.

### v8.0 — ROUTE-5: holds with names (2026-07-27)
Seven hold types across thirty-one routes meant every crimp behaved like every other crimp, and a route was something you remembered as a stat line. Ten routes now have **one named feature** each — the thing you would tell somebody about afterwards.

**The Rattler** on Chossmaster: a flake the size of a dinner plate that moves when you pull on it, and bites 2 harder for it. **The Death Block** on The Fridge, enormous and rounded and entirely without features, at +3 Grip. **The Guillotine** on The Priest — *people tape up for this one move*. **The Wet Jug** on Icebox Corner, which seeps, has always seeped, and which everybody knows about and nobody mentions. **The Thank God Hold** on Bergschrund, which is a gift at −2 Grip and which you do not know is there until your hand is already on it. Each behaves as its base type with the numbers moved and, where it earns one, its own ability. They read in red on the board under their own name, and the map tells you what is up there before you commit.

The placement had a bug that the check caught by counting: the signature was written **before** the cruxes, and the crux loop writes at random indices, so on two of the ten routes it was overwritten and the named hold never appeared. It is now placed afterwards and never onto a crux. A second false alarm was mine rather than the code's — the count read zero on two bosses because `holdDeck` is what *remains* after the opening board is dealt, and the named hold had simply been dealt out.

Three tests: **exactly one named hold per route that has one and none on a route that does not**, every signature real and used at most once and actually changing something rather than being a base hold with a name on it, and a named hold reading as itself while still stripping under a brush. 115 checks.

**One thing found and not fixed.** The campaign now measures **56–68%** where it read 30 / 42 / 49.6% at v7.7, and it is not the boons — with them off it is *higher*. The run has got easier somewhere across the last several versions and that wants a deliberate pass, not another feature on top of it. Logged as `BAL-9` at P0.

### v7.9 — BOON-2: the wild ones (2026-07-27)
Flagged when the first ten shipped: *"nothing here yet makes a run absurd, which was the point."* They were all sturdy and sensible and none of them changed how a turn feels. Four that do, each buying something large with a rule you then live under, and marked **BREAKS A RULE** rather than *changes* one on the find screen.

**Free Solo** — +1 Power on every move, and rests do nothing. **All In** — draw three more every turn, and everything left in your hand goes at the end of it. **Deadpointing** — every move is a dyno, twice the Power and half the Contact. **The Long Game** — twenty more turns of daylight, and every fall costs an extra skin. Against a 30% baseline they measure **46%, 52%, 38% and 29%**: three real gains and one that is a straight trade of safety for time.

Every one of those numbers is the second or third attempt. **Free Solo at +3 Power measured 83%** — a flat bonus on every move is worth more than losing every rest costs — and came down to +1. **The Long Game originally charged +1 Bite on everything and measured 0%**, which is the same lesson `MODE-2` and `ROUTE-4` both taught: a per-turn cost compounds out of all proportion. It charges skin on a fall instead, which is a run-level cost, and lands at neutral.

A fifth was cut. **Static** — *nothing you place ever comes off* — was measured five times across five different costs: **97%, 0%, 77%, 0% and 71%** against a 30% baseline. Never losing a card is worth more than any cost that still leaves a playable game, because it makes Contact meaningless and Contact is half the fight; removing Settle from it changed nothing at all for exactly that reason. A bounded version would only be a worse *Iron Fingers*, which already exists. Four that work is a better outcome than five with one I cannot balance.

Two tests, the useful one being that **a wild boon must cost something** — a boon that is pure upside is just a good boon, and the whole point of the set is the trade. 112 checks.

### v7.8 — FA-1c: she has an opinion (2026-07-27)
You name a line, you grade it, and until now **nobody said a word about it**. Grading is a social act — undergrading and overgrading are both claims about yourself — and there was a whole cast standing at the fire not mentioning it.

Marge does now, and differently for each. **Sandbagged:** *"Somebody came down off that saying it was V5. It is not V5."* — a pause — *"He used to do that too."* You can defend it, which costs psyche, or admit it, which restores some, and she tells you that getting it wrong on the low side is the only honest way to get it wrong. **Sprayed:** two people have been on it since and both came down laughing; it will get downgraded, they always do — *"The name will stick, though. The name always sticks."* **Fair:** *"That is what it is, near enough. You would be surprised how rare that is,"* and she hands you the cup without being asked, and writes the name in the back of something without showing you.

It jumps the queue when it fires, because it is topical and she has been waiting to say it, then steps back into her own thread. One fault caught while testing: it originally fired before you had ever met her, so **she referred to him before you knew who he was**. Gated behind her first conversation.

Six assertions, including that all three verdicts produce genuinely different words and that she never brings up the same line twice. 110 checks. `FA-1a`, `FA-1b` and `FA-1c` are all in: you find a line nobody has climbed, you name it and grade it, it turns up in later expeditions climbing at what it really is, and somebody who knew the last person to do this has something to say about how you did it.

### v7.7 — BAL-8: where the grade does its work (2026-07-27)
The finding from `FA-1b` was that `bumpFor` saturates at V6, so a V6 and a V10 carry the same difficulty bump and the number above the route is decoration. That was half right, and the wrong half was the important one.

Extending the curve above V6 — a single extra point of bump at V9 and above — was measured at **campaign completion falling from 33.5 / 42.0 / 54.5% to 20.4 / 34.0 / 40.0%**, with Act 3 deaths going from 52% to 64%. The bump is a **global multiplier on every hold of every route**, and the game has been balanced around it saturating for fifty versions. **The saturation is load-bearing, not an oversight.** Reverted, with a comment on it saying so and what it cost, because this is exactly the kind of thing that looks like a bug to the next person who reads it.

The actual defect was one line. `circuitRoute` sets its length to `5 + floor(grade * 0.7) + rng`, `skirmishRoute` to `5 + grade + rng` — and **`faRoute` set it to `7 + rng.int(3)`, ignoring the grade entirely.** So every first ascent shipped in v7.5 was the same length whether it was a V3 or a V10, which is precisely why two established lines three grades apart measured the same. Now scaled like the others: an act 1 line is 7–8 holds, an act 3 line 12–13.

The grade does its work through hold and crux counts, not through the bump. A test now pins that **all three generators scale their length with their grade**, so the one that did not cannot be the one that does not again. 108 checks, and the campaign is back to 30.0 / 42.0 / 49.6%.

### v7.6 — FA-1b: it stays in the book (2026-07-27)
A line you put up now turns up in later expeditions. It sits on the map beside The Priest and Chossmaster, named by you, marked **FA**, **reading at the grade you claimed and climbing at the grade it actually is** — so a line you sandbagged is quietly harder than its number and one you sprayed is a gift, and the map says which under its name: *stiffer than the grade you gave it*, *softer than you claimed, and everybody knows*.

The engineering was the whole risk and it drove the design. Established lines are **player-authored content living in the save**, and the act maps address routes by index into `ROUTES` — appending to that array is exactly what silently pointed the campaign finale at the tutorial boulder in v3.2. So nothing is appended. They get their own node type, are injected into a tier **at the end, never inserted**, because the forecast and every handler address a tier by position. The claim stores the line's full shape rather than a seed, so a line you established cannot change if the generator ever does. A test now asserts that establishing a line leaves `ROUTES.length` and the finale's index untouched.

Measuring it turned up something that is not about first ascents at all. Two established lines three grades apart came back within a point of each other, and the reason is that **`bumpFor` is `min(3, floor(grade / 2))`** — it saturates at V6. **Above V6 the grade is decoration**; only the hold and crux counts do any work, which is also why a V4 with 7 holds sent at 83% and a V4 with 8 holds at 39%. Logged as `BAL-8`, and it is the more important finding of the two.

Four tests, including an old save without the shape fields still producing a climbable line. 107 checks.

### v7.5 — FA-1a: your own line (2026-07-27)
The game is named for a man who found a line nobody knew about, and in fifty versions it has never let you do the same thing. Each act now holds **an unclimbed line**.

What makes it different from a climb with a naming prompt is that nothing about it is known. **The grade reads `?`** — not because you have not learned it but because nobody has been up it, and the number does exist underneath. Every hold carries **a season of dirt**, +2 Grip until it is brushed off, which finally gives `Brush`, `Wire Brush Pro` and `Perfect Beta` a job they have never had: `cleans` now strips the dirt as well as the ability, and on new rock that is worth doing before you try to pull.

Sending it does not go to a reward screen. It goes to **A FIRST ASCENT**: you name it, and you grade it. The name is yours — there is a generated suggestion in the placeholder if nothing comes — and the grade is a claim rather than a fact. The rock knows what it actually was, and the logbook records both under **LINES YOU PUT UP**: *fair*, *soft*, *sprayed*, or **sandbagged** — which is the title of the game appearing as a verdict on the player.

Three tests, and one caught a real fault: the act 3 generator produced a **V11**, above the ceiling the rest of the game uses. Also pinned that a known route can never come up dirty, and that brushing a clean hold does not make it easier. 103 checks.

`FA-1b` — established lines appearing in later expeditions at the grade you claimed — is where this becomes the best thing in the game, and it carries save-format risk, which is why this landed on its own first.

### v7.4 — LOG-2: the book pays (2026-07-27)
`STAT-2` gave the logbook every send, every best burn, every style and the conditions on the day — and rewarded **none of it**. A route you had sent five times played exactly like one you had never seen. The mechanic climbing already has for this is beta: **you remember a line you have done.**

A ticked line now starts with some of its sequence already known, one hold type per send and one more for having flashed it, capped at two. What it remembers is the **rarest hold on the line, not the commonest**, and that distinction was the whole balance of it: remembering the commonest hold was worth **+42 points of send rate** (12% → 54%), which would have made grinding known lines strictly better than climbing new ones. Even a single common hold was +25. Switched to the rare one and it lands at **+12** — worth having, never worth farming. It also reads better: on a crimp ladder what you remember is where the jug was.

Ticking **every line in an act** pays once — 120 XP and a rare card you then own permanently, so the guidebook turns into something you can hold. The logbook says so on each act header, counts your progress, and marks a reward as waiting until you collect it. Each route now also shows how many moves you remember on it.

Four tests, including the two that matter: **a procedural line may never draw on the book** (skirmish and Circuit routes have nothing to remember), and **an act pays once and only once**, with a partly-ticked act not counting. 100 checks.

### v7.3 — UX-9: cards that fit (2026-07-27)
Reported from a phone, which is the only place either of these was visible. Card names were cut off mid-word — *CRIMP GRI*, *OPEN HAN* — and rules text ran off the right edge, while the resolve preview (*+5 PUMP*) sat below the hold slot on top of the lane label.

The preview was simple: `bottom:-2px` put it **outside** the element it belonged to. Moved above the pips.

The cards were more interesting, because the text was not being clipped — it was being **covered**. The hand is a fan, each card overlapping the one before by 16–18px, and the cards were 84px wide with 6px of right padding. So the content strip was 71px while only 66px was ever visible: **every card in the hand hid its own right edge behind its neighbour**, by design, and nobody had measured it.

Fixed by measurement rather than by eye. Across all 217 card names, at the old geometry **24 names overflowed**; the fix had to widen the card without pushing the hand off the screen or the board past the viewport. Landed at **112px wide, 12px right padding, 8px overlap** — a 93px content strip inside 104px visible, which leaves exactly one name (*UNTOUCHABLE*) breaking mid-word. The cost is real and worth stating: you now see **3.4 cards of a five-card hand** instead of 5.3, and scroll for the rest. The vertical budget still fits at every text size (705 / 772 / 840px against 844).

Two tests: **a card may not hide its own content behind the next one**, and the inked border must be drawn at the width the card actually is — which it was not, and would have drifted again on the next change. **93 checks.**

### v7.2 — BAL-3, and eight ghosts (2026-07-27)
Auditing the to-do list against the changelog found that **eight of its twenty-one items had already shipped** — the journal, its later pages, skirmish, the second archetype, the new-player wall, cash and the trading post, the event engine. Thirty-eight per cent of the project's stated remaining work was fiction, for the same reason the stale `ART-1` rows were: **the changelog gets prepended to on every version and the to-do table only gets read when somebody looks**. Cleared, each with a note saying where it actually shipped. Thirteen items are genuinely open.

Hiding among the ghosts was a real bug logged at v2.2 and never fixed. `reachedBoss` in the harness fired on the **first boss node of any act**, so across three acts it read close to 100% and carried no information at all — which is why the per-act death split has been doing all the work in these notes. It now means the finale, and immediately says something worth knowing: at *Working It* **82% of runs reach The Lost Line and 52.5% send it**; at *Ground Up*, **20% reach it and 7% send it**. The ladder converts about 64% at the bottom and 35% at the top.

The corrected metric then exposed something else. **Headpoint and Flash return identical numbers — the same completion, the same reach, the same turn count.** The ladder is cumulative and correct, so that is not a game bug: *Flash* is the rung that makes camps give less back, and the harness's camp policy sharpens rather than rests whenever skin is healthy. **A rung that only affects resting is invisible to a player who never rests**, so one of the seven ascent styles has never been measured at all. Logged as `SIM-4`.

### v7.1 — ROPE-3: shrink, not expand (2026-07-27)
The item posed a choice — eight protection cards serving three roped routes out of thirty-one, so **either ropes expand or protection shrinks**. Expanding was tried first, because the alpine act is called *the wall* and having two of its ten lines roped is odd. Four more went multi-pitch, including the boss.

It measured badly in both directions. Campaign completion fell about six points with Act 3 deaths reaching 58%, and — the finding that decided it — **carrying a rack made a roped route dramatically worse, not better**: The Nose Direct went from 41% sendable to 5%, Bergschrund 27% to 1%. Three pieces of protection displace three climbing cards, and clipping only limits what a fall costs you. At **half a fall per burn** that is worth a hold or two. It is never worth three slots. Reverted.

Two real faults surfaced on the way and both are fixed. **69% of roped attempts ended in a "ground fall"** — the rope was modelled as starting from no protection at all, which is not how anybody climbs: you are tied to the belay before you leave it. The belay now counts as your first piece, and ground falls go to **zero**. And **clipping cost you a card**, which is not what a quick action is; every piece now draws to replace itself.

Protection cut from eight cards to three — a quickdraw, a wired nut and a bomber cam — because a pool should not carry eight cards for a mechanic worth a hold or two. Pool now 219. The honest residue is logged as `ROPE-4`: three routes and three cards is a defensible floor, but **the rope still needs a reason to exist**, and adding more of it is demonstrably not that reason.

### v7.0 — A11Y-3: reachable without a pointer (2026-07-27)
The audit was blunt: **35 interactive elements were plain divs** — not focusable, not announced, and impossible to operate without a pointer — and the interface contained **zero ARIA attributes of any kind**. No roles, no labels, no live regions, no focus styling.

All 35 now behave like buttons: a role, a tab stop, Enter and Space, and a visible focus ring. **The board describes itself** — *"left hand hold: crux, sharp crimp, 9 grip, 4 bite. 3 grip would remain"* — and so does your side of it, and every card in hand with its stats and rules text. The **pump meter** is a real meter with its value and maximum. The spotter is a polite live region; the outcome of a commit is an assertive one, so a turn's result is spoken rather than silently redrawn.

The conversion was done twice. The first pass used a regex to rewrite `onClick={...}` and **could not handle nested braces**, so it mangled two multi-line handlers into syntax errors — the same failure that killed `ENG-9`. Rather than patch forward a second time, it was reverted and redone with a **brace-matching parser** that walks each open tag tracking depth. One element was correctly left alone: the sheet's inner `stopPropagation` guard is not a control and should not be announced as one.

Two tests: **no element may be clickable without being focusable** (excluding that guard, by name), and **the game must announce itself** — a polite region, an assertive one, a labelled meter, and the three labels that matter most, checked by shape rather than by count so they cannot be quietly dropped. 91 checks.

### v6.9 — UX-8: back goes back (2026-07-27)
Auditing twenty-four screens for a way out turned up two real faults, and neither was the one the item described.

**BACK went to the menu from everywhere.** Every browsing screen called the same `toMenu`, so leaving the collection dropped you two levels and you had to walk back in through THE BOOKS to look at the logbook. There is now a hierarchy — a book backs out to THE BOOKS, THE BOOKS and BEFORE YOU GO back out to the menu, and the loadout backs out to BEFORE YOU GO where you opened it. The labels say so: **◂ BOOKS** rather than a generic BACK, so where you are going is readable before you press it.

**The Android back button closed the game.** On an installed PWA with no history entries, a back press leaves the page — which for `SHIP-2` means the app shuts. Each level now pushes a history entry and a `popstate` listener walks the same hierarchy, so the hardware button does what the on-screen one does. Run-end screens are deliberately excluded: a finished run returns to the menu rather than up a level, because there is nowhere up to go.

Two tests hold it: **every screen with a back control must have a parent, that parent must be a real screen, and following parents must terminate at the menu rather than loop** — and **the hardware back button must be handled at all**. The first caught a mistake made in the same turn: a blanket rename matched the beginning of *BACK TO THE GUIDEBOOK* and quietly put the run-end screens on the hierarchy, where they do not belong. 89 checks.

### v6.8 — NARR-6: the world stops repeating itself (2026-07-27)
A run draws about eight events from the twenty-two reachable in any given act, and nothing stopped it drawing the same one twice. Measured across 3,000 runs: **0.97 repeats per run, and 68% of runs contained one.** Deer ticks twice in one expedition does not read as bad luck, it reads as the world being small.

`rollEvent` now takes what you have already seen and draws from what is left, falling back to the whole range only if a run somehow exhausts it. **Repeats go to zero.** The list is run-scoped and saved with the run, so putting the game down mid-trip and coming back does not reset the world.

The useful part was a piece of my own dead code, caught by a test I wrote in the same turn. Alongside the repeat fix I added a filter for "dud" events — ones that only exist to hand you a journal page you already hold — and measured it as removing zero duds, which I nearly reported as a success. It removed zero because **it could never fire**: it required *every* branch of an event to give a page, and every page event has an alternative branch that gives something else. The filter was dead the moment it was written. Removed, and the test rewritten to assert what is actually true: **every page event must still be worth stopping for once its page is read**, which all four are.

Three tests. 87 checks.

### ART-3 — the store art (2026-07-27)
Two **stale `ART-1` rows** were still sitting in the to-do table. That work shipped in **v1.8** — paper texture, hand-inked borders, the topo line — and `ART-2` followed in v2.0 with the hold glyphs and the wordmark. The rows were never moved out. Removed, and worth noting as the kind of drift a roadmap accumulates when only the changelog gets attention.

What was actually open is `ART-3`, and it is now drawn: a **1024×500 Play Store feature graphic** and a **630×500 itch cover**, both made with the same ink the game draws itself with — the same seeded jitter on the borders and the wordmark, the same topo line with three holds worked and two to go, the same red X at the top. Nothing is illustrated by hand, so the store art and the game cannot drift apart.

**Screenshots are deliberately not included.** They have to be captured from the real build on a real device; rendering convincing mock-ups and putting them on a store listing would be a lie about what the game looks like. That part waits for `SHIP-1`.

### v6.7 — META-5: where kit comes from (2026-07-27)
Measuring first changed the item. The audit said a run only ever meets two of the twenty-two gear and boons; in fact a run **sees 6.9 of them (31%)** and nine runs meets all twenty-two. Seeing was never the problem. **Taking** was: a thirty-minute run ended carrying **2.2 items**, because there were only two places in the whole campaign where a find could happen.

Three new sources, each priced so it is a decision rather than a handout. **The van** is a fourth option at a camp — kit and beta instead of the rest you came for, and you do not get both. **A trading post** now sometimes has somebody selling beta as well as kit, at $110 against gear's $70. And **four events teach you something**: the old timer draws the sequence in the dirt, a rest day spent watching, beta written inside a chalk bag in biro, a hangboard session done his way rather than yours.

Measured against a player who actually wants kit: **items held at the end of a run goes 2.2 → 3.0**, at a completion cost of between −3.7 and +0.7 across the four climbers. Roughly break-even, which is exactly right for an optional trade — the van costs you a camp, and a camp is worth about what a piece of kit is worth.

Four tests, including the two that matter: **the van must cost you the rest** (it advances the tier, opens a find, and leaves your skin alone) and **nothing may ever hand you a boon you already hold** — checked against both the event path and the offer roll. 85 checks.

### v6.6 — a check that fails (2026-07-27)
