`ENG-9` — pulling the content tables out of `engine.ts` — was attempted and **reverted**. The structure was right and briefly existed: `types.ts` at 103 lines, `content.ts` at 611, `engine.ts` down to 2,154. What it fell over on was mechanical: moving the tables meant rewriting three import lists by regex, and each pass produced a new crop of missing or unused names — `Piles`, `MapNode`, `FeetKey`, `StyleKey` — until the lists were mangled beyond the point where fixing forward was safer than starting again. Reverted to the verified v6.5 source, which is green: **83 tests, 355KB, unchanged**.

The more useful thing is *why it went unnoticed for several steps*. The verification habit used throughout this project is `npx tsc --noEmit 2>&1 | head -3 && echo "TSC CLEAN"` — and **`head` exits 0 whatever `tsc` does**, so "TSC CLEAN" printed over a file full of errors. A check that cannot fail is not a check.

So: **`npm run check`** — typecheck, then both suites, as one command whose exit code means something. **`npm run ship`** runs that and then builds. Negative-tested by giving `PUMP_MAX` a wrong type, which the old pattern would have waved through and this one refuses. The README now points at it instead of at `npm test`.

`ENG-9` stays open, and the note on it now says how to do it: one section at a time with `npm run check` between each, rather than in a single pass.

### v6.5 — ENG-8: engine and screens (2026-07-27)
One file had grown to 4,863 lines, 80 state fields and 26 screens. The rule that the whole game lives in one file was right at a thousand lines and had started costing edit safety: **three of the bugs this project shipped were state-shape mistakes** — mutators never reaching `newRun`, a phase the harness did not know existed, a name exported from a barrel that did not exist — and all three were "this file is too big to see at once" problems.

Split at the natural seam. **`src/engine.ts`** holds the types, the dials, every content table and every rule. **`src/App.tsx`** holds the CSS, the ink, the sound and the screens, and imports 120 names from the engine. The build still inlines everything into one HTML file, and it is **exactly the same 355KB** — nothing about the shipped game changed.

The interesting part was what the split *found*, because a boundary only means something if it is enforced. Three things were on the wrong side of it. **`roughPath`** — deterministic border geometry, pure and tested — was sitting in the UI; it moved to the engine. **`familyOf`**, which decides what kind of thing a card is, was in the UI too, and the barrel-integrity test from `UX-7` caught it within seconds. And the **Web Audio layer** was in the engine while reaching for `window`, which is exactly the sort of thing that makes a boundary decorative; it moved to the screens.

Two tests now hold the line: **the engine may not contain JSX, a React import, a hook, `document` or `window`**, and **the screens may not define `resolve`, `startBurn`, `endSession`, `buildRoute` or `cardValue`**. 83 checks in total, all passing, with campaign balance unmoved.

### v6.4 — TEST-2: the other sixty-one (2026-07-27)
The suite covered the features and nothing underneath them: 61 of 117 engine exports had no test at all — the constants, the curves, the tables and the small pure functions everything else stands on. **`sim/test-core.mjs`** is 29 checks over exactly those, and `npm test` now runs both files: **81 checks, zero untested exports.**

What to assert was chosen rather than generated. Each one is either a rule this project has stated and never verified, or a place a bug has actually come from. **No condition may touch Power** — a rule since v0.7, never once checked, and the reason is that a weather which moved Power swung a battle 69 points and had to be reverted. **The ascent ladder must only get harder**, rung by rung. **A level-up may never hand you a curse or a beta card.** **An event outcome can never push skin, cash or psyche out of range**, tested against every choice of every one of the 32 events at both ends of each resource. **Procedural routes must always be climbable** — never more cruxes than holds, which is the exact bug `ROUTE-4` shipped. **Sharpening must improve a card and be idempotent.** **The rarity buckets must partition the pool**, so no card can be in two or none.

They passed first time, which is not evidence of anything, so two were **negative-tested**: injecting `dPower` into crisp weather failed the conditions check, and making `upgrade` non-idempotent produced *Crimp Grip++* and failed the sharpening check. They have teeth.

One test was written against a function I had assumed rather than read — `synth` takes a power and a contact, not a card shape — which is a small illustration of why the coverage gap mattered: **nothing had ever called it from outside the engine.**

### v6.3 — NARR-7: what you have not read (2026-07-27)
The v0 design baseline promised that the vanished first ascensionist's journal pages were lore **and** the finale's beta — that *exploration literally makes the finale sendable*. That was true when six pages were worth about 16 points of completion. After the `CARD-5` reprice it was worth **nothing**: 54.0 / 54.5 / 52.5% across zero, three and six pages. Better decks had made The Lost Line winnable without ever opening the journal, and the tie between the story and the climbing had quietly come undone.

Beta cards alone were never going to hold that up, because a card is worth a fixed amount and the deck around it kept getting better. So the pages now do the thing they were always described as doing: **what you have not read, you cannot climb.** Every hold on The Lost Line — and every crux — carries **+1 Grip for each page you are missing**. With nothing, the route is +6 Grip across the board and reads exactly as its own note describes it: no chalk, no tick marks, no trail. With all six, it is the route he actually climbed.

The finale in isolation goes from **0.5% sendable at no pages to 13.3% at six**, average Grip falling 15.8 to 9.8. In the full campaign, completion goes from flat to **29.6 / 43.6 / 52.0%**, and Act 3 deaths fall **56% → 42% → 34%** as you read. The map now says so plainly on the finale node, so the connection is visible before you commit rather than discovered afterwards.

Two tests: the unread route must be materially harder and the penalty must scale with pages, and — the one that matters more — **no other route in the game may change with the journal at all**. 50 checks.

### v6.2 — BAL-7: the spread (2026-07-27)
The item was titled "the Trad Dad", who had been last in every measurement since v2.2. Measured properly at n=600 he was **third**, and the real problem was much larger: **Comp Kid 3.3% against Alpinist 29.8%, a nine-fold spread**, which nothing had been watching.

The cause was not the signatures. It was that **every one of the four starting decks contained zero or one technique cards**, against the optimum of two to four in fifteen that `CARD-5` had measured the turn before. All four were rebuilt to a consistent shape — 3–4 foot moves, 3 rests, 3 techniques — which alone moved the Trad Dad from 15.3% to **42.6%**, overshooting in the other direction.

Two rounds of tuning after that, and the decisive one confirms a rule for the third time. Easing his Contact bonus from +2 to +1 barely moved him. Charging him a skin did nothing measurable — he dies to skin only 10% of the time, so life total was never his constraint. What worked was making his stated cost real: *Bomber* said "nothing settles past +1" while a high-Contact deck survives without ever needing to settle. Set to **nothing settles at all**, and he fell 39.2% → 17.2%. **Settle remains the most explosive stat in the game** — a single point of it is worth about 22 points of completion.

Final spread: **Boulderer 21.4 · Comp Kid 15.2 · Trad Dad 17.2 · Alpinist 23.0** — 1.5× rather than 9×. A standing test now fails if any climber drops below 5% or the spread exceeds 2.2×, because this went unnoticed for four versions purely because nobody was looking.

One regression found and logged rather than chased: **the journal no longer matters.** Six pages were worth ~16 points of completion; they are now worth nothing (54.0 / 54.5 / 52.5%). Better decks made the finale winnable without beta cards, so the exploration incentive has quietly gone flat — filed as `NARR-7`. **50 checks.**

### v6.1 — CARD-5: the dead quarter (2026-07-27)
The audit said 60 of 221 cards would never be taken. The first question was whether they were weak or merely mispriced, and the answer was almost entirely the second.

**Keywords were not the problem** — of 54 cards carrying one, *zero* read as dead. The dead pool was 48 plain technique cards: Chalk Up, Breathe, Brush, Tape Job. So the question became whether a technique card earns a deck slot at all, which is measurable rather than arguable. **A deck of fifteen moves sends 46.4% on a mid Act 1 boulder. Swap two of them for technique cards and it sends 88.0%.** Individually, **Breathe alone is worth +28 points of send rate**, and `cardValue` was scoring it 1.0 against a plain move's 10.3.

They were being valued at roughly a fifth of their worth. Repriced with a weight of 3.2 — and, because the same measurement showed nine moves and six techniques falling back to 62%, with a **saturation term**: full value up to about a fifth of the deck, then away sharply. Breathe reads 7.2 in a stock deck and **0.4** in a technique-heavy one, which is the shape the data actually has.

Four cards were genuinely filler rather than mispriced — *Tape Job*, *Approach Shoes*, *Headtorch* and *One More Go*, each a single point of a single effect — and were given a second point. **The unplayable share of the pool falls from 27% to 7%**, and what remains is correctly situational: four sequence cards, which are deck-specific by design, and four pieces of protection, which are supposed to be worthless on a boulder.

Two regressions on the way, both mine: scaling the **cost** penalty by the same weight as the benefits pushed a sequence card to −2.6, and a test asserting protection is worthless on rock was written with a definition of "pure clip" that excluded the shed-1 pieces which are, in fact, weak rests. Three tests added, including a standing guard that **no more than 14% of the pool may value below the line**, so this cannot drift back unnoticed. **49 checks.**

### v6.0 — SIM-3: the drafter could not see (2026-07-27)
An audit of the pool, the code and the test coverage turned up a fault in the measuring instrument itself. **`cardValue` scored all four sequence cards at −3.0** — `bonusValue` could see their cost and nothing else, because it had never been taught what `seq` means. The drafter therefore never took one, which means **every number reported for `ENG-7` measured a game in which sequences effectively did not exist.** It also priced protection identically on a boulder and on a rope, and ignored boons entirely.

A sequence is now valued as its payout discounted by the odds of holding it — and, more usefully, by whether *this deck* can meet the condition at all. **Trust The Feet** reads 1.2 in a stock deck and **4.9** in a feet-heavy one; **Find The Rest** reads 0.0 and **1.4** in a rest deck. Protection reads **−0.4 on rock and 5.6 on a rope**. Boons shift what cards are worth: Kneebar Merchant makes a rest worth 2 more, Big Hands makes a foot move worth more, Free Rein refunds a technique card's cost.

The correction is large and worth stating plainly. The **loadout builder** uses the same valuation, so it had been assembling decks with a blind one. Built-loadout campaign completion moves from **40.7 / 51.3 / 50.0% to 67.0 / 74.5 / 69.5%** — roughly 25 points, and every built-loadout figure quoted before this version was understated. Archetype numbers barely moved (18.7 / 11.0 / 10.7 / 26.3%), which makes sense: their loadouts are fixed, so only in-run picks improved.

Five tests pin it, including the exact blindness it had: **a sequence card must be worth more than its cost**, and **a sequence must be worth more to a deck that can hold it**. One test was written wrong first — it asserted all protection is worthless on rock, but *Screamer* sheds 2 and draws 1 and earns its slot either way, so the assertion now applies only to cards whose sole job is clipping. **46 checks.**

### v5.9 — ENG-7: sequences (2026-07-27)
A turn was: place three, commit. A **sequence** is a plan held across several turns that pays if you can protect it and is lost the moment you cannot — the first thing in the game that makes a turn about more than the turn.

Four of them, each started by its own card. **Linked Moves** wants a hold worked every turn for three turns and then hands you two holds free. **Static Sequence** wants two turns without resting and then draws three. **Breathing** wants two rests running and then dumps your pump entirely. **Committed** wants something on your feet for three turns and then gives everything on the board 2 Contact and a settle. One at a time; starting another replaces it.

The tension is that the conditions **fight the rest of the game**. Breathing asks you to rest twice while the clock runs. Static asks you not to rest at the exact moment you most want to. Linked Moves is impossible on a hold you cannot break, so committing to it is a bet on the next three turns of the route rather than on your hand. It shows on the climb screen while it is live, with what it needs and how many turns are left, so abandoning one is a decision rather than an accident.

Three tests: **the condition table is exact** (all eight cases pinned, because a plan that silently mis-reads its own condition is worse than no plan), every sequence is startable by a real card and pays something, and a broken sequence is dropped rather than carried. **41 checks**, and campaign balance is unmoved at 19.2 / 25.2%.

### v5.8 — MODE-2: make it harder (2026-07-27)
Eight stackable modifiers, chosen in BEFORE YOU GO, each paying a percentage of XP: **Greasy** (+25%, the conditions are never good), **The Sharp End** (+30%, every move has one less Contact), **No Shakes** (+40%, rests shed nothing), **Two Goes** (+50%, two burns instead of three), **Light Rack** (+40%, no gear to start), **Sandbagged** (+35%, every grade in the book is a lie), **Chossy** (+30%, four curses before you touch the rock) and **Dry Spell** (+30%, camps do nothing for your skin). The ascent ladder makes the game harder in a fixed order; this lets you choose which way.

Every price started as a guess and was replaced by a measurement, because **six of the eight were wrong**. Two were lethal: *The Sharp End* as "every hold is Sharp" and *One Go* as a single burn both produced **0% completion**, and capping the sharp penalty at once per turn barely helped — which is the same lesson `ROUTE-4` taught about `dTax`, now confirmed a third time. **Any per-turn cost compounds out of all proportion to its size.** The Sharp End was rebuilt as a deck-wide Contact penalty, a dial with a known magnitude, and came in at a sensible 5–12%.

The other two were silent. *Light Rack* and *Chossy* reported **numbers identical to each other and to the baseline**, which is what gave them away: both act inside `newRun`, and `newRun` builds from `freshRun`, which has no mutators on it yet — so **both had been doing nothing at all**. `newRun` now takes the mutators as an argument. Once they worked, Light Rack turned out to cost the Alpinist its entire run (27% → 0%) and was repriced from +10% to +40%.

Four tests, one written specifically for that trap: **run-time mutators must actually reach the run**, plus every mutator changing something and paying for it, no mutators changing nothing, and stacking raising the payout. **37 checks.**

### v5.7 — RUN-6: the draft economy (2026-07-27)
A card reward was three cards, take one. It now has prices on it. **REROLL** buys three different cards, starting at $25 and rising $15 each time within the same reward, so leaning on it gets expensive fast. **BETTER SHELF** buys the same three slots drawn a tier up, at a flat $45 — verified to actually be better rather than merely different. **TAKE TWO** hands you two of the three and puts a curse in your deck with them, which is the only one that costs something other than money.

The point is not the options themselves but what they do to cash. Until now cash had exactly one use, the trading post. Measured with the drafter told to spend when the shelf is poor, completion moved **+2.8, −1.6, +3.2 and +4.4** across the four climbers — modest and not uniformly positive — while **shop visits fell from 1.0 to 0.6 a run.** That is the interesting number: the two uses now compete, and buying a better card in the moment means walking past the van later.

Four tests: reroll costs must rise and never reach zero, **you cannot spend cash you do not have** (all three options no-op when broke, without charging), taking two brings exactly one curse, and the upgraded shelf is genuinely a better draw than a plain reroll. **33 checks.**

### v5.6 — ROUTE-4: which way up (2026-07-27)
Every climb was a difficulty number you accepted. Now, before you pull on, you pick a line: **as it goes** (what the book says), **the direct** (two fewer holds, three more cruxes), or **the traverse** (one more hold, two fewer cruxes, a longer day). The finale and the tutorial are authored and never vary.

Balancing this turned up the two most useful numbers of the session. **Hold count dominates everything else** — the first direct, at three fewer holds, nearly doubled completion to 26–38% while two extra cruxes did nothing to pay for it. And **`dTax` is the single most explosive dial in the game**: adding one point of it to that same line took it from 38% to **0%**, and the first traverse — three more holds *and* a tax — died with 93–100% skin losses without a single send. A per-turn cost compounds across an entire climb in a way a one-off does not.

Settled without any tax at all, on hold and crux counts alone: **guidebook 7–21%, direct 10–20%, traverse 7.5–23.5%**, with distinct shapes — the direct runs about 100 turns, the traverse about 175. Close enough that which one is right depends on the deck you are holding, which was the whole point.

Two tests added, one of which immediately earned itself: **a line must never produce an unclimbable route.** On *Mossback*, a short Act 1 boulder, the direct generated **more cruxes than there were holds**. The crux count is now capped below the hold count. The other test pins that authored routes are never varied. **29 checks.**

### v5.5 — BOON-1: things that change the rules (2026-07-27)
Gear gives you numbers. **A boon changes a rule**, and until now the game had nothing that did. Ten of them: **Second Wind** sheds 2 every third hold you work · **Free Rein** makes the first technique card each turn free · **Crux Junkie** draws when you work a crux · **Kneebar Merchant** makes a rest chip Grip as well · **Big Hands** doubles what your feet give · **Momentum** stops resting breaking your flow · **Glued On** settles everything twice as fast · **Iron Fingers** saves the first card that would blow each burn · **Weightless** removes the campusing tax · **Send Train** carries 3 beta into the next boulder.

They are found where gear is found — the act-boss reward now offers **three finds, gear and boons mixed and shuffled**, with a boon always among them. Taking the rule-breaker means going without the shoes, which is the decision worth having. Measured either way: gear-first completes 6–19%, boons-first 7–20%. Comparable, as two things competing for one slot should be.

**One mistake worth recording in full.** Implementing Big Hands as *"Support reaches both hands"* required the default to be that it reached only one — so the first version **quietly weakened the core rules to make room for the boon**, and campaign completion halved from 17–20% to 3–8% before the sim caught it. A boon must add to the rules and never subtract from the default. Big Hands now simply doubles Support, and the suite has a test that **`boonMods([])` returns nothing set at all**, so the next attempt to buy a boon with a nerf fails loudly.

Three boon tests added — every boon reachable in the offers and actually doing something, no rules changed when you hold none, and a boon neither stacking nor displacing gear. **27 checks.**

### v5.4 — SHIP-1: ready to push (2026-07-27)
`RCJLabs/Sandbagged` exists, is public, and is empty. The repo is now shaped so that a push is a deployment.

`npm run build:html` writes the PWA shell into **`docs/`** as well as `dist/`, because GitHub Pages can serve `/docs` from the default branch — so the source stays at the root, the built site lives in the repo, and shipping is a commit rather than a separate step. A `.nojekyll` goes with it so Pages does not run the file through Jekyll. Added a README, a `.gitignore` for `node_modules`, `dist` and the harness's temporary bundles, and the repository and homepage fields in `package.json`.

**One correction worth recording, because it has been wrong for thirty versions.** The repository is `Sandbagged` with a capital S, and GitHub Pages project paths are **case-sensitive**. Every URL quoted in this changelog before now — `rcjlabs.github.io/sandbagged/` — would have returned a 404. The live address is **`https://rcjlabs.github.io/Sandbagged/`**. The relative paths in the manifest, the service worker registration and its scope are all `./`, so they resolve correctly under the subpath without any change.

### v5.3 — UX-7: take it back (2026-07-27)
**TAKE IT BACK** undoes a placement, up to three deep, so a card dropped into the wrong lane costs nothing. The commit button also gained a quieter neighbour: *bail off this one* moved out of the button row into a text link beside the burn counter, because a mis-tap on BAIL used to end the whole climb.

The interesting part was deciding what may be taken back. A snapshot restore is trivial — placements are pure, they move a card between hand and board and consume no randomness — but **undoing a card that draws would let you look at your deck and then change your mind.** So the stack closes the moment anything reveals hidden information: playing a technique card, or committing. Undo is for misclicks, not for peeking.

Two tests pin the property that makes it safe: **a placement must not touch the run seed** — if it rolled anything, taking it back would desync the run from its seed and break `UX-5` — and **the previous state must not have been mutated**, since undo restores an object rather than replaying anything.

A third test came out of a mistake this turn. `pileFromHand` was listed in the harness's export barrel but never exported from the engine, and because **`tsc` does not cover `sim/`**, the name silently resolved to `undefined` and only failed at runtime inside a test. There is now a check that every name the barrel re-exports actually exists. 24 checks.

### v5.2 — STAT-2: every trip (2026-07-27)
**EVERY TRIP** keeps the last twenty runs: which climber, on a toprope or leading, how far you got, how many boulders you sent, how big the deck was, and what actually ended it — *skin ran out*, *lost the psyche*, *beaten by The Priest*, *topped out*. Circuit runs are in there too, recorded by score.

Each row carries **the seed, and tapping it copies it** into the seed field, so a run that went badly or interestingly can be climbed again exactly as it was. That is `UX-5` finally earning its keep: the seeding has been there since v0.6, and this is the first screen that makes it useful rather than merely possible.

The recording lives in the **phase graph**, not at the five call sites that can end a run, because that seam has now drifted from the harness three times and the fourth would have been a run that quietly finished without being written down. Finding them was worth doing carefully — one exit, failing the boss or running out of map, was missed on the first pass and caught by grepping for the phase rather than by trusting the edit.

Two tests added: **every way a run can end writes exactly one record**, walked over sixty full expeditions with more than one kind of ending observed, and **the history caps at twenty, newest first**. 21 checks now.

### v5.1 — BAL-4: the rope and the daylight (2026-07-27)
`BAL-4` was written at v1.x and said a new player completes the campaign 0.5% of the time. Re-measured against the game as it now stands — with gear, sharpening, trading posts, psyche and a competent drafter — **a genuine first expedition wins 4–14%**. The item was largely stale. But measuring it properly turned up two faults that were not stale at all.

**Nothing capped how long a burn could last.** A deck with enough rests in it can shed faster than the clock ticks, so a climb could simply never end — the Alpinist averaged **457 turns**, about ninety minutes, on a single expedition. There is now a **daylight cap**: at thirty turns the light goes and you come down. Thematically it is the limiter the v0 design baseline named and never built, and it brought the Alpinist to 198 turns.

**Half of first expeditions ended in the forest** — 20–58% cleared Act 1, which is a poor first hour. Rather than tuning difficulty behind the player's back, this is explicit: **you can climb on a toprope.** +3 skin and every hold bites one less, switchable from BEFORE YOU GO, shown on the map while you climb, and clearly labelled as not counting toward the ascent-style ladder. You toprope before you lead. First expeditions now clear Act 1 **54–87%** and win 16–59%, while lead numbers are untouched at 9–21%.

One trap caught on the way, and it is the same family as the last three: `newRun` inherits its defaults from `freshRun`, so the moment toprope defaulted to on, **every balance harness silently began measuring the beginner mode**. The harness now sets the real difficulty explicitly, with `TOPROPE=1` to measure the other path.

### v5.0 — TEST-1: npm test (2026-07-27)
Fifteen verification harnesses were written across this project, each proving something real, and every one was deleted the moment it printed a number. **They are now twenty kept checks behind `npm test`**, running in about eight seconds: preview accuracy against the engine, pile accounting, save round-trip and migration and refusal, seed determinism, boss-phase telegraphs, card families, act-map integrity, event range filtering, conversation reachability, the ending gate, and four static checks on the source rules this project has broken before — no `Math.random`, no unicode escapes in JSX, every CSS font size scaling with `--fs`, a migratable save version. `npm test -- slow` adds a campaign balance tripwire.

**It earned itself on the first run.** The balance guardrail reported campaign completion at **0%**, which was not a false alarm: `NARR-5` had routed the finale to a new `epilogue` phase the previous turn, and the harness's loop did not know that phase existed, so **every campaign win had been reading as a loss**. Exactly the `SIM-1` failure again — the phase graph lives in the engine, but the sim still enumerates phases. Fixed properly, with the choice at the top extracted as `endingStep` so both the game and the harness call the same function. Campaign completion reads 40.7 / 51.3 / 50.0% again.

Two smaller things it found on itself. The `Math.random` check first tripped on the **comments describing the rule**, so the source checks now strip comments before matching — a rule written in prose must not fail a check about code. And the suite was negative-tested by deliberately breaking the jug-sheds-on-clear rule in the preview: the preview check failed, as it should, so these have teeth rather than being decorative.

### v4.9 — NARR-5: the top (2026-07-27)
Beating The Lost Line — three acts, half an hour, six journal pages, Marge finally telling you which drainage — used to produce **one sentence**. The story had a beginning and a middle and then stopped.

Topping out the finale no longer goes to a result screen. It goes to **THE TOP**: a cairn of six stones with the top one lichened over, and under it a freezer bag gone brittle holding one folded page. **He did it.** Thirty years ago, alone, in the same nine-day weather window you just used, and he told nobody — not the guidebook, not the locals, not Marge. Page seven is his last entry, written up there, explaining why.

Then the game asks you one question, which is the right shape for an ending in a game about ethics and style: **write your name under his, or put the stone back.** Tell the book and people will come; leave it and two people have stood up there and neither said anything. The run-end text follows whichever you chose, and the choice persists.

**Marge's thread finally lands.** Two more conversations unlock once you have page seven — *"Thirty years I have been angry at a man for dying on something he had already climbed"* — and the last of them is the only place the naming gets settled. Verified by walking the whole cast across three acts and a second run: **all 17 conversations reachable, Marge's seven in order, and the final two correctly locked until you have been to the top.**

### v4.8 — UX-6: a menu that asks one question (2026-07-27)
The main menu had grown a control per feature for twenty-five versions and ended up with **twenty of them stacked on one screen** — climber picker, ascent picker, seed field, five destinations, six toggles and a practice list, all above the fold before you had decided what you were doing. It also had the order backwards: setup first, play last.

Rebuilt around one question. **CLIMB** comes first — The Lost Line as the primary action, with The FA and The Circuit beside it — and the Lost Line button carries its own state, so a run in progress reads *CONTINUE · Act 2 · stage 3/7 · skin 6 · psyche 2* instead of needing a separate line. Everything else goes behind one of two doors: **BEFORE YOU GO** (climber, ascent style, loadout, seed) and **THE BOOKS & SETTINGS** (collection, logbook, journal, numbers, glossary, saves, the six toggles, practice). The prepare button shows your current pick — *THE BOULDERER · WORKING IT* — so the setup you would have scrolled past is now a label you can read at a glance.

**Twenty controls to six, 720px to 410px** — the menu now fits half a phone screen instead of overflowing one. Nothing was removed; two screens were added and the first one stopped trying to be all of them.

### v4.7 — CARD-4: family marks (2026-07-27)
227 cards rendered identically — a hand was seven rectangles you had to read one at a time. Every card now carries a small ink mark in its top corner saying what kind of thing it is: **a grip over an edge** for a hand move, **a boot** for a foot move, **a droplet** for a rest, **a head** for a technique, **a carabiner** for protection, **a page** for beta, **a crack** for a curse. Same ink, same stroke weight as the hold glyphs from `ART-2`, and shape rather than colour, per the `A11Y-1` lesson.

Family is derived from what a card *does* rather than declared per card, in a fixed priority: curse, then beta, then anything that clips, then anything that sheds, then bonuses, then the feet lane, then everything else is a hand move. Verified across the whole pool: **227 cards, none unmarked, no ambiguity** — 106 hand, 36 rest, 31 technique, 30 feet, 10 curse, 8 protection, 6 beta.

Drawn at the real on-card size (15px) before shipping, not just at legend size, because a mark that only reads at 24px would be decoration. **HOW IT WORKS** gained a legend section explaining each one, so the marks are learnable rather than guessable — protection in particular needed it, since it does nothing at all on a boulder.

### v4.6 — PERF-1: measured, then fixed (2026-07-27)
The whole art layer went in without a single measurement. This is the measurement, and then the two things it found.

**Baseline.** 333KB, of which 332KB is the inlined JS — the game's own content is about 50KB of it (32KB of card table, 17KB of routes and events), so the rest is React and the engine. A climb screen renders **11 inked components, 22 paths, 168 quadratic segments**, and the resolve preview was running **15 times per render**.

**Finding one: the inked paths were rebuilt every frame.** ~3.7KB of path strings regenerated on every render, for paths that are pure functions of `(w, h, seed, amp, segs)` and therefore never change. Cached in a bounded map: **0.0851ms → 0.0052ms, 16.4× faster**, and verified byte-identical to freshly built output so the borders cannot drift.

**Finding two: the preview was recomputed fifteen times.** Six direct calls from the two boards, nine more inside `previewPump` recounting lanes for the unanswered and crux tallies. Now computed **once per render** in a `useMemo` and passed in: **0.097ms → 0.026ms, 3.7× faster.**

Also: board slots were being **remounted on every commit** to replay their CSS animation. With motion off there is nothing to replay, so the key is now stable and the remount is skipped entirely — which pairs with `A11Y-2` rather than duplicating it.

Total saved: **~0.15ms per render**, which is small in absolute terms and honest to say so. What I cannot measure from here is frame time on a real device — logged as `PERF-2`, along with the more interesting question the baseline raised: React is most of the bundle, and whether that matters on a mid-range phone is still unknown.

### v4.5 — A11Y-2: motion and text size (2026-07-27)
Shape-coding fixed colour in v3.1; this is the rest of it. **Motion** can be switched off — every shake, pop, tear and lift — and it also honours `prefers-reduced-motion` from the OS without being asked, which is the setting most people have already made elsewhere. **Text** comes in three sizes, Normal, Large and Larger, taking the smallest rules text from **8px to 10.4px**.

The scaling is one CSS variable rather than a second stylesheet: all 19 font sizes in the sheet were rewritten to `calc(Npx * var(--fs))` in a single pass, so nothing can drift out of step later. The two fixed-height surfaces — board slots and hand cards — grow with it, but **sub-proportionally on purpose**.

That coefficient was the actual work, and it was wrong first time. At full proportional growth the climb screen came to **868px against a 844px viewport** — it would still have scrolled, but you would have lost sight of both board rows at once, which is the entire point of that screen. Trimmed from 130 to 95 and re-measured: **705 / 772 / 840px** across the three sizes, all inside the viewport. Horizontally the hand fan reaches 408px against 366 available at the largest setting, which is fine — the hand has been a horizontal scroller since v0.8.

### v4.4 — SHIP-2: installable, offline (2026-07-27)
It installs to a home screen and runs with the plane on. The build now emits a **manifest** (standalone, portrait, paper-coloured theme and splash), a **versioned service worker**, and **two icons drawn in code** with the same jitter maths as the game's ink — a hand-inked border, a topo line with three holds worked and two to go, a red X at the top, and the wordmark. Composed inside the middle 80% so it survives maskable cropping on Android.

One caveat stated plainly, because it breaks a rule the project has held since v0.6: **a service worker must be a real file at its own scope and cannot be inlined**, so this is the first build that emits more than one artifact. `index.html` is still fully self-contained and still works opened on its own — the PWA files are additive, not required. The icons are the second deliberate exception: an OS icon has to be an image, the same reasoning that moved illustration to `ART-3`.

The worker is **cache-first and versioned to `package.json`**, so a new build drops the old cache on activate rather than serving stale HTML forever — the classic PWA trap. Navigation falls back to the cached shell when offline. Verified: `sw.js` parses under `node --check`, the manifest is valid with three icon entries including a maskable, and the head carries the manifest link, apple-touch-icon, status-bar meta and theme colour.

Ships as **`sandbagged-pwa.zip`** — 288KB, five files, zero external requests. Drop it on Pages and it is installable as-is.

### v4.3 — UX-5: seeds (2026-07-27)
Every roll in the game has gone through the seeded run RNG since v0.6, specifically so this would be possible one day. The one thing missing was that **the live seed advances as you play**, so the value on screen mid-run was never the one that produced it — a run is only repeatable if the *starting* seed is recorded. It is now, as `runSeed`, saved with the run.

Seeds surface as short base-36 codes — **at most seven characters**, `9IX` or `1Z141Z3` — shown on the menu, on the map, and on the run-end screen with *same seed, same run*. Tap to copy. A field on the menu takes a pasted code and uses it for the next expedition or Circuit; leave it blank and you get a fresh line. Input is forgiving: `"  7k3-n9q "` parses, whitespace and punctuation stripped.

Verified over 20,000 random seeds: **zero round-trip failures**. And verified that a seed actually determines a run — the same code reproduces the same **forecast, hold deck, opening hand and starting gear**, while a neighbouring seed differs. Which also means a seed is now the shortest possible bug report.

### v4.2 — UX-4: telegraphed phases (2026-07-27)
A boss phase used to arrive with no warning at all — *The Waist* closed a hand, *The Lip* took your rests away, and the first you knew was the log line afterwards. That is a shock, not a decision.

Three changes. A **countdown** appears two holds out — *2 HOLDS TO THE WAIST*, with the phase's own description, so you can spend those two turns preparing rather than discovering. The **map** now names what a boss does before you commit to it: *it changes at 50% The Waist*, or for the finale, *35% The Traverse · 60% The Crux Sequence · 85% The Headwall*. And a phase landing finally has **its own cue** — a low sawtooth and a burst of grit, with a stutter of haptics — which is the "no sound or visual break when one triggers" flag from `BOSS-1` closed.

Verified by walking every boss hold by hold from zero to top-out: **all seven phases across the four bosses are telegraphed before they land**, and the countdown never points at the phase you are already inside.

### v4.1 — UX-3: what is left (2026-07-27)
The deck counter on the climb screen is now a button. **WHAT IS LEFT** slides up over the board with three lists: **still to come**, **discarded** (which comes back on a reshuffle), and **burnt out** (gone for the burn). Cards are grouped with counts and show their rules text, sharpened ones in green.

Contents but **not order** — the deckbuilder convention, and the right one: knowing what remains is planning, knowing the sequence is solving. The panel is local UI state rather than a phase, so opening it cannot touch the run or the save, and the counter itself now reads all three piles instead of two.

Verified the sheet cannot lie by accounting: across 1,866 mid-burn board states, hand + draw + discard + burnt + the three lanes always summed to exactly the fifteen cards the burn started with. **Zero cards unaccounted for** — which matters because exhaust-on-blow means cards genuinely leave play, and a pile view that quietly loses one would be worse than no view at all.

### v4.0 — UX-2: the resolve preview (2026-07-27)
You placed three cards and committed **blind**. Nothing showed which holds would clear, which of your cards would burn out, or how much pump you were about to take — and every one of those numbers already existed in the engine. A card game should not hide its own maths.

Each hold now reads **WORKS IT** or *n* **GRIP LEFT**; each of your cards reads **HOLDS · n** or **BURNS OUT**; an unanswered lane shows the pump it will cost. Beside COMMIT sits the number that actually decides the turn — **PUMP 6 → 9**, or **THIS TAKES YOU OFF** in red.

A preview that lies is worse than none, so it was verified against the engine over 7,000 lanes and 5,800 turns, and the first pass was **wrong 26% of the time on lanes and 62% on pump**. Four faults, each a rule the preview did not know:

- the harness itself was reading `boardH` *after* the route refills a cleared lane, so a clear looked like a miss — the test was wrong before the preview was;
- the clock is charged **after** resolution, so **a card that blows leaves its lane unanswered** and taxed;
- resolve clamps pump at zero on *each* shed then adds the clock, while the preview clamped only at the end;
- and working a **jug sheds a pump** — Rest fires on the clear, which nothing in the preview accounted for.

Also corrected: Sharp fires on a blow regardless of Anchor, and Peel takes precedence over it. Final accuracy: **100% on lane outcomes and 100% on predicted pump, exactly, across every sampled turn.**

### v3.9 — EVT-3: events that know where they are (2026-07-27)
Seventeen events were spread across forest, desert and alpine and none of them knew which range they were standing in. Events now carry an optional `act`, and the roll draws from the generic pool **plus** whatever belongs to where you are.

**Fifteen new ones, five per range.** The forest gets deer ticks, a line under moss worth brushing, a gated logging road, midges at dusk, poison ivy round the best boulder. The desert gets a robbed water cache, midday at a hundred and six, a thunderhead over a dry wash you are camped in, something moving in the shade under the pads, and forty miles of washboard road. The alpine gets a thirty-hour weather window, altitude headache, rockfall in the gully, verglas you cannot see until your hand is on it, and a bleached stuff sack in the moraine holding a rope, a rack and **journal page four**.

They also use the currencies the game has since grown: outcomes can now pay or cost **psyche** as well as cash, skin, cards and pages — so waiting out the heat restores your head, pushing through altitude costs it, and a long drive costs money rather than skin. Verified across 4,000 rolls per act: **32 events total, 22 reachable in any given act, zero wrong-range leaks**, and every outcome field used is one the engine actually applies.

### v3.8 — STAT-1: the numbers (2026-07-27)
**THE NUMBERS** collects everything the game has been recording and never showing. Career — level and XP, boulders sent, expeditions started, Lost Line sends, success rate, falls taken. The rock — lines ticked out of 30, flashes, one-burn sends, hardest grade, best ascent style, styles unlocked. The circuit best. The collection, broken down by rarity, and climbers unlocked. The story — journal pages and conversations, out of 6 and 15.

Almost all of it is **derived from the logbook rather than separately tracked**: hardest send, flashes, one-burn ascents and best style all fall out of records the game was already keeping. Only two counters had to be added, because nothing could infer them — **expeditions started** and **falls taken**. Verified the derivations against a fabricated six-send logbook: ticked, flashed, one-burn and hardest all resolve correctly.

Pool figures worth having in one place, since this is the first screen that states them: **227 cards** (90 common, 69 uncommon, 45 rare), **30 named routes** across three acts, 7 ascent styles, 4 climbers, 6 journal pages, 15 conversations.

### v3.7 — ENG-6: slots, export, and a loader that migrates (2026-07-27)
**Three save slots**, each with a summary on the picker — level, cards owned, circuit best, and whether a run is mid-flight — plus per-slot wipe. The active slot persists, so the game reopens where you left it.

**Export and import by code.** A slot serialises to a ~540-character base64 string, copied to the clipboard where the browser allows it, and imports into any slot. That means a run can be moved between devices, backed up before something risky, or handed over so a bug can be reproduced on the exact save that caused it.

The important fix is one that had not bitten yet and would have. The loader **discarded any save whose `v` did not match exactly**, so the first `SAVE_FILE_VERSION` bump would have silently wiped every player's collection, journal and logbook. It now **merges older saves** — every field has a default, so a version bump fills gaps rather than erasing progress — and refuses only saves from a *newer* build than the one running, which is the case where half-reading really would corrupt things.

Verified headlessly with a stubbed `localStorage`: round-trip preserves level, collection, circuit best and archetype; a **v0 save loads intact with its missing fields defaulted**; a future-version save is refused; export/import moves a save between slots; and a malformed code is rejected rather than throwing.

### v3.6 — MODE-1: The Circuit (2026-07-27)
The game asked for a 32-minute campaign and offered exactly one short alternative: a single skirmish climb. **The Circuit** is the loop in between — endless, one deck, no map, no nodes, just the next line at a harder grade until the skin or the head goes. Procedurally named and generated, escalating about 0.7 of a grade per send, with a card offered every third line so the deck grows with the difficulty. **Every fourth line is roped**, two pitches, which is the only place outside Act 3 the rope machinery is used at all — a partial answer to `ROPE-2`.

The important bit is that it is **bankable at any point**: *WALK AWAY WITH N* ends the run and keeps the score, so the session is exactly as long as you want it. Best score persists in the save alongside the journal and the logbook.

Measured across the four climbers on default loadouts: median **7–8 lines**, best runs 12–14, a full push running 10–14 minutes. That is longer than the five minutes the item was pitched at, and the honest reason is that a line takes about nine turns, so five minutes is roughly three lines. Escalation was steepened from half a grade per send to 0.7 to bring it down; the rest is answered by the walk-away button rather than by making the mode end sooner than the player wants.

### v3.5 — RUN-5: psyche (2026-07-27)
Named in the v0 design baseline, unbuilt until now, and worth having only because it does something skin does not. **Skin is attrition from falling; psyche is attrition from failing.** Skin caps how much you can climb; psyche caps how much you can *fail*. A boulder that beats you costs psyche whether or not you fell much; sending one gives it back; camps restore it; bailing costs more. At zero the run ends — you drive home. Waiting a day for conditions is now priced in psyche rather than skin, which is the right currency for frustration.

At or below 2 you are in **doubt**: the clock runs one pump faster every turn until you rest. That tax is the whole mechanic — measured with it removed, psyche stopped killing anyone (2–4% of runs) and became decoration; with it in, psyche ends 9–15% of runs.

Three measurements shaped it. First build had **zero effect at all** — identical completion to the decimal, because skin always ran out first: a failed climb costs up to 3 skin from falls but only 2 psyche, so skin bound every time. `RUN_SKIN` went 6 → 8 to give it headroom. Then psyche proved a coarse step function: at a cost of 2 per failure, `PSYCHE_MAX` 5 versus 4 flipped it from killing 2% of runs to killing 28%. Cost dropped to 1 per failure for finer granularity, landing at `PSYCHE_MAX` 3. Runs now end to skin ~15% of the time, psyche ~12%, and the finale for most of the rest — which is the shape a roguelike should have.

### v3.4 — RUN-2: cash and the trading post (2026-07-27)
Open since v0.9 and finally justified by a measurement: **skin is worth ~16 points of campaign completion**, which makes it hopeless as the price of a small decision. Cash is the currency you can spend a little of. Every send pays, projects pay double, act bosses pay a bonus, and events can now pay in cash instead of skin.

**Two trading posts per act.** They stock three cards priced by rarity (30 / 55 / 90), one piece of gear at 70, tape and salve for +2 skin at 45, and will cut a card from your deck for 40 — the first time deck-thinning has been available outside a camp.

Two corrections during balancing. The posts were first placed **in the slots events used to occupy**, so visiting one cost you an event and completion fell 6–8 points across every climber; they are now an extra option in those tiers rather than a replacement. Then roughly **$100 was going unspent every run**, which meant cash was arriving faster than it could matter — income trimmed about 35% (`cashForSend` 8+3·grade → 5+2·grade, boss bonus 50 → 35), leaving ~$70 spare, which is closer to a real decision.

The result reads like projects do: a post suits a fast deck and costs a slow one (Comp Kid gains, the Alpinist mostly does not). One cost is logged rather than solved — posts occupy map nodes, and **camps are where dialogue lives**, so the drafter's conversations per run dropped 1.9 → 0.9 once posts existed. Filed as `BAL-5`.

### v3.3 — SIM-2: a drafter that can read (2026-07-27)
Every balance number in this project came from a policy that valued a card at `power × 2 + contact` and had never heard of synergy tags, gear, archetype signatures or deck bloat. Replaced with a real valuation — and it lives in the **engine**, not the harness, per the `SIM-1` lesson, so it can later drive a hint in the reward screen.

It prices **Contact against how far the archetype can settle** (the Trad Dad caps at +1 and so wants Power; the Alpinist settles to +3 and so wants Contact), **synergy in both directions** (a specialist gains from the deck, and a tagged card feeds any specialist already in it), **gear** (fresh rubber makes foot moves worth more), keywords that measured well in the per-card probe, and **deck bloat** — every card is worth slightly less in a bigger deck. It can also **decline an offer** that would drag the deck down, which the old policy could not.

What moved, and what held:

- **Baselines rose 5–6 points.** Boulderer 14.8 → 20.3, Alpinist 17.0 → 22.0, Trad Dad 8.8 → 10.3, Comp Kid unchanged at 9.0. Drafting skill is worth real completion, which is what it should be worth.
- **The journal's effect became monotonic and much larger.** Campaign completion by pages read 39 / 50.5 / 46% under the old drafter — non-monotonic, which should have been a warning. It now reads **45.2 / 60.4 / 61.6%**. The journal was always worth ~16 points; drafting noise was hiding it.
- **The project conclusion held in shape.** Still positive for fast decks and negative for slow ones (Comp Kid +4.8, Trad Dad +3.7, Boulderer −6.5, Alpinist −19.5). Magnitudes shifted; the design statement did not.

### v3.2 — POLISH-3: the warm-up boulder (2026-07-27)
The spotter taught rules; nothing taught the game. The menu now leads with **FIRST TIME? START HERE** until you have done it once — a single boulder ten minutes from the car, with a deliberately plain thirteen-card deck and **eight hand-picked holds that introduce one idea each**: place and commit, read the diamond against the circle, fill the feet lane, a Greasy sloper, the hang-tax clock, Settle, a sharp hold burning a card out, and finally a crux that needs Power 2 or the move does nothing.

This needed the first authored content in the engine — a `holds` array on the route spec that bypasses the style-weight generator entirely and deals the sequence **in order, unshuffled**, verified: `jug → jug → sloper → crimp → pinch → sharp crimp → crux → jug`, exactly as written. The scripted line replaces the contextual spotter for the duration, so the lesson never gets interrupted by a general hint. It sends **96% of the time in 4.9 turns** — roughly a minute — which is what a first climb should be.

One genuinely dangerous bug caught on the way. The tutorial was first inserted **mid-table**, which shifted every route index after it — and the act maps address routes by index, so `B(29)` silently stopped pointing at The Lost Line and started pointing at the tutorial boulder. **The campaign finale was the warm-up.** Moved to the end of the table with a comment explaining why it must stay there. Also fixed the logbook, which was counting the tutorial and any non-act route in its denominator, making 100% unreachable.

### v3.1 — A11Y-1: shape before colour (2026-07-27)
Every stat in the game was a red circle beside a green circle, which put the entire combat readout on a single channel — and red-green colour vision deficiency affects roughly 8% of men. **Offence is now a diamond and defence a circle**, so shape carries the meaning and colour is a secondary cue. Crux holds gained a ★, the forecast gained ▲/▼ alongside *in nick* / *out of nick*, and an optional **colour-safe palette** swaps green for a deep blue across all nineteen screens. Both settings persist.

The measurement corrected the premise, which is worth recording. Simulating deuteranopia, protanopia and tritanopia with the Viénot matrices and measuring CIE76 separation, the existing red/green pair holds up at **ΔE 37–49** — comfortably above the ~11 confusion threshold. The claim that it was unreadable was wrong. What was true is that it relied *entirely* on colour, which is fragile at 20px on textured paper in poor light. Shape coding makes the readout unconditional, and the optional palette raises separation to **ΔE 72–91**. A greyscale render of the board confirms the stats are still legible with colour removed altogether.

### v3.0 — ROPE-1: pitches, runout and the sharp end (2026-07-27)
The cut that defined the MVP, finally in. The three-lane fight does not change at all — what changes is **what a fall means**. On a boulder you come off and the burn ends. On a rope you fall **onto your last piece**, lose the ground above it, and carry on. That single reframe makes **runout** the tension.

Three roped lines in the alpine act — **The Nose Direct** and **Coffin Crack** (three pitches each) and **The Cornice** as a two-pitch project — plus **eight pieces of protection**: quickdraws, wired nuts, hexes, a number two cam, a bomber cam, a screamer. Clearing a hold without clipping raises the runout; clipping resets it and marks where the rope will catch you. Crossing a pitch boundary is a **belay**: pump to zero, runout to zero, a safe point you cannot fall below. Falling with nothing in is a **ground fall** and ends the route outright.

Four design failures on the way, each teaching something. Protection first occupied a lane like a move — **clipping cost a limb**, which is wrong: on real rock it is a quick action. Made bonus cards instead, and ground falls went from 67% to zero. Then roped routes would not *end*: caught, climb back, fall again, until skin ran out sixty turns later. On real rock you lower off and come back, so **a caught fall now costs a burn** — three and you are done for the day. Routes were also too long at clear 12 and dropped to 9.

Final measurement on an Act 3 deck: **without gear, 65% send but 16% ground falls; with gear, 51% send and 2% ground falls.** Carrying protection costs deck slots and speed and insures against catastrophe — which is the decision running it out has always been. Belays fire 1.7 a burn. Campaign completion 39–50% with deaths at 3 / 10 / 49, the alpine act now clearly the crux of the game.

### v2.9 — LOG-1: the logbook (2026-07-27)
A guidebook you fill in. Every send is recorded against its route: how many **burns** it took, the **ascent style** it went in, the **conditions on the day**, whether you **flashed** it, and how many times you have done it. Repeat sends keep the best of each field rather than overwriting — a one-burn repeat improves the record without erasing that you first did it at *Redpoint*. **28 lines to tick**, grouped by act, and a route you have already sent shows as ticked on the map with your best burn count.

It costs almost nothing because the game already produced every one of these numbers and threw them away at the end of each session. Act membership is **derived from the maps** rather than hardcoded, so a route can never quietly go missing from the book — verified: all 28 assigned, 11 / 8 / 9 across the acts. `logSend` verified on a repeat too: burns improved 3 → 1, style 2 correctly retained rather than overwritten by a later style-0 ascent, flash flag set, conditions updated to the better send.

The logbook persists in the save alongside the journal and the collection, which makes it the third thing that outlives a dead run — and the only one that is about the rock rather than about you. One naming collision on the way: `log` was already the in-climb combat log, so the record is `book`.

### v2.8 — SIM-1: one phase graph (2026-07-27)
No visible change, and the most important turn in a while. `ENG-2` guaranteed the sim and the game share a resolution function, but **screen transitions lived only in the UI**, so the harness reimplemented them — and in one session that produced two false readings: a policy that took projects before checking camps, and a gear→reward chain that returned to the map without advancing the tier, silently re-running the same node and reporting 8.5 projects a run when only six exist. Every balance decision in this project rests on the harness, so a harness that can lie is the most expensive bug available.

Extracted into the engine as **`takeOfferStep` · `pickGearStep` · `campStep` · `leaveEventStep`**, with a shared `toMapNext` that handles the pack interstitial. `pickGearStep` carries the comment explaining why it does *not* advance the tier — whoever set phase `gear` has already done so — which is precisely the invariant the harness got wrong. The UI and the sim now both call them; neither owns a copy.

Verified behaviour-preserving by regression: identical completion on both project policies, to the decimal (14.8 / 9.0 / 8.8 / 17.0 skipping, 11.8 / 13.0 / 14.5 / 2.3 taking). All six harness modes still run. Campaign with a built loadout reads 48–59% with deaths at 3 / 10 / 40 across the acts — Act 3 is the wall now, which is what a finale should be.

### v2.7 — ROUTE-3: projects (2026-07-27)
Every climb on the map was worth about the same, so there was no risk appetite — you took the easier line and lost nothing. Six tiers now offer an optional **project**: a long line at the act's own grade rather than a harder one, with **no consolation card if it does not go**, and **gear plus a card** if it does. Three of them: **The Sandbag** (given V2 by a man who never did it), **Furnace Arete**, and **The Cornice**.

Three failed designs before it worked, each teaching a number. Making projects *harder* (two grades up, one burn) was a pure trap — taking them dropped the Alpinist from 17.4% to 2.0%, because failing cost the entry skin plus three falls. Making them one burn did not help: a one-burn attempt at a route two grades up essentially never sends. The real constraint surfaced on the third attempt — **skin is worth roughly 16 points of campaign completion, which is more than any reward in the game is worth.** Nothing priced in skin can be a fair gamble. So a project now costs no skin at all; its price is the node itself and the guaranteed consolation card you forfeit.

Two harness bugs on the way, both of the same kind. The policy took projects *before* checking camps, starving itself of rest and sharpening. Worse, the sim's gear handler returned to the map without advancing the tier, so after a project send it re-ran the same node — 8.5 projects a run when only six exist. **The screen transitions live in the UI, not the shared engine, which is the one place the harness can still drift from the game** — logged as `SIM-1`.

Final measurement is the one worth having: taking projects is **+5.7 for the Trad Dad, +4.0 for the Comp Kid, −3.0 for the Boulderer and −14.7 for the Alpinist**. Mixed, not solved — projects suit a fast deck and punish a slow one.

### v2.6 — BOSS-1: phases (2026-07-27)
Bosses were previously just longer boulders with more crux. All four now run **scripted phases** at fractions of the top-out, announced in the log and banner-lined on the climb screen. **The Priest** steepens at 65% — *The Headwall*, the clock runs faster. **The Hourglass** pinches shut at 50% — *The Waist* closes the right hand entirely. **Summit Block** roofs at 45% then denies rests at 80% — *The Roof*, then *The Lip*. **The Lost Line** runs three: *The Traverse* speeds the clock at 35%, *The Crux Sequence* makes every hold committing at 60%, *The Headwall* removes rests at 85%. All declarative rows on the route spec.

The first version dropped completion to **0.6%**, and softening every number changed nothing — which was the clue that it was a bug, not a magnitude problem. `lockLane` blocked you from *playing* into a lane but the route kept filling it, so The Hourglass created a permanently unanswerable hold that bit you and taxed you every turn forever. **Act 2 became 68% of all deaths.** The fix is also the thematically correct one: if the tower pinches in there is nothing out right, so a locked lane now holds nothing at all. `allCrux` was likewise rewritten from "replace every hold with a wall" (+3 Grip, +1 Bite atop crux stats) to "harden it" — +2 Grip and the Committing ability, keeping its own stat line.

After the fix, a built loadout completes **54–66%** with deaths spread 9/12/25 across the acts. Archetype default loadouts sit at **7.5–16.8%**, roughly half what they were, which is bosses doing their job — logged as `BAL-4` since the gap between a default and a built loadout is now wide.

### v2.5 — CARD-3: synergy tags (2026-07-27)
With 209 cards, drafting was still "take the better stat line" — nothing rewarded building a *theme*. Every card now carries a tag — **crimp · sloper · compression · pocket · dynamic · crack · footwork · rest · headgame** — and ten **specialists** scale off them: `+1 Power for every 3 cards of their tag in your deck`. Crimp Specialist, Friction Master, Compression Beast, Pocket Poacher, Air Time, Crack Rat, Footwork, Old Hands, The Specialist, Silent Approach. Pool now **219 cards**.

Tags are **derived from a card's identity, not authored 219 times** — a word list per tag, with rests and foot moves and bonuses resolving first. The bonus is baked once at deck build rather than counted every resolution, so it costs nothing at runtime. Verified by construction: a Crimp Specialist in a generic deck (1 crimp card) sits at Power 1; in a crimp deck (10 crimp cards) it reads Power 4.

The loadout editor now shows the theme you are actually building — a live tag breakdown with a ★ once a tag crosses the threshold — plus each card's tag on its row. That is the part that fixes drafting: you can see what a card is *for*, not just what it costs. Tag distribution across the pool runs headgame 39 · rest 37 · footwork 30 · crimp 15 · crack 13 · dynamic 13 · compression 9 · sloper 8 · pocket 6, with 22% deliberately untagged as neutral cards. Pocket and sloper are thin to build around and that is a known limit, logged rather than padded. Campaign completion sits at 22.7–32.3% across the four climbers.

### v2.4 — ROUTE-2: the forecast (2026-07-27)
Conditions swing a battle **57–96%** and were rolled the instant you committed, which made the single biggest variable in the game invisible. Every climb node on the map now shows its weather and rock before you choose, colour-coded **in nick** or **out of nick** — and **WAIT A DAY** spends a skin to roll a whole new forecast, which is the other half of the decision every climber actually makes.

The forecast is derived as a **pure function of run seed, act, tier, node and a reroll counter** rather than rolled and stored. That means it can be displayed without consuming the run RNG, stays stable across re-renders, and needed no new state beyond a single integer — waiting a day just increments it.

Measured across 33,000 tiers that offer two or more climbs: **72% present genuinely different conditions**, median forecast spread 2 and p90 of 4, with all six weathers appearing evenly at ~17%. Notably, teaching the sim to pick the best-conditioned line did **not** raise completion (27.7 → 26.0 for the Boulderer, 19.4 → 22.3 for the Trad Dad — mixed and roughly flat), because route difficulty outweighs weather in its policy. That is the honest result and the right one: the forecast is one input to a judgement call, not a free win, and its value is a decision a player gets to make rather than a number an AI can farm.

### v2.3 — RUN-4: sharpen (2026-07-27)
Camps now offer three things instead of one: **rest**, **cut**, or **sharpen**. Decks previously only grew, with thinning as the sole lever; sharpening is the other half of deckbuilding.

Upgrades are **derived from a card's shape rather than authored 209 times** — moves gain +1 Power and +1 Contact (about +3 budget by the costing law, a full tier), rests shed more instead, and bonus cards either lose a pump of cost or gain a point of effect. One function covers the entire pool, and it survives future cards for free. Upgrades persist through the save by suffixing the name.

The first version was **worth nothing**: about 1.5 sharpens a run, each improving a single card in a twenty-card deck, against a rest worth ~16 points of completion. Nobody would ever choose it. Fixed by making a sharpen drill **every copy of that move** — which is both stronger and better design, since sharpening a 3-of now clearly beats a 1-of and the camp list shows the copy count. Completion moved from **22.0 / 16.3 / 14.7 / 24.3** on a rest-always line to **24.0 / 19.5 / 23.5 / 26.5**, with the Trad Dad gaining most because his loadout runs 3-ofs. Rest is still worth more raw points than a sharpen — which is correct: it should be a close call, not a solved one.

### v2.2 — META-4: four climbers (2026-07-27)
**The Boulderer** (*Projecting* — beta is worth double, worked holds return at −2 Grip), **The Comp Kid** (*Flash* — +4 Power on the first turn of every burn), **The Trad Dad** (*Bomber* — +2 Contact on everything, but nothing settles past +1), and **The Alpinist** (*Endurance* — moves settle all the way to +3, but everything has 2 less Contact). Each carries its own 15-card loadout, its own starting gear, and its own editable deck — loadouts are now stored per climber rather than globally. Unlocks at levels 1 / 4 / 8 / 12.

Four rounds of balancing, and each one measured a dial the game had never had a number for. The first pass ran **Comp Kid 2.7% against Alpinist 78.3%**, with Alpinist runs at 236 turns — 47 minutes. Losing beta entirely, it turns out, is crippling across three acts, while ignoring weather is enormous. The second pass measured the two blunt instruments: **1 skin is worth ~16 points of campaign completion and one burn ~15**, so `dSkin` and `dAttempts` are far too coarse to differentiate characters and were dropped from every archetype. The third found the real surprise — **`settleMax` 2 → 3 alone is worth 55 points**, because Settle is as explosive as Power and a high-Contact deck compounds it; it now costs the Alpinist 2 Contact to have it.

Final spread: **Boulderer 22.8% · Comp Kid 16.3% · Trad Dad 16.3% · Alpinist 24.0%** on default loadouts with a full journal. Run length varies 95–196 turns, which is the characters reading correctly — the Comp Kid races, the Alpinist grinds.

### v2.1 — GEAR-1: what is in the van (2026-07-27)
Twelve run-scoped passives across three slots — **shoes, chalk, kit**. You start every run with a random pair of shoes and pick one of three at each act boss, one per slot, so a run has an identity before you have drawn a card. Downturned shoes trade Contact for Power; fresh rubber sharpens the feet lane; liquid chalk sheds a pump every turn; a wire brush cleans the first hold of every burn; crash pads eat the first fall on each boulder. Gear is loot, not collection — it goes with the run.

The balance work was the story. First pass took campaign completion from 19% to **60%**, and the cause was the same lesson for the third time: **deck-wide flat modifiers are enormous.** A `+1 Power to every hand move` is precisely the weather `dPower` the v0.7 sweep showed swinging a battle ~40 points, and three pieces stacked it. Rewritten so that gear touching Power either pays for it in Contact or only touches the feet lane (4–5 cards, not 15), and nothing grants an extra burn.

That landed at **~48% at Working It**, and rather than nerf further this is taken as a deliberate rebase. The old 19% was punishing for a base difficulty — `BAL-1` had already flagged that a starter loadout finished 0.5% of the time. Verified the ascent ladder still carries the challenge: **48.3% → 10.7%** from *Working It* to *Ground Up*, a 4.5× range, with deaths spread 14/14/27 across the three acts. Base difficulty is now approachable and the ladder is where difficulty lives, which is what the ladder was built for.

### v2.0 — ART-2: glyphs, a wordmark, and deckled edges (2026-07-27)
The fork resolved by noticing the hero art was never for players — it is for the store listing, and none of it needs to live in a bundle shipped to a phone. So the game stays **100% procedural** and the illustration budget moves to `ART-3`.

**Eleven hold glyphs**, hand-authored SVG paths: a jug as an incut hook, a crimp as a thin edge with a curl under it, a sharp crimp serrated, a sloper as a dome, a pinch as two sides squeezing, a pocket as a circle with two dots, a crux as an inked star. They sit in the one corner of the hold card that was empty. This is the only layer that makes the board *easier* to read rather than merely better-looking — you can now identify a hold without reading it.

**The wordmark is jittered type, not a webfont** — each letter rotated and nudged from the same seeded hash, plus an inked underline. That is most of the hand-lettered feel for ~0 bytes, and it sidesteps the real risk: a display face at 11px on textured paper is precisely how the contrast won back in v1.8 would have been lost. Headings across the game use it. **Deckled card edges** came free — `roughPath` gained a segment count, and a torn edge is just an edge subdivided.

Verified by extending the mock with a minimal SVG path renderer, so it draws the **actual glyph paths and real deckled outlines** rather than stand-ins. Contrast re-measured as promised and it held: **ink 7.7–9.7:1, fade 3.4–4.3:1, red 4.0–5.0:1** — every region still above the 3:1 floor. **+2KB.**

### v1.9 — POLISH-2: the trade lands (2026-07-27)
A card battler lives on the moment a trade resolves, and until now the board silently redrew. **+4KB, still no asset files.**

**Sound is synthesised**, not sampled — Web Audio oscillators plus a seeded noise buffer, so the whole audio bed costs bytes rather than kilobytes and matches the reasoning behind the procedural art. Nine cues, tuned dry and tactile rather than musical: a chalk-scrape on **commit**, a bright two-note lift when a hold is **worked**, a low tearing thunk when a card **blows**, a long descending saw when you come **off**, a three-note rise on a **send**. The context is created lazily on the first tap because browsers demand a gesture, and every path is wrapped — a silent game still plays perfectly.

**Animation is per-lane.** `resolve` now records what happened in each of the three lanes, and the UI keys each slot on an `fxTick` so the CSS animation replays on every commit rather than firing once and going stale: an unanswered lane **shakes**, a worked hold **pops**, a blown card **tears** downward and fades. The pump bar pulses when anything landed. Outcome sounds are staggered 150–300ms behind the commit so a turn reads as a sequence rather than a jump cut. **Haptics** on commit, a stutter on a fall, a longer pattern on a send.

Both sound and the spotter toggle from the menu and persist in the save. Campaign balance unmoved at 12.4 / 18.0 / 18.8%.

### v1.8 — ART-1: paper, ink, and the topo (2026-07-27)
The look the game was pitched on, drawn entirely in code — no image assets, **+6KB total**.

**Paper.** Real fibre from an inline `feTurbulence` fractal-noise SVG baked into a data-URI background and multiplied against the page, plus a vignette and a faint field-notebook rule every 28px. **Ink.** Card and slot borders are no longer CSS rectangles but hand-drawn SVG paths — each edge a wobbled quadratic, inked twice at slightly different offsets so it reads as a pen gone round twice. Jitter is derived from a seeded hash of each card's `uid`, never `Math.random`, so a given card's border is *the same stroke every render* rather than crawling under StrictMode. Cruxes ink in red at heavier weight; empty lanes ink faint. **The topo.** The progress ticks are gone, replaced by a hand-drawn topo line wandering across the page — circled holds filled as you work them, dashed line ahead into unknown ground, a red × marking the top.

Verified by reimplementing the identical jitter maths in Python so the 390px mock draws the *real* borders rather than an impression of them. That mock immediately caught the cost: texture ate **four points of contrast** on small type, dropping `--fade` to **1.9–2.8:1**, well under the 3:1 floor for 8px text, with red borderline at 2.7:1 in the header. Paid back by softening the vignette and noise and darkening the two secondary colours — `--fade` to `#5f584a`, `--red` to `#8c3124`. Now **ink 6.7–9.9:1, fade 3.0–4.4:1, red 3.4–5.1:1** across every region that carries text.

### v1.7 — POLISH-1: the spotter (2026-07-27)
Every rule in this game was previously learned by losing — seven hold abilities, Settle, Support, campusing and seventeen card keywords, none of them explained anywhere. The fix is the thing climbing actually has: **someone on the ground shouting the beta up at you.** A single contextual line, priority-ordered so it always surfaces whatever is about to cost the most — you are one turn from coming off; your feet lane is empty and that is campusing; a crux needs Power 2 or the move does literally nothing; slopers are Greasy unless your feet are on; pockets ignore Support entirely; that card will not survive the Bite and will burn out for the rest of the burn. It teaches on every climb rather than only a scripted first one, and it can be switched off from the menu for players who no longer want the noise.

Alongside it, **HOW IT WORKS** — a full reference for every hold ability with its live Bite/Grip, and all 25 keywords in plain language. Both persist their state in the save. Longest spotter line measured at 88 characters, two lines at 390px, ~60px of vertical budget; campaign balance untouched at 13% / 18%.

### v1.6 — 209 cards, seven new mechanics, and a way out of the run (2026-07-27)
**Navigation first:** entering The Lost Line trapped you — the map had no exit and the only escape was closing the tab. It now carries a `◂ MENU` button, and since the run is already saved at every safe point, leaving and hitting CONTINUE resumes exactly where you were.

**The pool roughly doubled, to 209** — 86 common, 60 uncommon, 40 rare, 10 curse, 6 beta, 7 starter. Doubling a pool is only worth doing if the new cards are decisions rather than stat blocks, so it is carried by **seven new mechanics**: **Chip** (damage every lane at once — compression finally has a card shape), **Guard** (covers the *other* hand lane), **Momentum** (scales with flow), **Weight** (scales with your own board), **Echo** (returns to hand when it clears), **Peel** (draws when it blows), **Cycle** (draws when you place it) — plus **skin cost**, the first cards that spend run health instead of pump. The vocabulary went where climbing goes: closed crimps, ring locks, chicken wings, off-width grovels, thumb sprags, smedges, heel cams, bat hangs, antihydral, crash pads.

Verified with the per-card probe. Rarity ordering holds across the bigger pool — **rare +17.6 > uncommon +9.5 > common +3.2 > curse −11.0**, with **beta highest at +20.3**, which is correct since those are the finale payoff. One genuine outlier: **Psyche** at −25pt, a cost-1 utility card, fixed to cost 0 — the same lesson as Beta Spray in v1.0, that charging pump for utility is punishing when pump is your health bar. Campaign balance is unmoved at 13.0 / 18.3 / 18.7% by journal pages.

Doubling the pool also broke progression pacing — at 3 cards a level it would have taken ~67 levels to see it all. `PACK_SIZE` 3 → 4, `xpToNext` flattened to `25 + 10·level`, `xpForSend` raised to `8 + 4·grade`, bringing it to ~51 levels. Logged as `BAL-2`, since a long tail is intended but the grind is unverified.

### v1.5 — NARR-3: people at the fire (2026-07-27)
Camps were a rest button; now someone is sometimes sitting there. **Fifteen conversations across five characters** — **Marge**, who climbed with him for twenty-two years and was never told where he was going; **Dale**, the local who resents the ghost story and misses the man; **Nita**, a comp kid who climbs harder than he did on plastic and knows exactly what that sentence is worth; **Ranger Ellis**; and **Pim**, who wants a shower. Talking is free and does not consume the camp's rest-or-cut choice, so dialogue never competes with the mechanical decision.

Conversations chain: replies gate later ones through an `after` field, and **Marge's five-part thread** is gated partly on journal pages, so the pages you find change what she will tell you — and she is the one who eventually says which drainage. Two conversations hand *back* journal pages, closing the loop the other way. Crucially, `seen` persists in the save alongside the journal, so the story advances across runs even when the run ends badly. Measured at ~1.8 conversations per run, which puts the full cast at roughly eight runs — meta-narrative pacing rather than something you exhaust in one sitting. Campaign balance is unmoved (12.8 / 17.8 / 19.0% by journal pages), confirming dialogue pays in story rather than power. `EventOutcome.text` became optional along the way, since a dialogue reply already carries its own line.

### v1.4 — RUN-3: three acts, and The Lost Line (2026-07-27)
The campaign from the founding brainstorm now exists end to end. **Act 2 — desert towers** (The Gooseneck, Varnish, Sun Dagger, The Chimney, Rattlesnake Arete, Kiln, and The Hourglass as its boss) and **Act 3 — the alpine wall** (The Notch, Cold Shoulder, Icebox Corner, Whiteout Slab, The Diving Board, Bergschrund, Summit Block), finishing on **The Lost Line**, graded `?`. Sixteen new boulders, act-specific maps, skin topped up between ranges, and a run that carries one deck across all three.

**The journal pays off.** Every page you carry becomes a **beta card** in your deck for the finale only — six of them, each drawn from the page it belongs to. Measured: campaign completion **12.5% with no pages, 19.0% with all six**. Exploration literally makes the finale sendable, which is the claim the design baseline made on day one and the first time it has been true.

Two scaling faults came out of simming rather than guessing. The first pass finished at **1.0%**: the grade ramp (uncapped `bumpFor`, V9 → +4) outran deck growth, because rewards were one card a stage regardless of act. Fixed by capping the bump at 3 and **scaling reward rarity with the act** (70/25/5 → 50/38/12 → 35/45/20), which is what lets a deck keep pace with the grades. The second was an Act 2 hump — 35% of all deaths landed there — shaved by a crux off each of its boulders. Deaths now spread 23/29/29 across the three acts.
